abstract_pt: 'As redes de operadores de telecomunicações têm estado sob considerável
  pressão, resultado da escalada do número de subscritores bem como do aumento da
  oferta de serviços. A proliferação de tecnologias de entrega de serviços sobre fibra
  (FTTx), bem como as redes móveis 5G, levaram à rápida escalada de requisitos de
  largura de banda. Esta é consumida por diversos serviços, tais como transmissão
  de vídeo e online gaming. Além disso, os padrões de utilização de Internet, que
  foram tradicionalmente assimétricos e maioritariamente de tráfego downstream, têm
  visto um aumento significativo de tráfego upstream, em parte devido à popularidade
  de serviços de criação e partilha de conteúdo de redes sociais. A soma de todos
  estes fatores exige ligações de Internet com maior velocidade e menor latência.
  Como resultado, existe uma necessidade de melhorar as capacidades das redes de operador,
  bem como de flexibilizar a sua gestão. As redes de telecomunicações são sistemas
  complexos, tendo evoluído ao longo de várias décadas e sido cuidadosamente otimizadas
  e afinadas para extrair o máximo de potencial dos recursos existentes. No entanto,
  essa evolução gradual tem gerado redes com uma estrutura rígida, não sendo surpresa
  encontrar ser viços legados em utilização. Desta forma, é comum a infraestrutura
  ter considerável inércia à mudança, dificultando a adaptação às rápidas mudanças
  do mercado. Outro desafio que os operadores de telecomunicações enfrentam é a demora
  na introdução de novos serviços. Por exemplo, na criação de um serviço para subscritores
  de Internet é necessário a compatibilidade com um grande número de gateways. Em
  consequência disso, o operador necessita de preparar o novo ser viço para correr
  num diverso leque de dispositivos, de diferentes fabricantes ou modelos variados.
  Isto atrasa a introdução de novos serviços, devido à necessidade de serem testados
  para correr em diferentes equipamentos. Por outro lado, trocar todos os dispositivos
  já instalados nos subscritores normalmente não é economicamente viável. O surgimento
  de conceitos de virtualização de rede tais como SDN e NFV introduziram um novo paradigma
  de rede, com potenciais benefícios para operadores de telecomunicações – facilitação
  dos procedimentos de configuração da infraestrutura, consolidação de hardware, e
  simplificação do processo de desenho de serviço são alguns aspetos que podem ser
  alavancados com virtualização. Um exemplo disso é a virtualização de componentes
  da infraestrutura do operador, onde funções são movidas de hardware especializado
  e monolítico para instâncias baseadas em software, utilizando equipamento comercial
  (COTS). O trabalho apresentado nesta tese estuda a utilização de técnicas de virtualização
  de rede em múltiplos domínios de redes de telecomunicação, propondo várias contribuições:  -
  No domínio das redes de acesso, explora como equipamento da infraestrutura pode
  passar para um paradigma de virtualização, afastando-se das conhecidas desvantagens
  da utilização de equipamento legado. Mais concretamente, a investigação focou-se
  na virtualização do gateway residencial (RGW) e do BNG. O primeiro é um dispositivo
  presente nas instalações do subscritor, que o liga à infraestrutura do operador.
  O segundo é um equipamento presente na infraestrutura do operador, ligando as gateways
  dos subscritores à rede core.  - No domínio das redes móveis, esta tese explora
  a arquitetura modular das redes 5G baseadas em serviços (SBA) para propor uma framework
  de segurança para a monitorização de componentes do core 5G, utilizando sondas de
  segurança para capturar e processar eventos, para detetar comportamentos suspeitos
  ou não autorizados.  - Por último, esta tese também explora de que modo a virtualização
  de redes pode melhorar a ciber segurança de redes de IACS – de considerável importância
  em sistemas de controlo industrial tais como distribuição de energia e automação
  industrial – alavancando a virtualização para permitir monitorização flexível e
  mitigação ativa. O trabalho de investigação aqui apresentado foi desenvolvido no
  âmbito de vários projetos de investigação (Hollistic SDN, ATENA, Mobilizador 5G
  e POWER), cujos parceiros forneceram importantes requisitos, casos de uso e frameworks
  de validação.'

  with a growing number of connected subscribers and escalating service demands. The
  proliferation of Fiber-To-The-Premises (FTTx) connections and the advent of fifth
  generation mobile networks (5G) have led to an explosion of bandwidth requirements,
  consumed by multiple ser vices – such as video streaming and online video games.
  Furthermore, Internet usage patterns. which were traditionally asymmetric and mostly
  based on downstream traffic, have witnessed a significant increase in upstream use
  due to the popularity of content creation and social media sharing. When combined,
  these services require larger throughputs and lower latency from the access networks.
  Consequently, there is a pressing need to improve their capacity and manageability.
  Operator networks are often complex infrastructures evolved over several decades,
  carefully optimized and fine-tuned to make the most of available resources. Given
  their continuous evolution, it is not surprising to find legacy services still being
  used and maintained. This results in infrastructures with considerable inertia to
  quick changes, that struggle to cope with the fast-changing service offers and consumption
  habits. Another challenge faced by telecommunications operators is the long time
  it takes to introduce new services. For instance, when creating a service for sub
  scriber broadband gateways, compatibility with a diverse array of devices is necessary
  (due to legacy deployments). As a result, the operator must prepare the new service
  to run on a pool of different devices, encompassing multiple vendors or heterogeneous
  models. This delays the introduction of new services, as they must be developed
  and tested for all these different equipment. On the other hand, replacing all customer
  devices when a new model is often not be financially feasible. The emergence of
  software-defined concepts such as Software-Defined Networking (SDN) and Network
  Function Virtualization (NFV) introduced a new networking paradigm, with potential
  benefits for network operators – easing infrastructure configuration procedures,
  consolidating hardware, and simplifying service design are some aspects that can
  be leveraged with virtualization. An example is the virtualization of infrastructure
  components, where functions are moved from specialized and monolithic equipment
  to software-based instances capable of running in Commercial Off-The-Shelf (COTS)
  hardware. The work presented in this thesis studies the use of network virtualization
  techniques in multiple domains of telecommunication networks, proposing several
  contributions:    -  In the domain of broadband access networks, it explores how
  operator infrastructure equipment can be moved to software-based virtualized in
  stances, stepping away from the well-known disadvantages of the specialized equipment
  used in the traditional model. More specifically, the research focused on the virtualization
  of the Residential Gateway (RGW) and the Border Network Gateway (BNG). The first
  is a device deployed within the customer premises, acting as a gateway of the subscriber
  local network, and the latter is located at the edge of the operator’s infrastructure,
  connecting the RGW devices to the core network.   - In the domain of mobile networks,
  this thesis explores the modular Service Based Architecture (SBA) of 5G to propose
  a security framework to monitor components of the 5G core, using security probes
  to collect events and processing them to detect suspicious or unwanted behaviour.   -
  Finally, this thesis also explores how the flexible characteristics of network virtualization
  can be used to improve the cybersecurity of Industrial Automation and Control Systems
  (IACS) networks – relevant in industrial control systems such as energy distribution
  systems and factory automation – by leveraging virtualization for flexible monitoring
  and active mitigation. The research work hereby presented was developed in the scope
  of several research projects (Holistic SDN, ATENA, Mobilizador 5G, and POWER), whose
  partners provided valuable requirements, use cases and validation frameworks.'

# Applications of Network and System Virtualization in Multiservice Context

Jorge Diogo Gomes Proença 
 
 
 
APPLICATIONS OF  NETWORK AND SYSTEM 
VIRTUALIZATION 
IN MULTISERVICE CONTEXT 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Tese no âmbito do Doutoramento em Engenharia Informática, Arquiteturas, Redes e 
Cibersegurança , orientada pelo Professor Doutor Paulo Alexandre Ferreira Simões e 
pelo Professor Doutor Tiago José dos Santos Martins da Cruz, e apresentada ao 
Departamento de Engenharia Informática da Faculdade de Ciências e Tecnologia da 
Universidade de Coimbra .

ix

Abstract
T
elecommunications operators’ networks have been under increasing
pressure, with a growing number of connected subscribers and escal-
ating service demands. The proliferation of Fiber-To-The-Premises
(FTTx) connections and the advent of fifth generation mobile networks (5G)
have led to an explosion of bandwidth requirements, consumed by multiple ser-
vices – such as video streaming and online video games. Furthermore, Internet
usage patterns. which were traditionally asymmetric and mostly based on down-
stream traffic, have witnessed a significant increase in upstream use due to the
popularity of content creation and social media sharing. When combined, these
services require larger throughputs and lower latency from the access networks.
Consequently, there is a pressing need to improve their capacity and manageab-
ility.
Operator networks are often complex infrastructures evolved over several dec-
ades, carefully optimized and fine-tuned to make the most of available resources.
Given their continuous evolution, it is not surprising to find legacy services still
being used and maintained. This results in infrastructures with considerable
inertia to quick changes, that struggle to cope with the fast-changing service
offers and consumption habits.
Another challenge faced by telecommunications operators is the long time it
takes to introduce new services. For instance, when creating a service for sub-
scriber broadband gateways, compatibility with a diverse array of devices is
necessary (due to legacy deployments). As a result, the operator must prepare
the new service to run on a pool of different devices, encompassing multiple
vendors or heterogeneous models. This delays the introduction of new services,
as they must be developed and tested for all these different equipment. On the
other hand, replacing all customer devices when a new model is often not be
financially feasible.
The emergence of software-defined concepts such as Software-Defined Network-
ing ( SDN) and Network Function Virtualization ( NFV) introduced a new net-
working paradigm, with potential benefits for network operators – easing in-
frastructure configuration procedures, consolidating hardware, and simplifying
service design are some aspects that can be leveraged with virtualization. An
example is the virtualization of infrastructure components, where functions are
moved from specialized and monolithic equipment to software-based instances
capable of running in Commercial Off-The-Shelf ( COTS) hardware.
The work presented in this thesis studies the use of network virtualization tech-
niques in multiple domains of telecommunication networks, proposing several
contributions:
• In the domain of broadband access networks, it explores how operator
xi

infrastructure equipment can be moved to software-based virtualized in-
stances, stepping away from the well-known disadvantages of the special-
ized equipment used in the traditional model. More specifically, the re-
search focused on the virtualization of the Residential Gateway ( RGW)
and the Border Network Gateway ( BNG). The first is a device deployed
within the customer premises, acting as a gateway of the subscriber local
network, and the latter is located at the edge of the operator’s infrastruc-
ture, connecting the RGW devices to the core network.
• In the domain of mobile networks, this thesis explores the modular Service-
Based Architecture ( SBA) of 5G to propose a security framework to mon-
itor components of the 5G core, using security probes to collect events and
processing them to detect suspicious or unwanted behaviour.
• Finally, this thesis also explores how the flexible characteristics of network
virtualization can be used to improve the cybersecurity of Industrial Auto-
mation and Control Systems ( IACS) networks – relevant in industrial con-
trol systems such as energy distribution systems and factory automation –
by leveraging virtualization for flexible monitoring and active mitigation.
The research work hereby presented was developed in the scope of several
research projects (Holistic SDN, ATENA, Mobilizador 5G, and POWER),
whose partners provided valuable requirements, use cases and validation frame-
works.
Keywords: Broadband Networks, Virtualization, Residential
Gateways, Border Network Gateway, Cybersecurity
xii

Resumo
A
s redes de operadores de telecomunicações têm estado sob considerável
pressão, resultado da escalada do número de subscritores bem como
do aumento da oferta de serviços. A proliferação de tecnologias de
entrega de serviços sobre fibra ( FTTx), bem como as redes móveis 5G, levaram
à rápida escalada de requisitos de largura de banda. Esta é consumida por
diversos serviços, tais como transmissão de vídeo e online gaming . Além disso,
os padrões de utilização de Internet, que foram tradicionalmente assimétricos
e maioritariamente de tráfego downstream, têm visto um aumento significativo
de tráfego upstream, em parte devido à popularidade de serviços de criação
e partilha de conteúdo de redes sociais. A soma de todos estes fatores exige
ligações de Internet com maior velocidade e menor latência. Como resultado,
existe uma necessidade de melhorar as capacidades das redes de operador, bem
como de flexibilizar a sua gestão.
As redes de telecomunicações são sistemas complexos, tendo evoluído ao longo
de várias décadas e sido cuidadosamente otimizadas e afinadas para extrair o
máximo de potencial dos recursos existentes. No entanto, essa evolução gradual
tem gerado redes com uma estrutura rígida, não sendo surpresa encontrar ser-
viços legados em utilização. Desta forma, é comum a infraestrutura ter con-
siderável inércia à mudança, dificultando a adaptação às rápidas mudanças do
mercado.
Outro desafio que os operadores de telecomunicações enfrentam é a demora na
introdução de novos serviços. Por exemplo, na criação de um serviço para sub-
scritores de Internet é necessário a compatibilidade com um grande número de
gateways. Em consequência disso, o operador necessita de preparar o novo ser-
viço para correr num diverso leque de dispositivos, de diferentes fabricantes ou
modelos variados. Isto atrasa a introdução de novos serviços, devido à necessid-
ade de serem testados para correr em diferentes equipamentos. Por outro lado,
trocar todos os dispositivos já instalados nos subscritores normalmente não é
economicamente viável.
O surgimento de conceitos de virtualização de rede tais como SDN e NFV in-
troduziram um novo paradigma de rede, com potenciais benefícios para op-
eradores de telecomunicações – facilitação dos procedimentos de configuração da
infraestrutura, consolidação de hardware, e simplificação do processo de desenho
de serviço são alguns aspetos que podem ser alavancados com virtualização. Um
exemplo disso é a virtualização de componentes da infraestrutura do operador,
onde funções são movidas de hardware especializado e monolítico para instâncias
baseadas em software, utilizando equipamento comercial ( COTS).
O trabalho apresentado nesta tese estuda a utilização de técnicas de virtualização
de rede em múltiplos domínios de redes de telecomunicação, propondo várias
contribuições:
xiii

• No domínio das redes de acesso, explora como equipamento da infraestru-
tura pode passar para um paradigma de virtualização, afastando-se das
conhecidas desvantagens da utilização de equipamento legado. Mais con-
cretamente, a investigação focou-se na virtualização do gateway residencial
(RGW) e do BNG. O primeiro é um dispositivo presente nas instalações
do subscritor, que o liga à infraestrutura do operador. O segundo é um
equipamento presente na infraestrutura do operador, ligando as gateways
dos subscritores à rede core.
• No domínio das redes móveis, esta tese explora a arquitetura modular
das redes 5G baseadas em serviços ( SBA) para propor uma framework de
segurança para a monitorização de componentes do core 5G, utilizando
sondas de segurança para capturar e processar eventos, para detetar com-
portamentos suspeitos ou não autorizados.
• Por último, esta tese também explora de que modo a virtualização de
redes pode melhorar a ciber segurança de redes de IACS – de considerável
importância em sistemas de controlo industrial tais como distribuição de
energia e automação industrial – alavancando a virtualização para permitir
monitorização flexível e mitigação ativa.
O trabalho de investigação aqui apresentado foi desenvolvido no âmbito de vários
projetos de investigação (Hollistic SDN, ATENA, Mobilizador 5G e POWER),
cujos parceiros forneceram importantes requisitos, casos de uso e frameworks de
validação.
Palavras-chave: Redes de Operador, Virtualização, Gateways
Residenciais, Border Network Gateway, Cibersegurança
xiv

Foreword
T
he work described in this thesis was carried out at the Network, Com-
munications and Security ( NCS) group of the Center for Informatics
and Systems of the University of Coimbra ( CISUC), within the scope
of the following research projects:
• Holistic SDN . This project aimed at federating multiple SDN network
domains in a distributed platform, with unified management, abstract-
ing the low-level details and SDN interfaces. The work carried out in
the course of this project was accomplished in collaboration with an in-
dustrial partner (Altice Labs). The objective was to explore the use of
SDN and NFV in the context of residential gateway virtualization. The
main result was the exploration of new methods to streamline and accel-
erate the service design of network functions, in the scope of residential
Customer Premises Equipment ( CPE) such as the RGW and the Set-Top
Box (STB). Traditionally slow and cumbersome tasks were improved this
way, resulting in faster and more flexible provisioning of services to resid-
ential subscribers. The contributions related with this project are reflected
mainly in Chapter 3.
• H2020 ATENA. This project addressed the security of Supervisory Con-
trol And Data Acquisition ( SCADA) and Industrial Automation and Con-
trol Systems ( IACS) commonly adopted in Critical Infrastructures ( CI).
One of the research directions of the project – led by University of Coimbra
– focused on exploring the impact of networking paradigms such as SDN
and NFV in these environments – more specifically, how they could be
used to better prevent, detect and mitigate cybersecurity incidents. This
research was carried out in collaboration with several academic and indus-
trial partners. The primary relation of that work with the content of this
thesis was the exploration of how network virtualization techniques could
be used for security purposes in IACS scenarios. The flexibility offered
by SDN and NFV was leveraged to deploy security probes, and to re-
shape the traffic flows when cybersecurity incidents were detected. These
contributions are presented in Chapter 6.
• Mobilizador 5G . The main objective of this project was to research and
develop products that leveraged the novel features enabled by the fifth
generation mobile network ( 5G). It covered multiple domains, and con-
tributed to this thesis specifically in the scope of: (i) telecommunications
operators’ access networks, exploring the virtualization of gateway devices
in residential and business scenarios (in collaboration with partner Altice
Labs); and (ii) the security management of 5G core components (in col-
laboration with partner OneSource). These contributions are discussed in
Chapters 3 and 5, respectively.
xv

• Project POWER . This project aimed at creating an innovative portfo-
lio of products and services for Altice Labs, mostly based on cloud and
cognitive technologies, and covering a wide range of novel technological
aspects of operator environments. A subset of this project focused on
the virtualization of the Border Network Gateway ( BNG), the device re-
sponsible for connecting the subscriber premises to the operator’s infra-
structure. Achieved results include a reference architecture and a Proof
of Concept ( PoC) implementation of the Virtual Border Network Gate-
way ( vBNG) concept, that was used for demonstration and evaluation
purposes. Related contributions are addressed in Chapter 4.
Although these research projects targeted different domains, all of them some-
how embraced leveraging the flexibility that novel network paradigms can offer
across diverse application areas. While H2020 ATENA prioritized the security of
IACS as the main focus of research, projects Hollistic SDN, M5G, and POWER
specifically targeted service delivery and management of telecom operator net-
works. Nevertheless, the telecommunication infrastructure is itself a branch of
the IACS, interconnecting various types of Critical Infrastructures. Without a
working telecommunications service, the inter- and intra-dependability of Crit-
ical Infrastructures becomes compromised. As an example, different sites of
an energy distribution system would not be able to communicate. Moreover,
traditional Internet Protocol ( IP) networking is becoming increasingly common
within IACS, replacing legacy communication technologies. These may benefit
from the advantages that software-oriented networking concepts are capable of
delivering.
The research work presented in this thesis aldo led to the following publica-
tions:
Journal papers:
• Proença, J. , Cruz, T., Simões, P., and Monteiro, E. (2018). Virtualiza-
tion of Residential Gateways: A Comprehensive Survey. IEEE Commu-
nications Surveys and Tutorials , 21 (2): 1462–1482, Secondquarter
2019, doi: 10.1109/COMST.2018.2874827.
Main contributions: State-of-the-Art survey on the virtualization of
Virtualized Residential Gateway ( vRGW), and the proposal of a reference
taxonomy classifying vRGW-enabling initiatives.
• Adamsky, F., Aubigny, M., Battisti, F., Carli, M., Cimorelli, F., Cruz, T.,
Di Giorgio, A., Foglietta, C., Galli, A., Giuseppi, A., Liberati, F., Neri,A.,
Panzieri, S., Pascucci, F., Proença, J. , Pucci, P., Rosa, L., and Soua, R.
(2018). Integrated Protection of Industrial Control Systems from Cyber-
attacks: the ATENA Approach. International Journal of Critical
Infrastructure Protection, 21:72–82, doi: 10.1016/j.ijcip.2018.04.004
Main contributions: Contribution to the definition of the Intrusion
and Anomaly Detection System ( IADS), within the ATENA high-level
architecture.
xvi

• Cruz, T., Proença, J. , Simões, P., Aubigny, M., Oedraogo, M., Grazi-
ano, A., and Yasakhetu, L. (2014). Improving cyber-security awareness
on industrial control systems: The CockpitCI approach. Journal of
Information Warfare , 13(4):27–41, ISSN 1445 3347 (online) / ISSN
445-3312 (printed), Online: https://www.jstor.org/stable/26487465.
Main contributions: Contribution to the architecture definition for an
IACS cyber-detection and analysis layer. Definition of the threat detection
agents used in the architecture to capture security events.
• Cruz, T., Proença, J ., Simões, P., Aubigny, M., Ouedraogo, M., Grazi-
ano, A., and Maglaras, L. (2014). A Distributed IDS for Industrial Control
Systems. International Journal of Cyber Warfare and Terror-
ism, 4(2):1–22
Main contributions: Contribution to the architecture definition of a
IACS threat detection platform, as well as conception of detection agents
such as the specialized honeypot and Shadow Security Unit ( SSU).
• Cruz, T., Queiroz, R., Proença, J ., Simões, P., and Monteiro, E.
(2016). Leveraging Virtualization Technologies to Improve SCADA ICS
Security. Journal of Information Warfare , 15(3):81–100, URL: ht-
tps://www.jstor.org/stable/26502746
Main contributions: Contribution to the documentation of the State-
of-the-Art ( SoA) related to SCADA and IACS.
• Cruz, T., Rosa, L., Proença, J. , Maglaras, L., Aubigny, M., Lev, L.,
Jiang, J., and Simões, P. (2016). A Cybersecurity Detection Frame-
work for Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition Systems. IEEE
Transactions on Industrial Informatics , 12(6):2236-2246, doi:
10.1109/tii.2016.2599841
Main contributions: Contribution to the design of the Perimeter Intru-
sion Detection System ( PIDS) architecture; SSU and honeypot detection
agents.
Conference papers:
• Proença, J. , Cruz, T., Monteiro, E., and Simões, P. (2015). How to
use Software–Defined Networking to Improve Security–a Survey. In Pro-
ceedings of the 14th European Conference on Cyber Warfare
and Security 2015: ECCWS 2015 , page 220. Academic Conferences
Limited, doi: 10.13140/RG.2.1.4877.1686
Main contributions: A survey of how to use SDN to increase the cyber-
security of telecommunication networks.
• Proença, J ., Cruz, T., Simões, P., Gaspar, G., Parreira, B., Laran-
jeira, A., and Bastos, F. (2017). Building an NFV-Based vRGW: les-
sons learned. In 14th IEEE Consumer Communications and Net-
xvii

working Conference (CCNC 2017) , Las Vegas, NV, USA, 2017, pp.
653-658, doi :10.1109/CCNC.2017.7983208.
Main contributions: Customizing OpenStack through the development
of networking features to support a traffic steering function, enabling

— 1 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
T
elecommunications Service Providers ( TSP) play a pivotal role in
modern society, providing a comprehensive array of services that are
deeply integrated across multiple domains. Multiservice Operators
provide a range of services to their subscribers, from residential broadband access
to cellular networks and business services. The complexity and service support
requirements of these operator networks had an exponential surge in recent years,
which is predicted to continue. As a result, TSPs face a large set of challenges
in their operation. The mounting pressure on operators comes from a multi-
tude of factors, including the growth of subscribers and traffic, the proliferation
of connected User Equipment ( UE), and new services with domain-specific re-
quirements such as Industrial Internet of Things ( IIoT). Additionally, the nature
of certain services provided to subscribers has become increasingly demanding,
with high-bandwidth (e.g., high resolution streaming services) and latency sens-
itive applications such as Gaming-as-a-Service ( GaaS) requiring seamless and
responsive connectivity.
Looking at industrial domains, there has also been an increase in the adoption of
mainstream networking technologies within Industrial Automation and Control
Systems ( IACS), as a result of the benefits of connecting production systems
to Information and Communications Technology ( ICT)-based processes, includ-
ing easier monitoring and management tasks, and connecting the production
with stocking applications. Also, several control protocols originally developed
for isolated serial communication buses were later adapted to use Transmission
Control Protocol ( TCP) encapsulation, as is the case of Modbus. As industries
embrace the potential of Industry 4.0 and Internet of Things ( IoT), there is a
greater reliance on interconnected devices, sensors, and data-driven processes.
Novel private 5G networks can also be useful in IACS environments to intercon-
nect remote sites of an industry. Telecom operators are required to provision
and manage these networks, which must support critical applications as well as
real-time data exchange, a common requirement in these latency-sensitive do-
mains. This results in a growing need for segmented and secure communication
networks within these specific sectors.
Current trends in virtualization offer significant advantages for operators, includ-
ing increased flexibility in their infrastructure, reduced management complexity
and costs, as well as consolidation of hardware resources, allowing efficiency im-
provements. They must continuously innovate and optimize the infrastructure
to meet modern needs and reduce the financial burden while facing demands for
faster and more reliable connectivity, simultaneously accommodating an expo-
nential growth of connected devices.
1.1 Motivation and Problem Statement
Traditionally, telecommunication operators’ infrastructures are known to evolve
and change at a slow pace, adding new features on top of existing infrastructure
and processes. Due to their typically large and complex nature, technological
updates are slow and incremental, often avoiding complete re-designs of the
— 2 —

1.1. MOTIV ATION AND PROBLEM STATEMENT
infrastructure. This frequently results in legacy processes being maintained for
long periods. Consequently, innovations need to be deployed on top of them,
which limits their capabilities. As an example, updating wireless cellular network
generations is a carefully paced and deliberate process. A sudden change would
be unfeasible, not only financially, but also due to the potential to disrupt service
quality and continuity.
The time taken by most vendors to update equipment is proving to be a signific-
ant constraint in infrastructure development. Additionally, they are often closed
and proprietary, limiting their compatibility and creating further constraints.
As a result, operators face excessive costs in terms of both Capital Expendit-
ure (CAPEX) and Operational Expenditure ( OPEX), burdensome management,
and an infrastructure that is struggling to keep up with the speed of new service
offers.
Over the years, virtualization has offered numerous advantages across several
domains. For instance, hardware virtualization at datacenter level enabled op-
erators to host applications and services on VMs rather than deploying them
directly on bare metal servers. Despite initial drawbacks, like potential perform-
ance limitations, the benefits proved to be more significant, including reducing
excessive hardware use by consolidating several VMs in each server. This res-
ults in cost savings and flexibility. Technological evolution in hardware and
hypervisors such as hardware virtualization have significantly improved the per-
formance of virtualized applications. Today, server virtualization has become a
well-established technology in these environments.
Operators are now experiencing a growing pressure on their infrastructure as a
result of various factors. These include the growth of internet traffic, due to the
widespread adoption of digital technologies across several domains. Moreover,
the proliferation of connected devices ranging from smartphones and IoT to
broadband access subscribers, has greatly increased the amount of connected
devices the operator must support. Also, the introduction of new services and
bandwidth-intensive applications added another layer of complexity for operat-
ors to manage. Collectively, these challenges have created a highly demanding
environment that operators must adapt to.
In response to these evolving challenges, operators are trying to explore new
paradigms to improve their infrastructure, namely using software-defined net-
working concepts to extend virtualization to new parts of their infrastructure, in-
cluding equipment installed in the customer premises and access networks.
The research work described in this thesis aligns with those efforts, since it
addresses the following research question: how can network virtualization tech-
niques improve different domains of telecommunications’ networks . More spe-
cifically, this broad scope research question has been materialized into the fol-
lowing sub-questions:
• RQ 1 – How can telecommunications operators improve their service offer-
ings by migrating from specialized hardware appliances to software-based
virtualized functions?
— 3 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
• RQ 2 – What is the feasibility of virtualizing telecommunications operat-
ors’ hardware in the aggregation points of the network edge?
• RQ 3 – Which benefits can telecommunications operators expect when
virtualizing functions previously performed by hardware deployed at the
customer premises?
• RQ 4 – How can telecommunications operators take advantage of modern
mobile wireless network architectures such as the 5G SBA to improve
cybersecurity?
• RQ 5 – How can network virtualization concepts benefit the IACS domain?
1.2 Objectives and Contributions
The main goal of the research work presented in this thesis is to explore the po-
tential applications of virtualization principles in multiservice contexts, focused
on the domains of telecommunications networks and IACS, including possible
impacts on operation and management processes. To achieve this, the following
objectives have been defined:
• Literature review on the topic of telecom operator networks, covering dif-
ferent points of broadband subscriber services. This review identifies the
challenges associated with different stages of service delivery, from access
networks to subscriber premises, highlighting limitations of traditional net-
work architectures and possible benefits brought by new software-based
paradigms.
• To explore virtualization concepts such as SDN and NFV as means to
virtualize operator infrastructure components, particularly residential and
business gateways, and Border Network Gateway; to propose architec-
tures to take advantage of these concepts; and to validate them using PoC
implementations.
• To enhance the cybersecurity of novel communication architectures such
as 5G networks; and to explore virtualization concepts in the domain of
cybersecurity for Industrial Automation and Control Systems.
These objectives guided the research work associated with this thesis, and are
reflected across several chapters of this thesis.
The first objective (literature review) served as a foundation and aimed to gather
knowledge in the domain. As it encompasses various subtopics, it is split into
several parts: the broader concepts of the review are presented in Chapter 2,
as general background, and the more specialized domain-specific surveys are
presented in the beginning of each of the following chapters.
The second objective involves exploring virtualization concepts in broadband ser-
vice scenarios. Within this line of research, two main areas were identified: the
BNG, on the operator infrastructure access network, and the customer premises
— 4 —

1.2. OBJECTIVES AND CONTRIBUTIONS
gateway. Although they are intrinsically connected to each other in the service
delivery chain, this thesis addresses them separately, in Chapters 3 and 4.
The final objective is focused on the disruptive approach that virtualization con-
cepts bring to networking. On the one hand, the emergence of 5G brought a
new approach to telecommunication networks. The proliferation of virtualiza-
tion and a software-based approach to networking contributed to the modular
SBA adopted in 5G. While it brought several advantages, it also has the po-
tential to introduce new security risks. These concerns and possible solutions
are addressed in Chapter 5. On the other hand, the rise of virtualization con-
cepts may also be used to improve security strategies in IACS communication
networks. Initially developed several decades ago as fully isolated systems run-
ning specialized communication buses and protocols, these systems have since
been integrated into the ICT networks, and gradually connected to the inter-
net. However, several factors have been keeping them outdated and out of line
with modern best practices in network security. Network virtualization concepts
have the potential to improve the security of these specialized systems. This is
addressed in Chapter 6.
The research work hereby presented has also led to several contributions, of
which the following should be highlighted:
• A survey on subscriber gateways, detailing the evolution of the traditional
model, current challenges, and research efforts being done to tackle them.
• A SoA focused on the BNG component of the operator’s access network,
responsible for the aggregation of subscriber connections. This SoA also
describes how this component evolved over time, the complex problems
and limitations it poses for the operator, and current strategies under
research to tackle it.
• An architecture and two PoCs for virtualizing the CPE gateway, targeting
different aspects of its virtualization. The first one is specifically focused
on improving service design for operators, and the integration with legacy
Business Support System ( BSS) and Operations Support System ( OSS)
architectures. The second PoC is focused on service delivery, flexibility of
virtualized function deployment location, and subscriber experience.
• An architecture for virtualizing the BNG, proposing a modular approach
for deploying functions within a BNG instance, and integration with a
vCPE enabled infrastructure. A vBNG PoC focused on the impact of
applying traffic policies (such as Access Control List ( ACL), shaping and
QoS) when using software-based functions to build a vBNG.
• A framework to improve the security of 5G networks, focused on the se-
curity of individual components of the 5G core. A PoC of the architecture
to secure them from attacks has also been implemented and validated.
• Two specialized security probes to be deployed in partially virtualized
IACS environments, to feed the IADS security framework. These probes
are responsible for collecting data that will be used for intrusion detection
— 5 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
within the IADS framework.
1.3 Outline of the Thesis
The remainder of this thesis is structured as follows:
• Chapter 2 presents a general background for the research context. This
background will be latter complemented with domain-specific literature
surveys for each of the following chapters.
• Chapter 3 explores the virtualization of residential and business gateways,
the devices that connect the broadband subscriber UE to the operator’s
infrastructure. Moreover, the chapter presents two vCPE PoCs and asso-
ciated validation work.
• Chapter 4 proposes an architecture to enable the virtualization of the
BNG. This chapter also presents a PoC that was used to analyse perform-
ance aspects of BNG function virtualization.
• Chapter 5 proposes an Event Processing and Visualization Platform to
improve the security of 5G core components, which receives and processes
security events generated from 5G core nodes and probes.
• Chapter 6 discusses the role that network virtualization can have in the
IACS domain. Specifically, the following contributions are proposed: the
IADS (as part of a collective work from the CISUC team), and two security
probes (a domain-specific honeypot and a Shadow Security Unit for stealth
monitoring of Programmable Logic Controller ( PLC) and similar devices).
• Chapter 7 concludes this document, offering a synthesis of the thesis and
a discussing possible research paths for extending this work.
Figure 1.1 provides a visual representation of the structure of the thesis. The two
initial chapters are structured as introductory context, providing the necessary
background supporting the concepts that are present in the next chapters. Part
II presents different research subtopics, each in their separate chapter. Finally,
part III concludes this thesis, presenting the conclusions of the research work
and discussing possible paths for future work.

CHAPTER 2. SOFTW ARIZATION AND VIRTUALIZATION IN
TELECOMMUNICATIONS OPERATORS’ ENVIRONMENTS
I
n computing and communication infrastructures, there is an ongoing effort
to virtualize components and appliances that traditionally were built on
top of closed and proprietary devices. This enables not only shifting from
hardware-based components, but also to provide open and well-documented re-
quirements and standardized interfaces. As a result, interoperability between
different vendors is increased, fostering competition while reducing vendor lock-
in constraints.
This drive for vendor interoperability is similarly observed in various areas of
telecommunications infrastructures, including wireless mobile networks. For ex-
ample, one architectural design attribute of 5G is to decouple the internal com-
ponents of its core network, increasing flexibility and reducing vendor lock-in. In
a different context, broadband access operators are recognizing the benefits that
infrastructure virtualization can offer, including easier management, cost reduc-
tion, and improved service to their subscribers. The 5G architectural decoupling
is relevant within the scope of the research work presented in Chapter 5, sim-
plifying the integration between the components of the 5G core and the Event
Processing and Visualization Platform architecture. The broadband access scen-
ario, on the other hand, is relevant to the research work presented in Chapter 3
and Chapter 4.
This chapter serves as a short introduction for the underlying virtualization
technologies that have enabled telecommunications operators to optimize their
infrastructure and enhance their services. It details the base concepts of two key
approaches that were disruptive to the way that operators build, maintain, and
manage their infrastructures: Software-Defined Networking ( SDN) and Network
Function Virtualization ( NFV). It should be noted that parts of this chapter
have already been published in Proença et al. [2015].
It should also be noted that this chapter is essentially background material. The
domain-specific literature surveys will be presented at the beginning of each of
the next chapters.
2.1 Software-based Network Control
The term Software-Defined Networking ( SDN) was first introduced by Greene
[2009], referring to the OpenFlow project ( Pfaff et al. [2015]) developed at the
Stanford University, which eventually became the first widely used SDN-enabling
technology. The OpenFlow standard, managed by the Open Networking Found-
ation ( ONF), and disrupted the vertical integration that is characteristic of
the traditional networking model, by decoupling the control and data planes
(Prabha et al. [2022]; Amin et al. [2018] Kreutz et al. [2015]) to introduce direct
programmability into the network.
In SDN, the control plane is moved away from the forwarding elements (i.e.,
routers), and placed in a logically centralized controller. This does not imply a
— 10 —

2.1. SOFTW ARE-BASED NETWORK CONTROL
physical concentration of functionality, as the controller functions can be per-
formed by several instances to improve, for example, scalability and resilience
(Yeganeh et al. [2013]). The data plane remains in the forwarding elements
and their task becomes focused only on traffic forwarding. These changes allow
the controller to have a broader view of the network, compared with the nar-
row view that each individual device (e.g., router or switch) has in traditional
networks.
The SDN paradigm allows an increase in flexibility, and simplifies network man-
agement, especially on complex scenarios ( Kreutz et al. [2015]). Additionally,
packet forwarding is flow-oriented, taking into account a set of parameters in-
cluding both origin and destination, rather than focusing mostly on destination
(as is generally done in traditional packet forwarding). Flow policies are gran-
ted by a centralized controller, which is responsible for managing forwarding
element policies – as an example, an SDN-capable switch can be reconfigured
on-the-fly over the network, according to network service and application needs.
Furthermore, an SDN network can be abstracted into multiple logically isolated
networks, allowing multiple users to share the network individually using SDN
hypervisors ( Blenk et al. [2015]). As control functions are decoupled from for-
warding elements and consolidated on the SDN controller, the latter will have a
broad perspective of the network domain under its control, contrasting with the
narrow view that characterizes standalone forwarding elements in a traditional
IP network. Examples of SDN protocols include IETF Forwarding and Control
Element Separation ( ForCES [2014]) and OpenFlow ( Pfaff et al. [2015]).
OpenFlow
OpenFlow defines how traffic is forwarded using flow rules. As a result, custom-
ized control planes can be created to use OpenFlow as an interface to populate
the switch forwarding tables ( Yang et al. [2021]). Being open and standardized,
Openflow can control switches from different vendors simultaneously. However,
it lacked the ability to change the switches’ behaviour since they have a fixed
functionality. Initially, it supported few network protocols (Ethernet, VLANs,
IPv4, ACLs), but additional protocols were included in new releases of the spe-
cification as it adoption spread. Nevertheless, a fixed behaviour in the switch
Application-Specific Integrated Circuit ( ASIC) still restricts the flexibility for
creating and managing network infrastructures.
Programming Protocol-Independent Packet Processors – P4
Considering the limitations of a fixed switch behaviour in traditional equipment
development, Programming Protocol-Independent Packet Processors ( P4) aims
to reduce them. For example, adding a new feature to a switch can take up
to four years ( McKeown and Rexford [2016]). P4 was developed to tackle this
limitation. It is not an evolution of OpenFlow; rather, it considers programmable
networks from a different perspective ( Kaur et al. [2021]). OpenFlow forced
a bottom-up network design, as the ASIC capabilities would dictate how the
control plane could be built. So, in order to have a top-down design, P4 is used
with programmable chips to have a customizable data plane. It was designed
as a programming language specifically for network devices such as switches
— 11 —

CHAPTER 2. SOFTW ARIZATION AND VIRTUALIZATION IN
TELECOMMUNICATIONS OPERATORS’ ENVIRONMENTS
and routers. First presented by Bosshart et al. [2014], this language provides
a way to define how packets are processed and forwarded within a network,
enabling programmability and customization of network devices. This last point
is the main difference from OpenFlow: P4 programs the forwarding devices,
while OpenFlow defines the rules at control level, without changing the switches
behaviour. P4 allows network operators and researchers to define and implement
their own forwarding behaviours and protocols independently. At the end, the
two technologies can work together to improve network programmability.
Next Generation Software-Defined Networking
As the adoption for programmable networks grew, the limitations of the existing
solutions became more evident, but also more clear about what could be done to
improve them. OpenFlow set the stage for software-based networks but, despite
its improvements over traditional networking, it had limitations on its scope
of action. As described earlier, OpenFlow enabled separation of the control
and data planes, and the development of logically centralized control planes,
which enhanced network flexibility. However, the behaviour of switching ASICs
remained unchanged, presenting a limiting factor. P4 built on this by extending
SDN to data plane devices such as switches and routers with programmable
silicon, enabling their behaviour to be customized as needed.
To leverage these concepts, the ONF has bundled up a set of technologies into an
open source networking platform, referred as Next Generation Software-Defined
Networking (NG-SDN). The idea is to provide a hardware independent platform,
built on open source software deployable in white box hardware. NG-SDN is
composed of different ONF projects, including (from ONF [2019]):
• uONOS – A logically centralized, cloud-native SDN control and configur-
ation platform that manages a network of Stratum-enabled switches.
• Stratum – Thin switch Operating System ( OS) supporting Next-Gen SDN
interfaces (P4Runtime, network management interface ( gNMI), network
operations interface ( gNOI), and Open Config models.
• NG-SDN Verification Engine – Fine grained network measurement of every
packet and flow enables ongoing verification of the precise behaviour of the
network. Automated tuning and updates ensure the network is delivering
the precise behaviour defined by the operator, thus ensuring the network
is performing as specified.
• NG-SDN Operationalization Framework – Network becomes managed with
a CI/CD toolchain, enabling rapid updates, automated rollouts, verific-
ation and automated rollbacks. The network becomes a dynamic and
nimble resource that can be leveraged and updated without fear that
changes will break the operator’s infrastructure.
Knowledge-Defined Networking
Programmable networks have unlocked new possibilities in network manage-
ment, providing flexibility and northbound interfaces to create higher abstrac-
tion layers. Moreover, they can act as a foundation for new advances in network
— 12 —

2.2. NETWORK FUNCTION SHIFT FROM DEDICATED HARDW ARE
TO SOFTW ARE
management. To address the challenges of future networks, particularly in the
context of wireless cellular networks, there is a growing focus on introducing
intelligence into network management. One notable approach is Knowledge-
Defined Networking ( KDN), which aims at providing intelligence to networks
(Ashtari et al. [2022]; Wijesekara and Gunawardena [2023]). KDN aims to op-
timize network operation using Machine Learning ( ML) algorithms to analysing
telemetry data collected from the network. The integration of SDN plays a
pivotal role in KDN, providing a global view of the network, and also exposes
the interfaces to program the network in a flexible way.
In the future, these concepts are expected to further develop and evolve, building
on the concept of increasingly flexible and programmable networking.
2.2 Network Function Shift from Dedicated Hardware to
Software
As network applications and service requirements scale and evolve, both in terms
of capacity and complexity, the supporting operator infrastructure needs to re-
sort to specific network management and traffic policies that cannot be provided
by the network, in order to keep up with demands. Network Function Virtu-
alization ( NFV) provides a significant contribution to address this problem, by
enabling on-demand creation of flexible network services through a chain-based,
composition mechanism that uses network functions implemented in Virtual Net-
work Function ( VNF)1, that can comprise functions such as Network Address
Translation (NAT), Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol ( DHCP), BNG, Fire-
walls, implemented as VMs or containerized appliances (He et al. [2023]; Mijumbi
et al. [2016]; Chiosi et al. [2013]; Chiosi et al. [2012]).
The virtualization of Network Functions ( NFs) can bring several benefits to
the TSP. Consolidating network appliances into virtualized software running
on COTS hardware has the potential to reduce capital investment and lower
energy consumption ( Thyagaturu et al. [2022]). Moreover, it can reduce the
time to market of new services from months and years to weeks or days, ac-
cording to Lemke [2015]. Besides having a faster introduction, Han et al. [2015]
points out the possibility of targeted and tailored services as a result of NFV
flexibility.
NFV, as standardized by the ETSI NFV Industry Specification Group ( ISG)
(ETSI [2012]), promotes the decoupling between network capacity and func-
tionality, abstracting end-to-end services as entities using a service chaining ap-
proach, where services are modelled and described by composition of elementary
VNFs, Physical Network Functions ( PNFs) and endpoints, chained together by
a Forwarding Graph ( FG), as shown in Figure 2.1. This figure shows the virtu-
alization layer performing an abstraction between the physical hardware (hosted
in the NFVI point-of-presence, in data centres) and the software/virtualized in-
stances. Moreover, FGs can be nested to define complex function blocks.
1VNF instances are the building blocks that enable the NFV concept

While VNFs are implemented in the form of software modules (usually hos-
ted within containers or VM instances, providing reusable function templates),
PNFs are conventional network devices with close coupled software and hard-
ware that perform network functions. The logical links in a FG constitute a
network overlay, which can be implemented using SDN or network virtualiza-
tion technologies such as Virtual Local Area Networks ( VLANs) ( IEEE [2014])
or Multi Protocol Label Switching ( MPLS) pseudowires ( Bryant et al. [2005]),
as depicted in Figure 2.1.
NFV and SDN are complementary technologies: while the first attempts to
optimize and streamline the deployment of network functions (firewalls, load
balancers, etc.), the second targets the optimization of the network that supports
such services.
The ETSI Model
The ETSI NFV architectural reference framework is depicted in Figure 2.2, being
composed of several functional modules:
• The NFVI domain, which abstracts the computing, storage, and network-
ing resources (provided using COTS hardware, accelerators, hypervisors,
among other components) providing support for the VNF.
• The Virtual Network Function domain, containing the VNF instances (de-
ployed on VMs, for instance) which run on top of the NFVI, also including
Element Management System (EMS) to ease integration with existing OSS
and BSS.
• The NFV Management and Orchestration ( MANO) domain, which deals
with orchestration and lifecycle management of physical and/or software
— 14 —

2.2. NETWORK FUNCTION SHIFT FROM DEDICATED HARDW ARE
TO SOFTW ARE
resources that support the infrastructure virtualization and the lifecycle
management of VNF and the services built using them.
Also, NFVI resources may be distributed over different Points-of-Presence
(NFVI Point of Presence ( PoP)), geographically spread.

Figure 2.2 : ETSI NFV framework architecture (adapted from ETSI [2014]).
The MANO domain is in charge of the virtualization-specific tasks for the NFV
framework, encompassing several components: the NFV Orchestrator, which
takes care of network service lifecycle management across the operator domain
(datacentre included); the VNF Manager (VNFM) which deals with the lifecycle
management for the VNF instances; and the Virtualized Infrastructure Manager
(VIM), which is responsible for NFVI resource management. A set of metadata
sources describe service, VNF and Infrastructure requirements, providing the
MANO with information about the resources managed. This provides the means
for different resource providers (for instance VNF or infrastructure, such as an
hypervisor) to integrate within the NFV framework, by implementing the glue
Application Programming Interface ( API) and related descriptions.
In the ETSI model, SDN can play a decisive role to implement the network
virtualization mechanisms that support the logical links of a FG service chain,
but also to provide what the ETSI describes as the ” NFVI as a Service” use
case, enabling operators to lease NFVI resources from third-parties, to improve
service coverage.
As for the impact on OSS/BSS systems, the MANO components for the ETSI
NFVI have been designed and laid out to interact with existing OSS/BSS sys-
tems (albeit it is recognized that NFV will most likely have a profound effect on
traditional OSS/BSS architectures). However, the interfaces within the MANO
— 15 —

CHAPTER 2. SOFTW ARIZATION AND VIRTUALIZATION IN
TELECOMMUNICATIONS OPERATORS’ ENVIRONMENTS
domain, and between it and the OSS, still need to be standardized to reduce the
integration effort in a heterogeneous multi-vendor infrastructure. In order to
enable automation and agile management, the NFV MANO and OSS/BSS need
to agree on interfaces and associated information and data models, as well as
their business processes (such as billing or security). The impact on the existing
OSS will depend on its own nature – in some situations it may be as simple as
configuring an integration agent, while in others it might imply profound con-
figuration changes and even roll-out of new OSS components. The ETSI NFV
ISG is working to minimize the OPEX and integration complexity but, once
again, this is a work in progress as these aspects will need further development,
involving other standardization bodies and organisms.
The ETSI NFV model provides a base for the development of a consensus
among operators and vendors. However, as previously described, it is known
that some issues were left out in terms of interfaces and data models ( ETSI
[2014]). The lack of definition in some points of ETSI’s architectural framework
regarding interfaces and interoperability between vendors and operators reflec-
ted on several projects being ”based on the ETSI framework” (such as ZOOM
TM Forum [2016], CloudNFV Nolle [2016], CloudBand Nolle [2016], ExperiaS-
phere Nolle [2014], HP OpenNFV HP [2015], or Planet Orchestrate Blueplanet
[2014]) but without being compatible between them in terms of functions and
services ( Kołakowski et al. [2021]; Mijumbi et al. [2015]).
In the beginning of 2016 ETSI announced the project ETSI Open Source Mano
(OSM) (ETSI [2016b]). The main goal was to develop an NFV MANO software
implementation to tackle some of the issues that were left out by the ETSI NFV
architecture, leading to both initiatives ( OSM and NFV ISG) complementing
the work of each other.
The initial OSM proof of concept depicted a multi-vendor environment cap-
able of using and extending open-source based components, while being or-
chestrated by an open-source orchestrator. The OSM orchestrator manages a
heterogeneous cloud (composed by OpenStack 2 and OpenVIM 3) and deploys,
connects, and configures the NFV infrastructure that supports Virtual Private
Network (VPN) and Voice over Internet Protocol ( VoIP) services. The use case
covers a great deal of challenges that real production network would require,
such as performance, multi-site orchestration, and multiple vendors. It also
requires configuration of both physical and virtual components. The project fol-
lows a cadence of two releases per year, alternating between standard and Long
Term Support ( LTS). It is currently on release 15 (fifteen) as of August 2024.
OSM is aligned with ETSI NFV, and uses a well known Information Model ( IM)
to enable the full lifecycle of NFs. Moreover, it provides a Northbound Inter-
face ( NBI) based on NFV SOL005.
2https://www.openstack.org/
3https://osm.etsi.org/wikipub/index.php/OpenVIM_installation

2.3. SUMMARY
2.3 Summary
This chapter provided a context for the virtualization concepts that are used in
the remainder of this thesis. Specifically, the concept of virtualizing networks,
and adding programmability to their design and use; and virtualizing networks
functions in COTS equipment that traditionally were implemented using spe-
cialized hardware.
Although this chapter had a special focus on the operator network domain,
which was one of the main drivers for the development and adoption of these
technologies, they can be of use in different scenarios, such as Industrial Auto-
mation and Control Systems ( IACS). This last domain is approached in this
thesis in Chapter 6.

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
T
his chapter focuses on the effort to virtualize one specific type of Cus-
tomer Premises Equipment ( CPE) – the Gateway ( GW). This device
is commonly found within TSP customers (also known as subscribers)
premises, such as residential homes or business offices, acting as the connection
point between the subscriber’s private network and the operator’s infrastructure,
providing wireless or wired connection for subscriber User Equipment ( UE) such
as smartphones or computers. Considering that this device is used to connect
premises devices to the operator network and to the Internet, it must host a
set of NFs. Essential functions to provide basic functionality include routing,
firewall, DHCP, and NAT. Moreover, additional functions can be deployed to
improve the subscriber service and increase the operator revenue, such as VoIP
or URL filtering. Traditionally, these devices have been deployed as stand-alone
instances, with all the functions built into the device. However, operators have
been exploring the benefits of virtualizing the functions performed by this CPE
in an effort to improve service flexibility, accelerate the development and deploy-
ment of new services, and reduce operation costs.
In the existing literature, this gateway device is frequently mentioned by diverse
designations, including CPE, RGW, GW, and Home Gateway ( HGW). This
chapter will refer to the device as CPE and Residential Gateway ( RGW). Al-
though CPE is a generic term to describe several types of equipment located
in the customer premises, including STB, and VoIP base stations, it is used
in this chapter to refer specifically the gateway. Moreover, the scope of ap-
plication described in this chapter is relevant to both residential and business
scenarios.
The first part of this chapter provides a state of the art in virtualization of
the customers gateway, presenting its motivation and current initiatives and re-
search efforts. The second part of the chapter introduces two PoCs that were
developed to study two different aspects. The first analyses how virtualization
can improve service design and implementation, with a specific focus on flex-
ibility and easiness to create and deploy broadband services. The second PoC
analyses two different deployment options for the virtualized functions: a scen-
ario where all functions are being deployed at the operator infrastructure, and
a combination, with some functions being virtualized in the operator infrastruc-
ture and others at the customer premises. The work presented in this chapter
has been published in Proença et al. [2015]; Proença et al. [2017]; Proença et al.
[2018]; Proença et al. [2021]; and Proença et al. [2021a].
3.1 Context
The evolution of the access network infrastructures, with the broad deployment
of FTTx and Data Over Cable Service Interface Specification ( DOCSIS) tech-
nologies, has brought considerable performance benefits, opening up new oppor-
tunities for operators to rethink their role and move towards converged service
— 20 —

3.2. ACCESS INFRASTRUCTURE BACKGROUND AND EVOLUTION
delivery models (e.g., n-Play services). The RGW plays an important part in this
model, being deployed at the customer premises to connect the home network
to the operator network. This devices provide services such as Domain Name
System (DNS), DHCP, NAT, firewall, URL filtering, Digital Living Network Al-
liance (DLNA) server, routing, and (wired and wireless) connectivity. Moreover,
they also enable diverse value-added services, including Internet Protocol Televi-
sion ( IPTV) – e.g., via Internet Group Management Protocol ( IGMP) proxying
(Cain et al. [2002]) – Virtual Channel Identifier ( VCI)/VLAN) management
and Session Initiation Protocol ( SIP) gateways ( Rosenberg et al. [2002]) and/or
user agents (for integrated analogue telephone interfaces), among others ( Waring
[1999]; Hartog et al. [2004]).
The RGW service footprint and complexity have increased over time, resulting in
higher costs for the operator both in terms of deployment and maintenance. This
increase is amplified by the scale factor (one RGW per customer), representing
a considerable investment ( Xie et al. [2013]). Furthermore, device diversity
(vendor, model and even firmware version) and hardware obsolescence further
increase management costs and create an obstacle to the quick introduction
of new functionalities depending on the RGW. At the limit, introducing new
services may require massive RGW replacement programs whose costs surpass
the projected return from those new services.
With the rise of cloud computing and the virtualization paradigms, together with
the evolution of the access network role and supporting infrastructure, RGWs
are slowly becoming an anachronism that mostly embodies the legacy access net-
work model. In this perspective, virtualizing the RGW appears as an interesting
solution, leading to substantial research efforts. Several operators, equipment
vendors and academics have proposed vRGW frameworks, each of them with
their own specific characteristics, regarding the location of computing resources,
the architectural aspects of the virtualized solution, or the connection between
the home environment and the virtualized components. However, initial ap-
proaches suffered from several shortcomings, due to issues such as the need to
encapsulate customer data circuits up to the vRGW hosting data centre in a scal-
able way, or because of the increased infrastructure complexity. More recently,
the introduction of technologies such as SDN and NFV, which were previously
introduced in Chapter 2, have enabled more satisfactory vRGW framework ap-
proaches, tackling some of the limitations that hampered their wide acceptance
in the past.
3.2 Access Infrastructure Background and Evolution
Expansion of high-speed broadband access networks and increased coverage in
terms of connected households were instrumental to enable converged n-play of-
fers and cloud-based value-added services, which are displacing traditional com-
munication and service delivery models to give place to an everything-over-IP
approach as presented by Royon [2007] and De Smedt et al. [2006]. Con-
sequently, the residential Local Area Network ( LAN) ecosystem has evolved to
encompass a series of devices such as Personal Computers ( PCs), STBs, VoIP
— 21 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
telephones, smartphones, media players, smart Televisions ( TVs) and storage
devices, providing access to a diversified range of services to broadband custom-
ers. This has progressively pushed TSP players towards a service-centric model,
where the RGW stands in the nexus between the connectivity, services, and
customer environments.
Deployed within customer premises, RGWs are mostly embedded systems plat-
forms which mediate between the home and the operator access networks and
whose main role has not significantly changed over time. As explained in the
previous Section, the RGW is responsible for the handling of a number of local
network services.
The RGW accounts for a considerable share of the broadband network access
service deployment costs, also constituting a single point of failure. Deployed
right at the intersection of the customer premises and the access network, an
RGW failure may render inoperative services such as TV or fixed telephone –
especially in consolidated n-play service scenarios. As such, its failure has a
potential impact in terms of logistics (due to on-site maintenance, in worst-case
scenarios), management and customer loyalty.
Also, the RGW may pose an obstacle for remote diagnostics and troubleshooting
of devices and services within the customer premises LAN (for instance, due to
NAT translation), also constituting an obstruction for introducing new services
or adding features to existing ones. In fact, service time to market is frequently
dependent on the device manufacturer to introduce support for new services,
something often aggravated by the subsequent need to remotely perform a mass
update of already deployed equipment – this may be impossible at all for RGW
models past the end of their lifecycle support, or because their capabilities are
too limited for the intended purposes.
Device model and manufacturer diversity is another problem: it is difficult for an
operator to maintain a homogeneous set of RGWs, as even a single model may
have minor firmware and hardware revisions that gradually compromise uni-
formity and hamper troubleshooting and management operations. Equipment
diversity means that even small differences may hamper the deployment of new
services (e.g., a limited flash memory capacity for embedded firmware may limit
update possibilities). Even when they are possible, mass firmware upgrades – a
potentially troublesome operation – depend on specific model or vendor-related
procedures or the management of different device data models (aggravated by
the use of vendor-specific extensions) and pose a significant risk.
This made the case for rethinking the RGW, in order to ease or solve its in-
herent problems, streamlining its architecture in some way or even removing it
completely. In this perspective, virtualizing the RGW comes as an interesting
proposition, by moving the bulk of its functionality from the customer premises
into the operator infrastructure.
Moving functions such as DNS cache servers, SIP gateways and content caching
mechanisms out of the RGW will reduce its footprint, with potential benefits
in terms of service manageability and functionality. Moreover, network func-
— 22 —

3.2. ACCESS INFRASTRUCTURE BACKGROUND AND EVOLUTION
tionality such as NAT, URL filtering, firewall or DHCP LAN servers could also
be migrated to the operator, being hosted or co-located in a carrier-grade infra-
structure. As an example, a DHCP server can be moved outside the RGW, even-
tually being replaced by a relay agent (implementing DHCP option 82 ( Patrick
[2001])), located in the access node (i.e., in the OLT) or in a network bridge
deployed at the customer premises.
Virtualizing the RGW can enable the deployment of a simple device that has the
potential to be as minimal as a simple bridge (dataplane functionality), eventu-
ally equipped with wireless capabilities, as presented by Bronstein and Shraga
[2014] and Mijumbi et al. [2016]. In this streamlined device, wireless support
could be added by using conventional radio System-on-Chip ( SoC) components
to implement a thin Access Point ( AP), with management/configuration capab-
ilities being ensured by a lightweight agent for remote operator access. Potential
alternatives could also be considered, such as Control and Provisioning of Wire-
less Access Points (CAPW AP) by Calhoun et al. [2009], using wireless controllers
virtualized in the operator cloud or the CloudMAC proposed by Vestin et al.
[2013], which allows to further decouple Wi-Fi AP capabilities, by offloading
Media Access Control ( MAC) frame processing to the cloud. Regardless of the
specific implementation details, it is expected that the removal of function and
service dependencies from the RGW may potentially increase its future-proofing,
regarding the introduction of new services.
The potential benefits of a vRGW are manifold. Some, like Xie et al. [2013],
estimated that it had the potential to reduce up to 90% in call centre costs
and up to 46% on the product return cost. Also, a financial study by Tsirakis
et al. [2017], comparing a physical, a partially virtualized (hybrid) and a fully
virtualized CPE, demonstrated that virtualization provides an economic benefit
for TSPs, which increases along with the number of virtualized functions. In
a vRGW scenario, having hardware requirements for new services is no longer
a problem since the gateways are software components without a physical de-
pendency to the underlying hardware supporting them. Moreover, Sanchez and
Brazewell [2015] point out that failure rates can be lowered, software simpler to
customize, and also easier to introduce new services. However, for the vRGW to
be feasible, certain requirements must be satisfied, such as the ones listed below
(from Xia et al. [2014] and ETSI [2014]):
• Scalability – vRGW streamlining and optimization are required, in order
to handle the hundreds of thousands or even millions of instances typically
required by a single TSP (ETSI [2014]; Dustzadeh [2013]).
• Management and orchestration – For operators, increased service co-
ordination challenges will result from functional dispersion, lifecycle man-
agement, resource orchestration, or integration of existing OSS/BSS 1.
Moreover, users are reluctant to relinquish control of the old RGW func-
1In a telco environment, the OSS are the components that operate the back-office of the
telco network, including provisioning and maintaining customer services. The BSS are
responsible for maintaining the front-office services, such as billing, customer relationship
and call center operations.
— 23 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
tionalities, requiring some sort of ”shared management” compromise to
ease the transition.
• Resiliency and continuity – The decoupling of functional and physical
components enabled by a vRGW poses significant challenges in terms of
service continuity and reliability, introducing additional potential points
of failure and requiring infrastructures to be designed from the ground up
to limit the impact of disruptive events.
• Security – The evolution to a virtualized paradigm provides an opportunity
to improve customer and infrastructure security. However, it can also
expose TSPs and/or their customers to threats which did not exist in the
legacy model, therefore requiring extra care to ensure that security is not
compromised.
• Service dynamics and elasticity – To cope with the dynamics of the applic-
ations and services, vRGW deployments require efficient orchestration and
resource elasticity (especially for computing and networking resources), in
order to scale as needed.
• Component portability – This is important for two reasons. First, it allows
moving software functions across different Data Centres ( DCs) in an easier
manner. Second, it eases the introduction or replacement of third-party
components over time, thus enabling true vendor-independence.
• Coexistence – Compatibility of virtualized and legacy infrastructure com-
ponents must be guaranteed, to ensure a smooth migration path.
• Energy efficiency – planing and design targets must attempt to balance
the consolidation benefits with the added energy expense of moving the
RGW functionalities to the TSP infrastructure.
• Improved Quality of Experience ( QoE) – Operators must ensure end-users
are able to experience QoE benefits from a vRGW migration, both by
improving existing services and through the introduction of new, value-
added services.
• Optimization of Vulnerability Assessment Scanner (V AS) delivery – vRGW
migration must ease the introduction of new V ASfor end-users (e.g., via a
self-care portal). Also, resource consolidation and sharing can contribute
to reduce V AS operation and maintenance overhead, which can have a
positive impact on OPEX.
If satisfied, these requirements can potentially turn the vRGW into a source of
benefits in terms of CAPEX, OPEX and flexibility, pushing the RGW into the
service-centric era.
The first attempts at virtualizing some of the RGW functions started with gen-
eric network router devices: Egi et al. [2007] and Egi et al. [2010] proposed
a solution using hypervisor-hosted VM (Barham et al. [2003]), together with
the click modular router and eXtensible Open Router Platform ( XORP); Pisa
et al. [2010] studied virtual router migration scenarios using hypervisor hosts.
— 24 —

3.2. ACCESS INFRASTRUCTURE BACKGROUND AND EVOLUTION
The virtualization performance overhead for network appliances is discussed by
Bazzi and Onozato [2011] and Zeng and Hao [2009]. Basak et al. [2010] proposed
implementing distributed firewalls using virtualized security appliances. There
were also some first proposals using Open Service Gateway initiative ( OSGi)
and VMs, such as Ibáñez et al. [2007].
In 2011, the Eurescom Project P2055 studied the issues associated with the
mass virtualization of RGWs for broadband access network environment from
an operator standpoint. It proposed three alternative approaches to displace the
physical RGW from the customer premises ( Abgrall [2011]):
• Moving RGW functionality to the access nodes, by placing packet pro-
cessing near the subscribers and distributing the load across several dis-
persed points. This approach requires extensive hardware upgrades in the
access nodes, fragmenting resources across the network, increasing com-
plexity and costs.
• Integrating RGW functionality on BNGs. This approach has the benefit of
keeping the network design unchanged, but at the expense of requiring the
BNG to support mass RGW virtualization, something that was tradition-
ally beyond their capabilities. This would require the need for hardware
upgrades and fragmentation of computing resources among BNG.
• Supported in an independent Network Element ( NE) within the operator’s
network. While having the advantage of not interfering with existing net-
work elements, this introduces a new component on the infrastructure,
further increasing costs and maintenance requirements.
Physical Residential Gateway ( pRGW) replacement was also proposed by
Da Silva et al. [2011], embedding transport capabilities on the access node OLT
and decoupling other functionality such as Authentication Authorization Ac-
counting ( AAA), DHCP and NAT. A different approach, by Dustzadeh [2013],
proposed introducing vRGW line cards in the OLT (see Figure 3.1), also integ-
rating the aggregation switch and edge router functionality (such as MA5600T
OLT from Huawei [2010]). This was tested on a small scale pilot by the Spanish
operator Telefónica.

EQUIPMENT
age of Broadband Forum ( BBF) TR-101 ( Anschutz [2011]) and TR-156 ( Bath-
rick [2017b]) reference frameworks for VLAN aggregation topologies in Digital
Subscriber Line ( DSL) and Gigabit Passive Optical Network ( GPON) access
networks, respectively. This proposal (see Figure 3.2) uses a mix of Customer

nels. Client- VLAN trunks are aggregated at the access nodes and aggregated
into VLAN sub-trunks – customer-specific VLANs are then encapsulated within
MPLS pseudowires at the BNG. The MPLS core network provides circuit ab-
stractions, allowing the customer VLANs to reach the DC, where the vRGW
instances are hosted.

This proposal also included a plan for progressive migration of the vRGW from
a stand-alone virtual machine replicating the RGW system image (and hosted
on the operator data centre) to a distributed approach, by decoupling and co-
locating components, to streamline the vRGW image footprint (see Figure 3.3).
To a certain extent, this approach already suggested some sort of functional de-
coupling, but without detailing how integration could be implemented, somehow
anticipating a solution that is conceptually similar to NFV functional decoup-
ling.
Limitations such as the absence of service decoupling mechanisms, flexible in-
frastructure support for pushing the customer premises boundaries towards the
operator DC, the increased complexity added to the access nodes or even ac-
cess network restrictions (such as the limit for VLAN or Q-in-Q circuits) have
ultimately shown these early vRGW proposals to be still unfeasible for deploy-
