ment in large scale scenarios. This was aggravated by the fact that most of the
proposals practically ignored the vRGW management overhead, opting instead
to focus on proof-of-concept scenarios. Meanwhile, developments such as SDN
and NFV brought the potential to fill some of the gaps of those early proposals
and reshape the vRGW concept around more flexible and scalable frameworks,
as presented in the next two Sections.
3.3 Virtual Residential Gateway-Enabling Developments
The emergence of Network Function Virtualization ( NFV) and Software-Defined
Networking (SDN) technologies (cf. Chapter 2) have prompted the industry to
develop standards and solutions to incorporate their benefits within operator
infrastructures, with a potentially disruptive effect. The vRGW concept is not
immune to these developments, which have rapidly become the main drivers for
its evolution. Moreover, the fog computing concept can help optimize vRGW
function placement at the edge of the operator infrastructure, where they are
most needed, which is important in latency sensitive services.
3.3.1 Performance Optimizations
Performance stands among the various requirements for the vRGW: it must be
at least at the same level of traditional counterparts ( Mijumbi et al. [2016]).
The vRGW performance is deeply tied to underlying technologies such as NFV.
ETSI has published several recommendations and best practices for the deploy-
ment of VNFs over a compute host ( ETSI [2014]). There has been some research
in hardware and software virtualization that directly or indirectly has been be-
neficial to NFV performance. A while back, there were constraints in network
performance in VNF environments that could limit their usability in vRGW
scenarios ( Han et al. [2015]; Wang and Ng [2010]). However, developments
in hardware virtualization and software optimization have made it possible to
increase the networking performance of the virtualized functions. It is worth
— 27 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
noting that, in addition to the increased performance of VNF, acceleration tech-
niques also offer a valuable means of optimizing hardware utilization and to
lower both deployment and running costs.
Next, we look into optimizations that can benefit the performance of NFV (and
vRGW implementations), divided into three categories: network; virtualization
node; and integration of hardware acceleration mechanisms.
Networking
One way of increasing networking performance is by optimizing the libraries used
by applications to interact with the Network Interface Card ( NIC). Released
by Intel, the Data Plane Development Kit ( DPDK) is a set of libraries for the
development of applications that require intensive network packet processing.
DPDK processes network packets in polling mode instead of the default inter-
rupt mode. This reduces the number of Central Processing Unit ( CPU) cycles
used per processed packet by continuously checking the NIC for state changes,
which mitigates the CPU interruption in packet processing. DPDK is able to
provide substantial improvements of packet processing performance compared
with traditional libraries on COTS hardware, by allowing applications to access
the NIC without the overhead of the OS (Belkhiri et al. [2023]; Cerrato et al.
[2014]). On the other hand, when using this library, there is no interrupt when
packets are available in the NIC. Traditional libraries such as Linux LibPCAP
limit performance, while DPDK allows a more efficient use of the hardware. As
an example, Kourtis et al. [2015] showed packet processing of a virtual Deep
Packet Inspection ( DPI) application on a 10Gbps NIC was capped at around
1Gbps using LibPCAP. On the other hand, it provided near line rate when the
same application was using DPDK.
Hardware virtualization techniques can also play an important role to improve
the performance of NFV by reducing processing overheads. Single Root I/O
Virtualization ( SR-IOV), specified by the Peripheral Component Interconnect
Special Interest Group ( PCI-SIG [2024]), can divide a piece of hardware into
multiple PCI Express Requester IDs (virtual instances of PCI functions) and
allocate each one directly to a VM. This reduces the interactions between the
hypervisor and the VM, as the VM has direct access to the hardware and enables
near native performance. As an example, while performing packet processing
operations on a 10Gb link, Kourtis et al. [2015] managed to reduce performance
degradation in a virtualized deployment to 19%, comparing with a physical
deployment, by using SR-IOV. Despite the performance benefits that SR-IOV
can bring to NFV, care must be taken as there are deployment scenarios where
it might not be a good option. For example, Anderson et al. [2016] describe
a Docker-enabled container scenario where packet forwarding between VNFs
in an intra-host could have up to 100% more Round Trip Time ( RTT) when
using SR-IOV, than with macvlan, bridged, or Open vSwitch ( OVS)-enabled
interfaces. The reason for this increase is that the switching is offloaded to
a Peripheral Component Interconnect Express ( PCIe) device and the effects
are greater with a larger number of chained functions and an increased packet
size.
— 28 —

3.3. VIRTUAL RESIDENTIAL GATEW AY-ENABLING DEVELOPMENTS
Another networking optimization in packet switching is the SnabbSwitch. This is
a virtual switch for the Kernel-based Virtual Machine (KVM) hypervisor running
in user space. SnabbSwitch is aimed at high performance packet processing in
NFV environments. Tests showed that SnabbSwitch was capable of processing
packets at near line rate in some scenarios, according to Paolino et al. [2015].
This project is currently is referred as Snabb 2.
Some proposals target specific areas and leverage hardware acceleration to im-
prove the performance of NFV. For example, Zazo et al. [2015] proposed repla-
cing NICs with Field Programmable Gate Arrays ( FPGA) boards with on-board
networking interfaces (such as NetFPGA [2016], Kfoury et al. [2024]). The au-
thors offloaded some processing logic directly to the hardware responsible for
handling network packets. When a packet arrives at an interface, it may be
processed by the FPGA and sent directly to another network interface inside
the FPGA board, or sent to the software appliance to be further processed. This
way the network function can be software-based, hardware-based, or a mixture
of both.
Virtualization node
Research efforts have also addressed the use of container technologies for hosting
VNF instances, eliminating the need for an individual guest OS for each virtual-
ized function ( Wiedner et al. [2024]; Zahoor et al. [2023]; Anderson et al. [2016];
Bonafiglia et al. [2015]; Evens [2015]; Raho et al. [2015]). Containers provide
a way to reduce the overhead associated with hosting several OS instances,
providing lightweight OS-level virtualization instead of a full, hypervisor-based
approach as Cziva et al. [2015] described, allowing the deployment of a higher
number of instances for the same hardware. Modig and Ding [2016] studied re-
source usage in a container-based RGW virtualization approach using OpenVZ.
Tests showed that using containers can increase the performance and reduce
resource usage, particularly in relation to memory and storage.
Container-enabled virtualization has been compared with VM virtualization in
the literature. Anderson et al. [2016] compared the networking performance of
Xen- and Docker-enabled functions, with a special focus on latency and jitter.
They have also measured the CPU cycles impact of these virtualization tech-
niques when using a range of networking technologies (direct, macvlan, SR-IOV,
bridge and OVS). Their research showed that the use of OS-level virtualization
(i.e., using containers) can increase performance, when compared with hyper-
visor virtualization using the same hardware. This advantage in terms of latency
and jitter has also been pointed out by Evens [2015]. Bonafiglia et al. [2015] also
found Docker to have a lower latency, when compared with KVM deployments
in chained VNF scenarios.
Another point that must be considered is the maturity of large-scale container
orchestration. Compared with large-scale VM management, container manage-
ment at scale is fairly recent with tools such as Kubernetes [2016] and Fedora
[2016]. Also, security and isolation must be guaranteed, as Rotter et al. [2016]
2https://github.com/snabbco/snabb
— 29 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
noted.
Improvements in some NFV scenarios might also be obtained by exploring al-
ternative server architectures. Cerrato et al. [2014] note that the traditional
server architecture may not be the best to fully exploit some NFV scenarios.
Traditionally, server architecture follows a model of having a small number of
powerful CPU cores. However, looking specifically into the vRGW scenario,
many of the involved VNFs are not CPU-bounded, focusing instead on network
Input/Output (I/O). As a result, these functions may benefit from an approach
with a higher number of low power cores instead of a small number of powerful
cores. Mellanox Technologies [2019] TILE-Mx100, which is an Advanced Re-
duced Instruction Set Computer Machine ( ARM)-based SoC with 100 cores, is
an example of such architecture. Architectures using a large number of small
cores together with mechanisms such as DPDK may increase the efficiency of
network I/O intensive VNF according to Cerrato et al. [2014].
Integration and Standardization of Hardware Acceleration Mechan-
isms
Table 3.1 summarizes the main performance-enhancing techniques for NFV en-
vironments that might be relevant for vRGW scenarios.

ETSI has also done some work related with the use of hardware acceleration
— 30 —

3.3. VIRTUAL RESIDENTIAL GATEW AY-ENABLING DEVELOPMENTS
techniques to improve the NFV performance, which resulted in a set of docu-
ments from the ETSI GS NFV-IF A group ( ETSI [2015]; ETSI [2016c]; ETSI
[2016d]; ETSI [2016e]). Some preliminary work on the subject has been made
with a proposal of a common architecture and an abstraction layer. This type
of acceleration aims to help VNFs with special performance needs, for instance
to meet certain latency or Service-Level Agreement ( SLA) requirements.
This work is largely related to the NFVI section of ETSI architectural framework
for NFV, since it covers all the hardware and software components that compose
the environment supporting the deployed VNFs. Moreover, it covers the use
of acceleration in all domains covered in the NFVI: compute, network, and
storage. Also, this includes hardware acceleration, software acceleration, and
any combination of the two.
ETSI proposed an architectural approach that uses an Acceleration Abstrac-
tion Layer ( AAL) to enhance the interoperability between the Virtual Network
Function Component ( VNFC) and the accelerators (see Figure 3.4). VNF ac-
celeration implementations may range from tightly-coupled software and hard-
ware (passthrough model) to loosely-coupled software that takes advantage of
an acceleration abstraction model. This model shows some advantages over the
passthrough model. With passthrough, the drivers for the hardware are con-
tained in the VNF. This way, a new hardware release requires vendors to update
the VNFs. In the abstraction model the hardware drivers are a responsibility
of the NFVI that provides functionality to the VNF, and it includes a generic
driver that does not make any assumption about the underlying NFVI.
VNFC Implementation

[2015]).
The used cases presented in ETSI [2015] resulted in an accelerator taxonomy. It
classifies them based on a number of criteria, such as the software that uses the
accelerator, it’s type, location, and functionality. The taxonomy is based on use
cases organized in three categories: compute, network, and storage acceleration.
Some of the example use cases for computing acceleration are key for vRGW
scenarios, such as IPSec tunnel termination, Next Generation Firewall ( NGFW)
Acceleration, Virtual Acceleration Interface for VNFs, Transcoding and DPI.
Network acceleration can be used in scenarios of Load Balancing and NAT, NFVI
Virtual Networking Offload, NFVI Secure Overlay Offload, and in Dynamic
Optimization of Packet Flow Routing.
This work has been carried further in ETSI [2016c], to give more detail to the
proposed concepts and architectures. Among the developments, there is the
— 31 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
identification of common design patterns to enable a VNFC instance to set-up
and use accelerators in run-time. Additionally, the document also describes how
VNF vendors can take advantage of the accelerators without being dependent on
the implementation. Finally, it defines methods and requirements that enable
VNF independence from specific accelerator implementations.
ETSI has also approached some aspects related to Virtual Switch ( vSwitch)
performance in ETSI [2016d]. This is a critical point of the NFV infrastructure
due to being a convergence point for VNF traffic, whether it is from VNFs of
the same compute node or different compute nodes. This work also describes
some characteristics of vSwitch use in these environments, listing some of the
functions they may perform (such as load balancing). It also describes some use
cases and deployment scenarios, as well as the specific characteristics needed for
the measurement and benchmarking of the virtual switching environment.
Other proposals to enhance the performance of NFV can be found in the literat-
ure, such as Fast Path Offloading ( FPO) proposed by Touitou and Roch [2014],
which is also beneficial for vRGW scenarios. FPO may be used to improve the
performance of an L7 load balancer, by offloading the flow redirection to the fast
path, and performing only the flow configuration at the VNF level. This method
is similar to the OVS slow path/fast path architecture, where a forwarding rule
is executed at kernel level (fast path) after being processed the first time at user
level (slow path).
3.3.2 Functional Placement
Initial vRGW proposals did not encompass a clear description of functional
or nodal distribution within the infrastructure, which can be done in several
ways.
A first possible approach would be, for example, to have each function from a
client being handled by one dedicated VNF. This approach provides a simple
and straightforward implementation, but is prone to scalability problems like
those already pointed out in previous solutions where each vRGW is individually
virtualized as a single instance ( Dustzadeh [2013]).
One way to minimize this scalability problem is to improve the efficiency of the
software providing the functions, as described by Kozicki et al. [2014]. This can
be done using software containers to run several different functions in one VM
instance, instead of having separate VMs for each function – thus reducing the
computational overhead of having multiple OS instances. The computational
overhead can be further reduced if the functions become capable of handling
multiple tenants. As a multi-tenancy example, in a scenario with a function
providing DHCP service the function would provide connectivity for devices
from multiple home networks, instead of having a dedicated DHCP function for
each home network. However, this poses new challenges that must be addressed
to assure the security of the devices in the home networks: when functions
become shared between different customers their isolation must be guaranteed.
This is necessary when using containers to execute different functions within the
— 32 —

3.3. VIRTUAL RESIDENTIAL GATEW AY-ENABLING DEVELOPMENTS
same VM but, more importantly, when using the same function to serve multiple
clients in a multi-tenant scenario.
Bonafiglia et al. [2016] presented a hybrid solution supporting network function
placement both on the operator infrastructure and on CPEs, making it possible
to host complex functions in the operator data centre while keeping lightweight
or latency-sensitive functions running natively on CPEs. However, the use of
local functions may affect the future-proof capabilities of the RGW device, in
comparison with scenarios where all functions run in the operator infrastructure,
depending on which functions are intended to be hosted locally.
The fog computing paradigm aims at bringing cloud application services near
the edge of the operator network. This allows for applications that traditionally
run in the core of the network to be dynamically placed closer to the end devices
which consume such services. As a result of having the computation physically
closer to the end devices, a significant improvement on end-to-end latency can
be obtained, which may be critical to different types of services.
There are several application fields that can use or take advantage of the fog
paradigm, including: Augmented Reality ( AR), smart grids, traffic lights, wire-
less sensor networks, smart building control, IoT, and Cyber-Physical Sys-
tem ( CPS) ( Yi et al. [2015]; Stojmenovic and Wen [2014]; Garcia Hernando
et al. [2017]). Yi et al. [2015] note that in one way or another, these are latency-
sensitive applications whose experience can be ruined or severely worsen if the
end-to-end delay is greater than tenths of milliseconds.
Fog computing can be useful in vRGW scenarios to define placement, as a means
to improve the user experience in some services – for instance, NFs can be
instantiated at the edge of the network, closer to the RGW devices ( Lombardo
et al. [2015]). For example, if data is being transmitted between devices in the
customer premises LAN, and some action needs to be done to that traffic (e.g.,
passing through a firewall) it will need to be redirected to the network functions
located in the operator side. Different latency results may be obtained if this is
done in a central DC, which can be quite far from the subscriber, or at the edge
of the TSP network, using fog computing technologies. The latter can result in
a significant decrease in end-to-end latency.
3.3.3 The role of Management and Orchestration in the NFV-Enabled
vRGW
Apart from functional placement, NFV Management and Orchestration is also
a key part of the NFV-enabled vRGW. While the BBF proposed the concept of
NERG orchestrator, the ETSI architecture goes beyond with the proposal of a
MANO subsystem (Orchestrator, VNF Manager and VIM) with defined APIs,
data models and interfaces. However, the original architecture proposal was
missing some points such as the management of the VNF life cycle, the service
package format and the service template metadata formats – VNF descriptors
and Network Service Descriptor ( NSD).
— 33 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
Those points are essential to have a standardized or de facto management and
orchestration that can be used by different vendors, manufacturers and operat-
ors. Mijumbi et al. [2016] noted the importance of defining guidelines to address
these loose ends in the shortest amount of time possible. The main reason be-
ing the investment that operators were making in using NFV, and taking too
long could result in each one developing vendor-specific solutions that are fin-
ancially difficult to revert, impeding standardization efforts. Nonetheless, ETSI
addresses this in the standardization work in the scope of its NFV-Interfaces
and Architecture ( IF A) workgroup ( ETSI [2016f] ETSI [2016g] ETSI [2016h]),
which addresses interfaces between architectural components such as the or-
chestrator and VIM, and the VIM and the VNFM. Also, ETSI started a collab-
oration with the Organization for the Advancement of Structured Information
Standards ( OASIS) to define adequate metadata formats for service and VNF
descriptors, based on the Topology and Orchestration Specification for Cloud
Applications (TOSCA) language, specified by Lipton and Crandall [2017]).
One of the important aspects of the MANO role is that a large number of
instances of virtualized functions must be deployed and configured. For that to
be feasible, the management and orchestration must be highly automated and
coordinated, in order to provide the necessary provisioning and instantiation.
On top of that, those functions will need to be connected using the underlying
network architecture in order to create the necessary service chaining to provide
the service subscribed by the customer. The high-level orchestration of that
layer is a responsibility of the MANO, which coordinates the underlying network
components (e.g., OpenFlow controller in an SDN enabled scenario) through its
southbound interfaces.
Despite the issues previously mentioned, operators adopted NFV in their in-
frastructures. OpenMANO, developed by Telefónica [2015], was one of the ex-
amples of a framework to manage and orchestrate NFV, and was based on
ETSI’s recommendations and guidelines for NFV. In addition to the MANO
capabilities, OpenMANO included other features as well with its own VIM.
The application of these management frameworks to the vRGW use case is ad-
dressed, for instance, by Proença et al. [2017]. OpenMANO was integrated and
used as a base for the ETSI OSM project (described in Chapter 2), and was
deprecated as a stand-alone project.
The OpenMANO framework is an example that aimed to be a complete im-
plementation of the ETSI framework, but other efforts might implement only
part of it. The issues regarding the missing pieces in ETSI’s guidelines that
were mentioned previously grow in importance in these cases, as the different
frameworks need to understand each others’ interfaces. Another example of a
framework that benefits from this interoperability is Open Platform for Net-
work Function Virtualization ( OPNFV), an open source project announced by
the Linux Foundation [2014]. The project was launched with the objective of
providing an integrated platform to accelerate the introduction of new NFV
products and services. In 2021, this project was merged with Cloud iNfrastruc-
— 34 —

3.4. INITIATIVES AND PROPOSALS FOR GATEW AY VIRTUALIZATION
ture Telco Taskforce ( CNTT), originating the Anuket project 3.
3.4 Initiatives and Proposals for Gateway Virtualization
This Section analyses how the emergence of SDN and NFV enabled a new gener-
ation of vRGW proposals, more mature and focused on issues such as functional
decoupling, scalability and dynamic accommodation of service and application
requirements. It includes an overview of the proposals and initiatives related
to RGW virtualization. Its contents will be organized along three different
groups, namely: standards-related initiatives, research work, and industry pro-
posals.
3.4.1 Initiatives from Standardization Bodies
ETSI [2014] has described several use cases for NFV, among which the Virtu-
alization of Home Environment scenario is of particular interest, as it provided
initial descriptions on how the RGW (and even an STB) could be virtualized
and moved to a service platform in the network. In this scenario, depicted in
Figure 3.5, the vRGW is still responsible for providing private addressing and
mediating service delivery to the home LAN.

Apart from ETSI, the Broadband Forum ( BBF) end-to-end architectures group
have also published efforts on virtualizing business and home gateways, doc-
umented in TR-317, which includes the NERG architecture, and TR-328 spe-
cifying the Virtual Business Gateway ( vBG) ( Fabregas [2017]; Alter [2014]; and
Dalle [2016]). The NERG architecture (Figure 3.6) splits the vRGW function-
ality across a virtual Gateway ( vG) – in charge of service and network functions
such as IP forwarding, NAT and IP addressing – and the Bridged Residential
3https://anuket.io/

Gateway (BRG), left at the customer premises and responsible for the forward-
ing plane, which may be an Optical Network Terminal ( ONT) with an integrated
Ethernet switch, possibly with SDN support. The NERG is designed to accom-
modate SDN support in order to enable BRG control from the vG or another
controller – such an arrangement requires in-band transport of SDN protocol
interactions, demanding special attention to potential security and availability
issues.

TR-317 discusses several nodal distribution scenarios to decouple vRGW func-
tionality across the operator infrastructure (Figure 3.7), using SDN and NFV
to ease deployment and maintenance while improving its capabilities.
While some TR-317 scenarios are reminiscent of the Eurescom P2055 4 ideas
– contemplating vRGW deployment within the access node, the BNG, or a
DC/cloud – there are also new distribution concepts, such as the hybrid boxing
model presented by Alter [2014]. Hybrid boxing splits the vRGW into a Service-
vRG (dealing with services and SDN control) and a Network-vRG (in charge of
the forwarding plane), taking advantage of SDN to implement policy-based sub-
scriber traffic forwarding. These proposals filled the void left by the ETSI vRGW
use case, as they detail SDN-compatible functional decoupling strategies which
are compatible with the introduction of NFV elements. Moreover, the NERG
also introduced a platform for leveraging the potential of x86 virtualization ad-
vances, pushing for a component-based model akin to an application store, to
add new functionality to the vRGW. Such a solution had already been proposed
for conventional RGWs, in the form of TR-157 ( Carey and Kirksey [2011]) com-
ponent management for execution environments such as OSGi [2012].
In the wake of the previous developments, TR-328 proposed an even more flexible
approach for the vRGW, encompassing three deployment models, as shown in
Figure 3.8: within the network (hosted in a Multi-Service Broadband Network
Gateway or an elastic virtualization environment); at the customer premises
(with the customer providing the NFV infrastructure, for example in business

environments); or as a set of decoupled functions spread between the customer

Besides the mapping of SDN-based use cases to its own reference network archi-
tectures described in TR-101 ( Anschutz [2011]) and TR-145 ( Cui and Hertoghs
[2012]) (and further documented in WT-302: Architecture and Connectivity of
Cloud Services for Broadband Networks; and SD-313: Business Requirements
and Framework for SDN in Telecommunication Broadband Networks), the BBF
worked on the concept of flexible service chaining, as described in SD-326 ( Al-
ter, C., and Daowood, S. [2014]). This is a generic concept, since it does not
necessarily involve the use of VNFs, going in line with the proposals laid out
by Niu et al. [2014]; Nadeau and Quinn [2015]; Boucadair et al. [2015]; Dunbar
et al. [2015]; and Jiang and Hongyu [2014].
Another proposal for functional distribution of vRGW components by Lee and
Ghai [2015] and Lee and Xie [2015] is very similar to the BBF hybrid box model
(Figure 3.9), also splitting the vRGW into a Virtual Customer Premises Equip-
ment Packet Forwarder ( VPF) and a Virtual CPE Controller ( VC), keeping a
simple bridge device at the customer premises (although, unlike the BBF pro-
posal, this one is devoid of SDN-like forwarding control).
In this architecture, the VPF is an SDN-enabled packet processing device, with

basis.
3.4.2 Research Initiatives
Research efforts also adopted the use of SDN and NFV to tackle RGW vir-
tualization. An example is the virtualized MAnagement and Networking DO-
main ( vMANDO), an NFV and SDN-based vRGW presented by Moyano et al.
[2017a]. The main focus was on the design of a gateway management approach
directed at end-users with low or no technological background, enabling them
to easily manage the home environment. Providing an interface tailored to less
technical users has the potential to minimize operator costs by reducing tech
calls and on-site maintenance, while maintaining user satisfaction levels.
A vRGW concept focused on Machine-to-Machine ( M2M) communications was
proposed by Dillon and Winters [2014], including the description of a PoC gate-
way based on open-source components. Modig and Ding [2016] addressed the
use of containers to reduce the resource usage in RGW virtualization. The
authors used full and individual RGW virtualization, with resource savings de-
riving mostly from having multiple instances sharing the underlying OS.
Huang et al. [2017] proposed a vRGW implementation based on SDN and NFV,

using multiple flow table strategies. For customer connectivity, a simple SDN-
enabled switch is placed in the premises, while the functions are hosted at the
operator edge. As a strategy for migration between physical RGW and virtual-
ized instances, Herbaut et al. [2015] proposed the integration of NFV into the
RGW using modular gateways. Being limited devices, the modules installed in
the gateway are used to redirect the traffic to virtualized functions .
3.4.3 Industry Initiatives
Telecommunications operators also showed interest in the virtualization of the
RGW, to take advantage of the potential benefits ( NEC [2014]; Ericsson [2014];
NEC [2016]; Proença et al. [2017]). For example, Spanish telecom Telefónica
has done a small trial in Brazil during 2014 (where it operates as ViVo 5), with
deployments consisting of a simplified gateway to provide basic connectivity,
acting as an access point, switch and modem. The adopted approach followed
an NFV-based model, with functions moved from the customer home to the op-
erator infrastructure, as described by Palancar et al. [2015]. One thing that has
been common in most industry initiatives is the support of the functions in an
SDN-connected NFV infrastructure. However, publicly available data from in-
dustry trials is usually restricted, given the sensitive business implications.
In a broader scope, the Central Office Re-architected as Datacenter ( CORD)
project ( ONF [2017]; Moyano et al. [2017c]) aimed at providing a reference
implementation of a cloud-based service delivery platform for TSP, combining
the use of cloud computing, SDN and NFV. Having started as an Open Network
Operating System ( ONOS) use case published by Berde et al. [2014], CORD
became an open source project of its own, being supported by the ONF, as
well as the Linux Foundation. The main objective was to provide an unified
platform to improve the provision of telecommunication services, using open
source software and commodity hardware components such as servers, switches
5http://www.vivo.com.br/
— 39 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
and I/O.
A set of services has been derived from the CORD initiative. Together they form
the Residential Central Office Re-architected as Datacenter ( R-CORD) service
profile ( ONF [2017]), which involved several industry players namely, AT&T,
Comcast, NTT, DT, and Telefónica. The main services provided by R-CORD
follows:
• Virtual Optical Line Terminal ( vOLT) – Virtualization of the OLT to
replace its proprietary components with a mixture of COTS hardware and
open source software ( CORD [2017a]).
• virtual Router ( vRouter) – Placed at the edge of the Internet Service Pro-
vider ( ISP) network, the BNG connects the access networks to the core
network of the TSP in traditional environments. In the CORD architec-
ture the vRouter is used to provide the routing functionality between the
CORD and the access network ( CORD [2017b]),
• Virtual Subscriber Gateway ( vSG) – One of the main use cases of the
project is a vRGW implementation designated as vSG by Peterson et al.
[2016]. The CORD architecture accepts different implementation alternat-
ives, such as having the functions to host the customer’s bundle of network
functions on a VM, on a lightweight container or on a chain of lightweight
containers. Nonetheless, the main idea is that the functions will be loc-
ated in the Central Office ( CO) and the customer is left with a simple
device in its premises (i.e., a bare-metal switch), simple enough to provide
connectivity between the CO and the customer home ( CORD [2016]).
As of now, CORD is no longer an active project. However, it laid a foundation
for ONF broadband projects, including vOLTHA, SEBA6, and AETHER 7.
Table 3.2 summarizes the proposals discussed in this subsection, classified ac-
cordingly with their generation (before and after the emergence of NFV and SDN
technologies), scope (standards-related initiatives, research works, and industry
proposals) and focus (gateway, services, and architecture).
3.5 Reference Taxonomy
This section finalizes the discussion of vRGW developments with the proposal of
a reference taxonomy for classifying past and ongoing initiatives. It is organized
around four key axis: Integration with the Operator’s Infrastructure ; Function
Distribution; Scope; and Standards Compliance . Figure 3.10 provides a visual
illustration, along with the categorization of previous proposals already discussed
in this chapter.
Integration with the Operator Infrastructure addresses the relationship
of the vRGW with the operator ecosystem, considering two aspects: integra-
tion with current OSS and BSS systems; and the support for coexistence with

match the Operator Cloud category. Finally, proposals that use a mixture
of network equipment and operator cloud placements are categorized as
Hybrid. NFV-based approaches fit into the two later categories, depending
on whether they are exclusively based on data centre deployment or on
mixed solution.
• Function Coupling – Considers three subcategories: Vertical, Co-Located,
and Distributed. A Vertical approach is used when virtualizing each in-
dividual vRGW as is. An example are most of initial proposals, with a
straightforward implementation to test the concept. However, these are
usually associated with severe scalability issues for real-world deployments.
Co-located coupling matches scenarios with some shared functionality, such
as DHCP or NAT, but on a local domain. Distributed approaches apply
when the functionalities are massively distributed across geographically
dispersed locations.
The proposals can also be classified by their scope, considering three main
— 42 —

3.5. REFERENCE TAXONOMY
categories: Research Work, Industry Proposals, and initiatives directly originated
from Standards Bodies.
Finally, the taxonomy considers Standards Compliance for the main stand-
ards organizations currently pushing the vRGW developments.

Previous Sections provided an overview of the existing proposals for virtualizing
the RGW, and Table 3.3 summarizes the main features of each one. Looking at
— 43 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
those proposals, it is clear that traditional RGW architectures are quite different
from the new proposals, designed for emerging/future architectures and services.
Next, we will discuss some of the key aspects and open challenges related with
the vRGW concept.
Moving away from vertical segmentation
Most of the earliest vRGW proposals relied on some sort of vertical segmentation
of RGW instances (cf. Figure 3.3). These were used as baseline for performing
initial tests and implementing PoCs. However, they had considerable limitations
in terms of applicability. They were a good start to validate the virtualization
idea, but were not applicable to real world scenarios where (several) thousands
of instances would need to be deployed. We can point out two distinct vertically
segmented type of proposals: those based on dedicated vRGW line cards, and
those using virtualization, the latter being a rather simplistic approach which
took the traditional RGW and virtualized it as a full RGW implementation.
Both have limitations in terms of scalability to the millions of instances that
one single provider might need to deploy ( ETSI [2014]; Dustzadeh [2013]).
Scaling and performance
As previously mentioned, initial proposals were focused mostly on functional
aspects, with little attention paid to scaling issues. Moreover, at the time they
were devised there were still few hardware acceleration solutions for the data
path. Later approaches have prioritized scalability and performance, aiming to
integrate more efficient vRGW segmentation solutions with advances on virtu-
alization and hardware acceleration. As a result of these efforts, there has been
a substantial decrease in the overhead imposed by the underlying support infra-
structure. Additionally, hardware acceleration mechanisms have been leveraged
to streamline and optimize the operation of VNF instances. Some of the these
optimizations are related with aspects such as networking, containerization of
software instances and virtual switches, as described previously in Table 3.1.
Nonetheless, this is still work-in-progress with considerable space for improve-
ment.
Still in this scope, it should also be mentioned that there is a remarkable lack of
quantitative data publicly available. In fact, very few projects released quantit-
ative data, even at partial level – presumably due to its strategic value for in-
volved operators and manufacturers. Without this quantitative data it becomes
considerably more difficult to assess the overall performance and scalability of
vRGW platforms, as well as their economical impact.
Novel vRGW-enabled services
The virtualization of the RGW offers not only flexible deployment and cost re-
duction but also enables the introduction of new services that may not have
been feasible with traditional gateways. In the past, TSPs have increased their
service portfolio as the infrastructures grew. When broadband access allowed
for operators to provide high speed Internet to their costumers, new services,
such as IPTV and VoIP, emerged to take advantage of it. More recently, these
services have expanded to a broader range, including smart-home applications
— 44 —

3.5. REFERENCE TAXONOMY
and utilities such as the ones mentioned by Park et al. [2017], in M2M com-
munication scenarios by Dillon and Winters [2014] and in healthcare. Now, a
new wave of innovative services has the potential to be driven by the flexibility
of instantiating network functions and easily interconnecting them, without the
need for costly and time-consuming hardware and firmware updates.
In addition to the RGW, more extensive virtualization of other CPE devices can
be expected, such as the STB. These devices can leverage the benefits of the
vRGW and also consolidate storage capacity in the data centre. For example,
when multiple customers record the same TV programme, only one physical
copy needs to be stored in the operator infrastructure. This contrasts with the
traditional model of maintaining individual copies in each STB, as in the case
with traditional STB equipment. Online console gaming is also expected to
benefit from virtualization. The most crucial factor that significantly affects
gaming experience is latency, which is already considerable reduced with cur-
rent Fiber-To-The-Home ( FTTH) connections compared with older broadband
network architectures. Also, operators can offer stream-based GaaS, by having
the game running on virtualized instances on their infrastructure, and taking
advantage of the FTTH high bandwidth and low latency to deliver the service
to the customer premises.
Finally, the virtualization of the RGW enables the seamless deployment of more
common services, such as VPN, for both business and home markets. In a
business environment with multiple branches, each equipped with a virtualized
gateway, establishing a VPN connection becomes a straightforward process by
linking the virtual gateways in the data centre(s). Moreover, services currently
offered, such as managed security services, Content Delivery Networks ( CDNs),
and Virtual Desktop Infrastructure ( VDI), can be greatly improved. Nonethe-
less, these use cases only scratch the surface of the potential applications. The
introduction of NFV technologies opens up for customization and niche markets
that were not economically attractive before.
The impact of NFV on Service Design and Customization
As already mentioned, the emergence of the NFV concept was one of the most
significant developments for the vRGW concept. By providing a flexible way
to deploy network functions as individual VNFs, a service can be split into
several distinct functions that, altogether, represent a customer gateway service.
Providers have a pool of functions that can be used to build an end-to-end
service. Graph-like SFCs provide a guideline path for traffic steering, enabling
the customer network flows to reach each of the individual network functions
that compose the service, such as URL filters and firewalls. Traditionally, service
design is a task that can take a substantial amount of time from its initial design
phase to be ready for customer use. However, using the flexibility of NFV,
the creation of tailor-made services to smaller customer groups can be done
on-demand, by structuring the required functions and building the respective
service chains.
The NFV concept is also partially responsible for the horizontal segmentation
of current vRGW proposals. By providing a flexible way to instantiate and
— 45 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
deploy network functions, it is substantially easier to develop flexible multi-
tenant functions, such as those used by Proença et al. [2017] – e.g., instead
of having one URL filter instance for each customer, a broader service serves
multiple customers per instance.
From a research point-of-view, this deep connection between the NFV and
vRGW concepts opens both new prospects and new challenges. For instance,
the definition of metadata formats and interfaces for VNF and service tem-
plates must be flexible enough to easily accommodate the introduction of new
elements or the nesting of already defined service abstractions within existing
vRGW instances, in a seamless way.
OSS/BSS integration
While earlier vRGW PoC focused on other functional aspects, large scale de-
ployment of vRGW is not possible without the solid management capabilities
currently provided by the operator OSS and BSS systems (for operations and
business management, respectively). As these are often mature systems that
operators have long used and extensively refined for their operations, it is im-
portant that new vRGW architectures are capable of integrating with them,
either directly or using some sort of adapters.
When using an NFV-based approach, the VNF lifecycle operations should be
able to be integrated into current OSS/BSS systems, ideally using automated
management techniques to reduce error rate, and avoid vendor lock-in, to max-
imize the operators acceptance.
Undergoing efforts in this specific direction include for instance the
OASIS/TOSCA metadata formats, as detailed by Lipton and Crandall [2017],
whose objective is to support the automated deployment of applications and
their management. It uses an Extensible Markup Language ( XML)-based format
to model an application architecture, its components, and the relationships
among them in a topology graph.
Migration paths/coexistence of legacy and vRGW
In addition to the operator OSS and BSS systems, the acceptance of new vRGW
architectures requires the co-existence between current (legacy) and new archi-
tectures. This arises from the fact that the large infrastructure already deployed
and the high number of customers served by it, which sometimes reaches millions
of customers for a single service provider, make it difficult (if not impossible) for
the vRGW to be deployed without taking care of its coexistence with existing
architectures.
The coexistence of RGW/vRGW encompasses various aspects, with the integra-
tion of OSS/BSS being particularly important. From this perspective, incorpor-
ating the vRGW into existing OSS/BSS systems could facilitate this integration,
and ongoing efforts are underway to develop function data models and inter-
faces. This would allow for a gradual and smooth deployment of new vRGWs
and enabling physical devices, in parallel with the decommissioning of the cur-
rent classic physical RGW. ETSI’s proposal for NFV aligns with this concept,
— 46 —

3.5. REFERENCE TAXONOMY
emphasizing the importance of coexistence between virtual and physical func-
tions within the same environment, as detailed in ETSI [2013, 2014]. A PoC
following this mindset, with an NFV-based vRGW integrated with an existing
legacy operator infrastructure, was presented by Proença et al. [2017].
Security and User acceptance
The way operators offer these services to customers may influence their accept-
ance to this new paradigm. Some may be suspicious of having less equipment
in their houses, fearing a loss of control or property – even though legacy equip-
ment such as the RGW and the STB are already partially managed directly by
the operator. This can be counteracted with offering sufficient control of their
environment in the user portals provided to customers. Current RGW configura-
tion management requires a level of technical knowledge higher than the average
user has. Changing the RGW management paradigm to one more focused on
the users, as seen in Moyano et al. [2017b] and Moyano et al. [2017a] proposals,
can help them be more involved in the management and configuration of their
network.
Finally, some operators may include new services in the customers service pack-
age to make them more susceptible of accepting a virtualized RGW. Moreover,
if the service charge is not increased, customers may benefit from a cost reduc-
tion in their electricity bill, as the bridge devices that support a vRGW scenario
have lower power requirements compared with traditional devices.
Impact of Fog Computing
As the functions within the supporting infrastructure are abstracted and capable
of running on non-specialized hardware, the infrastructure becomes more homo-
geneous. This homogeneity enables the possibility of geographically decoupling
functions (Shinde et al. [2023]; Hu et al. [2017]). Consequently, function deploy-
ment location required for the vRGW can be optimized by instantiating them
closer to the end customer in smaller data centres. This optimization offers sev-
eral advantages, specially for latency-sensitive applications such as cloud-based
gaming, as demonstrated by Liao et al. [2016]. Another example is the case of
the Virtual Set-Top Box ( vSTB) discussed by Cruz et al. [2013a], where cer-
tain components of the service can be moved to the operator data centre in a
thin/zero-client fashion, as described by Suksomboon et al. [2015]. Addition-
ally, Desktop-as-a-Service ( DaaS) services that provide on-demand applications
and desktops can benefit from low latency between the data centre and the
customer premises ( Baun and Bouché [2024]; Guo et al. [2017]). According to
Fanning and Cannon [2015], customers can also benefit from this flexibility to
launch the services as close to their premises as possible, resulting in a better
experience.
Future Trends/Research Directions
Based on the previous discussion, it has become clear that the majority of ex-
isting proposals rely on NFV and SDN as fundamental building blocks for im-
plementing the concept of vRGW.
— 47 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
Current research often overlooks the requirements, strategies, and implications of
deploying network functions in close proximity to customer premises to enhance
device-to-function latency. Although a few authors do provide some insights,
they are rather limited. However, it is expected that the spread of 5G mobile
network implementation will prompt further developments in this domain, even-
tually leveraging the potential of fog computing to improve latency and QoE for
service consumption.
The simplification of the RGW device, along with the flexibility provided by
the SDN-enabled data plane, is key for several proposals, allowing operators
to improve QoE for multimedia applications, such as gaming or streaming, by
having a more controlled flow prioritization, as discussed by Amiri et al. [2017].
Similarly, Huang et al. [2016] and Flores Moyano et al. [2020] used those cap-
abilities to improve QoS, by allocating the available bandwidth using SDN flow
classification.
Another promising trend concerns the scalability and performance enhancement
of vRGW approaches based on SDN and NFV, where some of the intrinsic
performance and scalability limitations of the OpenFlow protocol are targeted
by P4 (P4 Language Consortium [2023]) – a notable development which has been
subject of considerable advances in the past few years. P4 is a language designed
for programming network device dataplanes, allowing to express how packets are
processed by a forwarding element. Unlike OpenFlow, which is a protocol that
provides the means for describing flow-oriented rules, P4 is a domain-specific
programming language designed in a protocol-neutral fashion. Support for P4
programmable data planes gained traction in several project communities such
as ONOS and CORD.
The already mentioned lack of publicly available quantitative data regarding the
scalability, performance and cost-effectiveness of vRGW frameworks also opens
a fundamental field for future research. While this lack of public data may be
essentially related with the strategic business value of such data for operators and
manufacturers directly involved in PoC implementations and trials – motivating
them to hold its public release – the truth is there is a considerable gap that
still needs to be filled before wider acceptance of the vRGW concept.
After massive deployment of virtualized gateways in production, it can be expec-
ted the emergence of new services (and improvements on existing ones). With
the increased service independence regarding physical gateway functionalities,
operators and researchers should have more freedom to exploit new services and
improving existing ones.
The introduction of virtualized residential gateways is also expected to bene-
fit other domains beyond the scope of telecommunications operators services,
improving support for other types of service providers and use cases, including
smart metering scenarios from utility providers or health-care services.
— 48 —

3.6. PROOFS OF CONCEPT
3.6 Proofs of Concept
This Section presents two PoCs that explore the concept of virtualizing the
RGW. Each PoC approaches different aspects. The first one focuses on enhan-
cing flexibility in service design and deployment of vRGW functions, leveraging
concepts of NFV and SDN. The main objective was to evaluate how virtu-
alization improves time to market between service design and implementation
of RGW functions. The second PoC introduces a container-based vRGW and
explores different deployment locations for the virtualized functions, depending
on the type of subscriber. Two main possibilities are tested, one targeted to a
residential scenario and another for business subscribers. Depending on the sub-
scriber, the functions can be fully virtualized in the operator infrastructure, or
use a hybrid model with some functions deployed on the operator infrastructure
and others at the subscriber premises, in the CPE device.
3.6.1 Flexible Service Design on Virtualized Residential Gateways
In the scope of the virtualization of the RGW, a PoC was developed with Altice
Labs within the scope of the HollisticSDN project, with the results published in
Proença et al. [2017]. The implementation of the vRGW prototype was based
on the principles of ETSI NFV architecture. Moreover, it used the OpenMANO
framework, which later was used as a base for ETSI OSM ETSI [2016a]. In this
PoC, an end-to-end orchestrator was used in collaboration with the Network
Function Virtualization Orchestrator ( NFVO). The first element (orchestrator)
establishes the connection point between BSS and OSS components and the net-
work infrastructure while delegating the management of the data centre-based
service components to the second ( NFVO). In this hierarchical architecture, the
integration of legacy and novel cloud-based components is done seamlessly, mak-
ing use of what is already available in terms of management systems in network
operators.
Virtualized Residential Gateway Logical Architecture
A simplified version of a traditional RGW deployment is composed of the fol-
lowing elements:
• ONT/RGW – the device that performs routing between the customer home
network and the provider optical network.
• OLT – this device aggregates the traffic from all nearby ONTs (access
network) and delivers them to the BNG.
• BNG – this device performs routing between the access/aggregation net-
works and the provider core network.
Although the RGW virtualization end goal is moving all its services to a cloud
environment, this prototype is focused only on the services provided by the
ONT/RGW. With that in mind, a data centre was deployed to instantiate the
virtual resources that support them. This data centre connects to the BNG
through a MPLS network, as shown in Figure 3.11.
— 49 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT

(MPLS)
Figure 3.11 : vRGW infrastructure.
To connect the devices located in the local network to the services available
in the data centre, a layer 2 Generic Routing Encapsulation ( GRE) tunnel is
established between the ONT and the private domain within the data centre
where the virtual resources are deployed. This way, from the local network
devices point-of-view, there is no difference between the traditional RGW and
the vRGW.
ONT OLT BNG Datacentre
VNFs

NFVO
Figure 3.12 : vRGW Proof of Concept.
Figure 3.12 shows the prototype’s logical architecture, where the following com-
ponents were added:
• User-Management Portal – a proprietary component from Altice Labs to
allow the end-client to configure the vRGW and the use of VNFs per
device when connected to the home network.
• End-to-End Orchestrator – is responsible for the management of end-to-
end services including the physical and virtual devices that support it.
This prototype uses the Network Activator ( NA), an AlticeLabs propriet-
ary product, to perform this function.
• NFVO – manages virtual resources in the data centre that take part in
network services. A customized version of Telefónica’s OpenMANO 8 was
used to create this component.
• Data centre – provides virtual resources as a service for the deployment
of VNF instances. In this prototype the chosen platform was OpenStack.
Additional extensions were used to create this component, which are de-
tailed further below.
Next, additional insights are provided on novel elements.
Custom Openstack
8which since then has been integrated in ETSI Open Source Mano ( OSM) ETSI [2021]
— 50 —

3.6. PROOFS OF CONCEPT
In this prototype, the OpenStack platform (IceHouse release) was used, integ-
rated with the following extensions previously developed by Altice Labs:
• Attachment-Point – this functionality enables the establishment of the
layer 2 tunnels, in this case GRE tunnels, to virtual networks in OpenStack
(Igor D. Cardoso [2014]).
• DHCP-Radius – this extension enables AAA on devices located in the local
network by using the DHCP server to send Radius messages to an AAA
server (Cunha et al. [2016]).
• Traffic Steering – this extension enables SFC on OpenStack through an
ordered list of traffic redirection ( Soares et al. [2015]).
The first extension enables the crossing of multiple domains transparently and
with minimum impact on traditional operations. This is important for the near-
future coexistence of novel and legacy services on top of the same infrastructure.
The other two components enable value-added services such as deploying content
filtering VNFs for children devices only. This is possible by using per device
authentication and SFC to redirect traffic to the VNF responsible for filtering
URLs ( URL filter).
User-Management Portal
This proprietary portal is deployed for the end-client to enable the configuration
of the vRGW and to register devices that are connected to the home network. By
registering devices, users are not only able to configure the available services for
them but are also able to set a default configuration for un-registered ones. This
approach provides the means for implementing a shared-management model in
which the end-client can manage the vRGW instance, similar to the local device
management capabilities of conventional RGWs. The management portal also
provides the configuration interface for the included services, namely: content-
filtering, application-filtering and firewall capabilities. The content-filtering ser-
vice enables the user to choose which Internet content should be filtered for a spe-
cific device, (e.g., webpages containing adult content). The application-filtering
service provides the means for the user to be able to block specific network-based
applications from specific devices (e.g., online-gaming). Finally, the firewall ser-
vice implements the traditional firewall capabilities available in most consumer
routers. Each time the users access the portal to set a specific device config-
uration, the portal communicates with the End-to-End Orchestrator through a
secure interface. The latter will then interact with the infrastructure bellow to
complete the configurations.
OpenMANO
In the ETSI model (cf. Figure 3.13), the MANO domain oversees the
virtualization-specific tasks, encompassing the NFVO, that manages network
services on the operator domain; the VNF Manager(s), which take care of VNF
instances: and the VIM, responsible for managing the NFVI computing, stor-
age and networking resources. The NFV framework is to be driven using a
set of metadata for describing the Service, VNF and Infrastructure requisites,
— 51 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
providing the MANO with information about available resources. This enables
resource providers ( VNF or Infrastructure) to develop compatible solutions that
can be integrated within the same framework.

Figure 3.13 : ETSI reference architecture for NFV (adapted from ETSI [2014]).
For the MANO role, the vRGW PoC resorted to the OpenMANO project 9,
which has developed an open-source implementation of the ETSI MANO archi-
tecture. OpenMANO is used as an orchestrator to instantiate and orchestrate
the infrastructure resources that support the functions, such as virtual machines
and tenant networks. OpenStack was used as VNFM by OpenMANO to manage
the NFVI.
The OpenMANO framework encompasses three main components: openvim,
openmano and openmano-gui, which respectively provide a VIM, NFV orches-
trator and web management Graphical User Interface ( GUI) capabilities. In
this prototype, several modifications were made to the openmano orchestrator
to support some features that were not available at the time. More specific-
ally, the orchestrator component was modified to create, manage, and delete
OpenStack routers, and to manage floating IPs and authentication keys of the
OpenStack VMs.
Virtual Network Function
The VNFs used in the PoC include an URL filter and firewall. The URL filter
used the DansGuardian Barron [2016] web content filter as a base to block spe-
cific webpages content by defining URLs and phrase-matching configurations,
9previously mentioned in Section 3.2
— 52 —

3.6. PROOFS OF CONCEPT
while the firewall VNF is based on the Alpine Linux distribution [ 2016] to im-
plement the firewall service and to block specific applications.
These services were selected to provide a minimal NFV-based implementation of
a vRGW with enough capabilities for integration and functional validation.
Test Scenario
The test scenario consisted on the complete service lifecycle, which included the
following stages:
• Service Design – by using the openmano-gui web GUI component, the
service designer can use the already available VNFs to define a new vRGW
service. In the example illustrated below, it is composed of two VNF.
Although the complete service design also involves designing the service
in the End-to-End Orchestrator, this is almost a one-time only process
because the actual service uses the legacy infrastructure in an overlay
fashion.
• Service Provisioning – when the ONT is turned ON, it will send a DHCP
request to the BNG, which will reply with an IP address after going
through the AAA processes. Moreover, the BNG will signal the End-
to-End Orchestrator to start the instantiation of the virtual resources in
the data centre. Finally, the OpenMANO orchestrator will be requested
to instantiate the necessary resources for the network service.
• Service Runtime – after the service has been provisioned, the client may
use the User-management Portal to configure the vRGW.
• Service Destruction – when the client wishes to terminate the service,
the BSS and OSS systems interact with the End-to-End Orchestrator for
the destruction of the resources. At this moment, the latter will signal
the openmano orchestrator to destroy the resources and re-configures the
network infrastructure elements.
Service Design
The prototype provides a significant flexibility improvement in terms of service
design, when compared with the process in a traditional RGW infrastructure.
A solution can be tailor-made to the customer needs by selecting the required
VNFs components, and their chaining structure. The OpenMANO framework
allowed an easier VNF-based service design with the use of its GUI component.
By providing a drag-and-drop web interface (illustrated in Figure 3.14), the
custom-made service can be built in an intuitive manner.
The service is built by dragging the elements that compose it (e.g., VNF com-
ponents and networks) to the service area of the web interface, and establishing
the connections among them. This enables the OpenMANO orchestrator to pro-
vision all the necessary resources in the NFVI for the remaining components of
the prototype. It also establishes the connection between the customer functions
and the operator components. Once these steps are completed, the service is
activated.
— 53 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
Figure 3.14 : vRGW Proof of Concept.
Functional Validation
Prototype validation was focused on functional and management aspects of the
platform in terms of provisioning and usability. Provisioning tests ensured the
correct integration and operation of the MANO and OSS/BSS systems for re-
source and VNF instantiation, as well as the vRGW NFV service lifecycle man-
agement. Moreover, the User-management portal was also tested to demonstrate
the possibility of blocking specific games and webpages on a per device basis.
Each configuration took a couple of seconds to become active and without dis-
rupting the service to other devices connected on the same network. Functional
tests were focused on validating the usability of the prototype, using smart-
phones and laptops as consumer devices. Those devices were connected to the
ONT wireless and wired networks and used to test/assess several use cases,
namely: webpage browsing, online gaming and consumption of media services.
Such functional tests were executed with success, over several time spans and
with different devices and users, in order to stress the platform.
Conclusion
This Section presented an implementation of an NFV-based vRGW, highlighting
the advantages it provides operators in terms of simplifying and accelerating
RGW service design. An architecture and a PoC were presented, providing a
vRGW scenario where NFV functions compose the services. One key benefit
of this architecture was the seamless integration and co-existence with legacy
infrastructures and their respective OSS/BSS components, such as the one used
by Altice Labs. This capability is crucial as it facilitates the adoption of the
vRGW paradigm in existing infrastructures, enabling deployments without the
need for major architectural changes within a limited time frame. This not
only ensures a smoother transition but also leads to a reduction of CAPEX,
since current deployments can proceed through their planned lifecycle, with
new concepts being gradually deployed.
— 54 —

3.6. PROOFS OF CONCEPT
3.6.2 Flexible Function Deployment
This Section presents the second PoC, demonstrating a virtualized RGW with
a distinct focus compared to the one previously discussed. The previous section
described a scenario where the functions were fully virtualized and instanti-
ated within the operator infrastructure. In contrast, the purpose of the PoC
presented in this section is to demonstrate a scenario where the functions can
be instantiated in a hybrid model, both within the operator infrastructure and
on the subscriber premises. This effort was developed as part of the M5G pro-
ject, in collaboration with Altice Labs, and its results have been published by
in Proença et al. [2021].
The two instantiation methods are representative of two distinct environments
and cater to different types of subscribers, namely residential and business.
These subscribers typically have different requirements for their services, en-
compassing factors such as service complexity, performance and reliability. As
an example, service downtime can impact residential subscribers’ trust in their
operator. On the other hand, it can have a significantly more damaging effect for
business subscribers, with a possible impact in their revenue. Moreover, some
businesses may have signed a SLA with their operator, and a service failure may
result in a breach or violation in that contract. Additionally, for residential sub-
scribers, service cost is frequently seen as one of the main factors when choosing
an operator.
These two types of subscribers also provide different sets of challenges for op-
erators. A residential scenario is challenging as it can scale up to millions of
devices per operator. It is a large and complex operation to manage, which can
benefit from centralized and managed function. On the other hand, a business
scenario is feature rich, as a result of complex network functions, often requires
distributed services, and can benefit from higher-capacity CPE devices.
Considering these differences, the PoC demonstrates two scenarios, represent-
ing the different needs of these subscribers. In a residential scenario , all
functions typically supported by the home router are instantiated in the oper-
ator environment, either in the core data centre or at the edge. The physical
Customer Premises Equipment ( pCPE) extends the client domain to the data
centre by establishing a Layer 2 ( L2) tunnel, for example using a Virtual Extens-
ible Local Area Network ( VXLAN) tunnel. Business scenarios may require
specific and feature-rich functions that may benefit from being hosted in the
customer premises. To address those functions, it is possible to follow a hybrid
approach with some services deployed locally, while the remaining are deployed
in the operator infrastructure. To increase flexibility, instead of a typical CPE
with limited computing capacity, a device with some computational power and
virtualization capabilities was used, so that both centralized and local func-
tions become virtualized (thus simplifying management and making it possible,
according to the circumstances, to decide where each function is located).
This results in two distinct types of devices to be deployed, depending on the
service being delivered:
— 55 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
• vCPE Small – for residential scenarios, all functions are virtualized at the
data centre, with a very basic pCPE.
• vCPE Large – for business scenarios, hosting of virtualized functions is
spread between the operator infrastructure and the pCPE.

Figure 3.15 : vCPE high-level architecture (business example with premises-

Figure 3.15 presents the high-level architecture of the vCPE. The vCPE Man-
ager provides the business logic, stores the function images, the data related
with the vCPE and client information, managing all operational tasks for cli-
ents, services and vCPE instances.
As already mentioned, two main scenarios were instantiated, one for residen-
tial and another for business customers. The first uses the vCPE Small as
pCPE, leading to an environment where the physical device deployed at the
customer premises is the most stripped and simplified, without local virtualiz-
ation of functions. This device is used when all functions are instantiated at
the operator infrastructure, which could be in an edge or core data centre. This
simplifies the physical device requirements to a minimum, to provide a basic set
of functionalities:
• Establish VXLAN tunnels for L2 connectivity between the customer
premises and the operator infrastructure.
• Providing wired and wireless LAN connectivity to the UE devices within
the customer premises.
• Bridging capabilities to forward packets within the local UE devices, and
between the customer premises and the data centre, through the VXLAN
tunnel.
For business environments with higher service requirements the vCPE Large
is used. In addition to the basic functionality provided by the vCPE Small,
the pCPE used in this scenario has increased computing capabilities, allowing
it to instantiate virtualized network functions locally at the customer premises.
This enables to further extend VNF location deployment options for function
instantiation: cloud (centralized), edge (central office) and customer premises,
depending on their specific requirements. As an example, functions such as rout-
— 56 —

3.6. PROOFS OF CONCEPT
ing should be deployed on the operator side, considering that the VXLAN tunnel
extends the edge of the customer L2 domain from the customer premises to the
data centre. Other functions might benefit the customer and/or the operator
by being moved to the customer premises, due to stricter latency requirements
or to reduce traffic at the operator infrastructure.
In this business scenario, the service offered to the customer may have more
advanced features, including distributed services to be deployed when there are
multiple branches associated with a business customer. An example of a value-
added function used in this PoC is Adkill, a VNF that filters unwanted ads by
blocking DNS queries to known URL addresses. The Adkill VNF is used as an
example of a function that reduces the load on the operator network, as some
of the DNS queries are resolved locally in the customer premises, offloading the
burden from the operator side, both in terms of traffic and associated service
load. At the same time, it enhances the customer service experience.
Architectural Approach
The PoC follows the architectural approach depicted in Figure 3.16. The oper-
ator infrastructure (represented as a Cloud Central Office) contains the vCPE
Management components, and a virtualized environment for the VNFs that are
deployed at the operator. These are instantiated in the form of container-based
instances.

Validation
A functional validation was performed addressing both scenarios, deployed in

two Intel(R) Xeon(R) Gold 5120 CPU @ 2.20GHz CPUs and 256GB of RAM.
However, the deployment did not use all the capacity available in the hypervisor.
The scenario represents a residential and a business customer service, as shown
in Figure 3.17. There are two subscriber premises illustrated, and the operator
infrastructure, with the access network representing a typical FTTx connection
between operator and subscriber. An external connection is used to forward
customer traffic to/from the Internet.

— 58 —

3.6. PROOFS OF CONCEPT
The validation was focused on highlighting the functionality of a vRGW scenario,
showing the two service models described earlier in this Section. The operator
infrastructure is represented by the VNF virtualization environment and the
vCPE manager, which manages customers, the catalogue of available VNFs,
the active VNF instances for each vCPE, and the lifecycle of the provisioned
vCPE devices and instantiated VNFs. The dashboard used to interact with
the vCPE Manager, for service design and subscriber management, is shown in
Figure 3.18
Figure 3.18 : Operator dashboard for service design and subscriber management.
The two service models were implemented, representing residential and business
scenarios. In both cases, the CPE device is connected to the data centre using
VXLAN tunnels to extend the L2 domain from the customer premises to the
operator infrastructure. When the service is fully established, the client UE
devices can be connected to the pCPE. The device performs a DHCP broadcast
request, which is received by the VNF handling these requests. The instantiation
process of the residential and business vCPEs, are shown in Figure 3.19 and
Figure 3.20, respectively.
There are two VNFs that provide the basic connection service: vRouter, which
provides access to the internet, and DHCP/DNS. The latter function is based
on an Alpine Linux-based container using Dnsmasq as DHCP and DNS servers.
This is a known lightweight and open-source DHCP and DNS server. Depending
on the service model (i.e., pCPE small or large), this function is instantiated
in the data centre or locally at the physical gateway. When the UE receives
an IP address lease, it can communicate with the devices connected to the
private network as well as access the internet through the vRouter function. All
VNFs expose a webservice that customers can use to manage their functions.
Moreover, each function provides a set of parameters that can be configured,
with some of them only available to be changed by the operator. An example
of the VNF configuration interface for the subscriber is shown in Figure 3.21. It
— 59 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
Figure 3.19 : Instantiation of residential vCPE.
is worth noting that this is a simplified interface as the subscriber management
experience was out of the scope of this PoC. In a real scenario an intuitive
dashboard would be essential, following the ideas presented by Moyano et al.
[2017a], considering its use by users with little technological background.
Finally, an example of an value-added function was implemented. This demon-
strated how a vCPE architecture can be used to offer new functions quickly to
the subscribers, without the need for slow firmware upgrades or even hard-
ware replacement as a result of new functions not being supported by the
device deployed in the customer premises. The VNF used in this example is
the Adkill, which reduces the amount of advertisement shown in UE using a
DNS-based filtering of known URLs. This VNF was built with open-source soft-
ware. The filtering engine uses Pi-hole 10, a well known advertisement filtering
service. A wrapper was also used to provide the basic web-interface to man-
age the VNF11. Figure 3.22 depicts the subscription process in the subscriber
management portal.
Figure 3.23 shows a subscriber web browsing experience with and without the
NF activated on the left and on the right, respectively. The process deploying
the Adkill VNF showed no noticeable downtime.
Conclusion
This section introduced the second PoC, which demonstrates an implementa-
tion of a virtualized RGW architecture that specifically addresses the particular
challenges faced by residential and business subscribers. Due to their distinct
requirements, operators have the flexibility to deploy VNFs in different ways.
As was shown, for residential subscribers, the service can be deployed with all
functions as virtualized instances in the operator data centre. This approach

3.7. SUMMARY
Figure 3.20 : Instantiation of business vCPE.
simplifies the customer premises device, providing financial and management
advantages for the operator. Consequently, customers benefit from faster de-
ployment of new functions and reduced failure rates resulting from streamlined
management on the operator end.
On the other hand, business subscribers can leverage a mixed deployment scen-
ario. While some functions, such as routing, may need to be deployed in the
operator infrastructure, more complex functions can be maintained in the sub-
scriber premises, as virtualized instances. This arrangement enables the enforce-
ment of low-latency requirements for specific functions while still capitalizing on
the advantages of virtualization for service introduction and updates.
Finally, as an example of value-added network functions and service update, an
advertisement filtering VNF is added to a subscriber service.
3.7 Summary
This chapter explored the concept of virtualizing the gateway that serves as the
connection point between the telecommunications operator infrastructure and
the subscriber premises. It provides an overview of the context and the mo-
tivations that operators have for this virtualization, considering the challenges
posed by traditional RGW architectures and the potential benefits of a virtual-
ized environment.
Furthermore, this chapter presented a comprehensive State-of-the-Art analysis
of the subject, highlighting initial proposals and the role of virtualization con-
cepts such as SDN and NFV in driving advances in this domain. It describes
initiatives from both industry and academic sectors, as well as those driven by
standardization bodies. Additionally, the chapter discusses performance optim-
ization strategies.
— 61 —

CHAPTER 3. VIRTUALIZATION OF THE CUSTOMER PREMISES
EQUIPMENT
Figure 3.21 : Subscriber web-interface to manage a VNF.
Also, a reference taxonomy was proposed for classifying past and ongoing efforts
in this domains. It provides a framework for categorizing different approaches
and can serve as a valuable resource for understanding and categorize the land-
scape of virtualized gateway initiatives.
Finally, this chapter presented two Proof of Concepts implementations that ex-
plore the virtualization of RGW from different perspectives. The first primarily
focuses on the improved flexibility that a virtualized architecture offers to tele-
com operators during the service design process. Traditionally, service design
has been a lengthy and vendor-dependent endeavour. However, within a virtu-
alized RGW architecture, as demonstrated in this PoC, service design can be
significantly streamlined.
The second PoC explores how to leverage the distinct requirements of two differ-
ent segments, namely business and residential subscribers. These have unique
needs that can be addressed through different implementation options. For
residential subscribers, all network functions can be deployed at the operator
infrastructure, streamlining the device installed at the customer premises, with
a considerable cost impact as these can scale up to millions per operator. In
contrast, business scenarios often demand more complex network functions and
stricter requirements, such as low latency or distributed services. In such cases,
a mixed deployment approach that combines operator infrastructure and sub-
scriber premises can be beneficial, accommodating the specific needs of the busi-
ness subscribers.
— 62 —
