CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
I
n the domain of telecommunications network operators, particularly in
broadband access services, the Border Network Gateway ( BNG) assumes
a pivotal role within the infrastructure, by establishing and managing sub-
scribers’ connections. It serves as a crucial component responsible for various
essential services that allow subscribers’ connections and help optimizing infra-
structure resources, including enforcing QoS policies and implementing ACLs to
safeguard against unauthorized access.
Similarly to other elements within the operator infrastructure, the BNG has
faced escalating challenges due to the surge in bandwidth demand, rapid sub-
scriber growth, and the introduction of novel services with unique requirements,
as noted by Prabhu [2015]. As a single point of failure for subscriber-operator
access, it becomes crucial to enhance management flexibility, simplify the device,
and improve overall resilience. Consequently, there has been a growing interest
among operators in virtualizing the BNG, following a trajectory which is similar
to the virtualization of the CPE gateway discussed in Chapter 3.
This chapter examines the case for virtualizing the BNG, starting with an ana-
lysis of the underlying motivations driving this virtualization effort. It provides
an overview of the SoA, highlighting the current proposals and advances related
with the Virtual Border Network Gateway ( vBNG) concept. Furthermore, it
presents an architecture for a vBNG, also exploring the seamless integration
with a vCPE infrastructure. Finally, a practical PoC is presented to validate
key concepts of virtualization required for the feasibility and support when im-
plementing a vBNG on COTS hardware. The contributions of this chapter have
been partially submitted in Proença et al. [2024].
4.1 Introduction
The Border Network Gateway ( BNG), which has also been formerly referred to
as Broadband Remote Access Server ( BRAS), is a router located near the edge
of the telecommunications network infrastructure, being responsible for authen-
ticating and mediating access between subscribers and the operator’s network.
It serves as a centralized point for aggregating subscribers’ connections and for-
warding their traffic to the core network. Originally, it was developed from
older telephone switching centres, and evolved to become the termination point
for subscribers’ multi service access. Positioned at the edge of the network,
the BNG assumes a central role in managing subscriber access, providing a
wide range of functions including authentication, policy management, address
assignment, traffic forwarding, accounting, shaping and filtering. Examples of
commonly used protocols for authentication and tunnelling are Point-to-Point
Protocol ( PPP)/Point-to-Point Protocol over Ethernet ( PPPoE) and Internet
Protocol over Ethernet ( IPoE), which establish a tunnel between the subscriber
gateway (CPE) and the BNG. Figure 4.1 illustrates an example of a deployment
scenario for the BNG, situated between the aggregation and core networks.
— 66 —

4.1. INTRODUCTION
Aggregation Network Core Network Internet
BNG

Figure 4.1 : Example of deployment location of the BNG (adapted from Bifulco
et al. [2013]).
The initial specifications for the BNG were defined in TR-092 ( Shrum [2004]),
which outlined the BRAS requirements. This was complemented in TR-101 ( An-
schutz [2011]), which described the migration of Asynchronous Transfer Mode
(ATM) networks to an Ethernet-based architecture. Initially focused on triple-
play deployments for residential and business subscribers using DSL as primary
broadband access technology, the architecture specified in TR-101 was widely
adopted and adapted to other technologies, such as FTTx/Passive Optical Net-
work (PON). Given its broad adoption, and aiming to reduce operational com-
plexity while maximizing interoperability, TR-156 ( Bathrick [2017b]) specified
details to encompass additional technologies such as GPON and XG(S)- PON.
The BNG functionality was also expanded in TR-178 Bathrick [2017a], offer-
ing services to residential and business subscribers, as well as defining mobile
backhaul deployments.
Over time, operators noticed an opportunity for new services to be supported
by the BNG, adding more functions and services to it, which increased service
offer and quality to subscribers. Consequently, this also increased the complex-
ity of the services supported by BNG, which also increased its management
complexity.
4.1.1 The need to Virtualize the Central Office
In a typical operator infrastructure, the BNG is a component of the Central
Office ( CO), which sometimes is still referred to as telephone exchange due
to historical reasons. In the past, this was the point where telephone lines
connected to the infrastructure using circuit-based switching. However, with the
evolution of operator networks and the adoption of packet-switched technologies,
it now serves as an aggregation point for business and residential subscriber
traffic. As a result, it is a critical point of the infrastructure that can potentially
be a contention point and bottleneck, impacting the latency and throughput of
subscriber connections, or even loss of connectivity ( Raj et al. [2022]).
— 67 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
The traditional CO has been under increasing pressure due to the evolution and
demands of modern networks. As an example, AT&T witnessed a network traffic
increase of 100 000 percent over an eight-year period, as reported by Prabhu
[2015]. Also, the introduction of new features often takes several months to
take effect while waiting for the vendor to release the next appliance update.
In some cases, a new feature may take years to be release while waiting for
standardization processes to go through.
For some telcos, the CO represents a large infrastructure that has been evolving
over several decades. As an example, in 2016 AT&T operated 4700 COs, with
some having up to 300 distinct hardware appliances, as reported by Peterson
et al. [2016]. This complexity imposes a heavy burden on the operator, leading
to excessive CAPEX and OPEX, and less management flexibility.
The increasing number of problems in traditional CO, including higher costs,
infrastructure complexity, lack of management flexibility, strong vendor lock-in,
and the long time needed to implement and deploy new features, has driven
operators to search for new solutions.
Cloud Central Office
In an effort to tackle the issues faced by telecommunications network operat-
ors in the traditional CO, the Open Networking Foundation ( ONF) created the
Central Office Re-architected as Datacenter ( CORD) project. Its primary goal
was to overcome the limitations of traditional CO design, which is heavily de-
pendent on closed and proprietary hardware. The project’s objective was to
re-architect the CO as a data centre, by taking advantage of SDN and NFV to
deploy network functions running as software instances on COTS hardware and
white-box switches. CORD used this equipment arranged into a rack-able unit,
referred to as Point-of-Deployment ( POD), to be deployed at the telco CO. The
reference implementation used four open source projects as support: OpenStack,
Kubernetes, ONOS, and XOS.
Disaggregation of legacy devices was a central point of CORD. In an operator
infrastructure, there is a set of key devices that have evolved significantly over the
years. They have become progressively more complex and cumbersome devices,
frequently being forced to support legacy systems. The main devices that were
targeted for disaggregation within the CORD project were: CPE, OLT, and
BNG, which are commonly found in the customer premises and on the access
network and aggregation networks, respectively. As noted in Chapter 3, CORD
is no longer an active project of ONF. However, it was an crucial part of the
virtualization of CO components, and an important stepping stone for further
ONF broadband projects.
The BBF has also worked towards the standardization of the Cloud Central
Office ( CCO), within their CloudCO initiative 1. In general, the CloudCO initi-
ative aims to evolve operator networks to be increasingly adaptable, agile, scal-
able and dynamic, while reducing CAPEX/OPEX. Moreover, it has the goal
of facilitating the introduction of new revenue-generating services. Contrasting
1https://www.broadband-forum.org/projects/cloud
— 68 —

4.1. INTRODUCTION
with traditional operator infrastructures, where different network technologies
with distinct ways of management must coexist, the BBF is defining broadband
networks to be increasingly homogeneous, with operators managing a single net-
work with different access technologies, and flexible enough to deploy innovative
services. However, they are aware that there is a considerable period of co-
existence with long-term non-virtualized components of the infrastructure. As
a part of this effort, the BBF has proposed a reference architectural framework
for a CCO in TR-384 ( Karagiannis [2018]), based on the principles of SDN and
NFV, with the capability of being deployed on COTS hardware.
4.1.2 Motivation for Virtualizing the BNG
The traditional BNG faces a set of challenges posed by the growth of subscribers
and by the number and complexity of services it supports. Customers can sub-
scribe to different services, from basic high speed internet access, to more com-
plex services such as VoIP, IPTV, Over-the-top ( OTT) streaming, and VPN
connections. Multiple factors, including the growth of connected subscribers,
and the number and complexity of services offered by operators, led to the BNG
facing challenges such as the ones pointed out by Patel [2023], and Bifulco et al.
[2013], including:
• Under or over-using of BNG instances.
• Management and maintenance challenges of operating geographically dis-
tributed BNG deployments.
• Being a single point of failure for subscriber connections.
• Service provisioning across all BNG instances and time to market of new
services.
• Difficulty of upgrading functionalities of hardware-based boxes as new ver-
sions may take a long time to be available, delaying the release of new
subscriber services.
• Monolithic and non modular nature. Operators may need to pay for all
functionalities, even in cases where they only want part of them (e.g.,
PPPoE tunnelling but not IGMP support).
Traditionally, the BNG has been a complex, proprietary, and expensive device
where subscribers gateways connect. As it can be a single point of failure in
the infrastructure, operators tend to be cautious when introducing new services,
as operations may be disrupted by an unexpected problem or incompatibility.
Being a connection point for thousands of subscribers, a problem in one BNG
instance can result in a large number of customer contacts for problem resolution,
which can put a considerable strain on customer support services and increase
truck roll expenses.
Another challenge faced by operators has been keeping revenue streams stable.
For several years, n-play bundles provided a growth of revenue per client. How-
ever, according to Benu Networks and Intel [2021], part of n-play subscribers
— 69 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
have been switching over to single-play broadband access. A contributing factor
for this may be the rise of popularity of OTT services used in applications such
as video streaming platforms. As a result, this puts additional pressure on ISPs
to offer new services. A virtualized BNG provides a flexible base for them to
develop services that can captivate their customers.
Considering these drawbacks, operators turned to NFV as a possible path to vir-
tualize the traditional BNG. A software-based approach can be easily composed
and configured, dynamically deploying it in different points of the operator net-
work, following the principles of NFV introduced in Section 2.2. Moreover, as
NFV began to take its first steps, the vBNG was presented as one of the first
use cases for this technology ( Nemeth et al. [2015]). An NFV-based approach
function deployment is easier and more flexible. As a result, it is simpler to
test new or updated BNG functions in a small subset of subscribers, reducing
the consequences of unforeseen problems, and accelerating the development and
deployment of new functions.
The virtualization of the BNG is also referred as disaggregation, which some
distinguish between horizontal (for functional decomposition) and vertical (when
separating hardware and software, or control and forwarding planes) ( BT Group
et al. [2020]).
The infrastructure supporting the BNG would tend to have lower cost with the
use of COTS equipment, and consolidate hardware as a result of using virtualized
instances. Moreover, traditional deployments require both the Control Plane
(CP) and the User Plane ( UP) to be updated at the same time. However,
subscriber growth and bandwidth demand may not be symmetrical. When just
one needs to be scaled up or down, the vBNG allows the operator to only scale
the affected components. Moreover, hardware upgrades can also be individually
targeted to one or the other. OPEX savings can also be achieved as a result
of separating the Control Plane ( CP) and User Plane ( UP). Traditional BNG
deployments use multiple geographically dispersed instances, each one being
a stand-alone instance in which management tasks must be done individually.
Moving to a vBNG scenario, which separates the control and data planes, allows
operators to have a centralized CP responsible for multiple UP instances. This
increases management flexibility, as it allows the BNG to become one centralized
instance managing multiple UP. This also allows the UP to be easily scaled up
or down, reducing or eliminating the pre-planning needs for resources such as IP
address pools. Software updates also benefit with the vBNG, as CP upgrades
can be done centrally rather than individually in multiple BNG instances.
A voiding vendor-lock in is also a target of the vBNG efforts. Standardiza-
tion consortiums such as BBF and ONF are working on standardized interfaces
and data-models to enable multi-vendor interoperability between different BNG
functions. As a result, operators could deploy components from different vendors
if known and open interfaces are used, allowing them to use multiple solutions
within their architecture to best suit their technical and business needs.
Time to market can be shorten when running a virtualized infrastructure both
in the development and deployment processes. As the update of virtualized
— 70 —

4.2. RELATED WORK AND STATE OF THE ART
instances is simplified, the development cycles and releases can be shorter and
more frequent. Moreover, operators do not need to wait for a new appliance
or hardware version release from the vendor. Updating running instances can
be as simple as restarting the software instance and loading the new image. As
a result, operators can make gradual updates to introduce small new features.
Also, software bugs can be corrected in a shorter time after being detected.
Overall, these are some of the main reasons why operators began looking for
options to shift into a virtualized, disaggregated BNG paradigm.
4.2 Related Work and State of the Art
The BNG virtualization has been a topic of research both for the academia and
the industry. This section presents research proposals and initiatives related
with the vBNG effort from three main areas: standardization bodies, academic
research, and industry initiatives.
Standardization Initiatives
To address known limitations of the traditional BNG model, the BBF presen-
ted the Disaggregated Border Network Gateway ( DBNG) project as part of the
CloudCO initiative. It was first presented in MR-459 in late 2019 ( Wan [2019]),
with its architecture and requirements published later in TR-459 ( Wan [2020]).
The main objective was to take the functionality of the BBF Multi-Service Bor-
der Network Gateway ( MS-BNG) and decouple the CP and UP, to be deployed
as virtualized instances. As a result, the available resources can be used with
greater efficiency and increasing the flexibility of management tasks. The be-
nefits of the DBNG were further detailed in MR-459.2 ( Newton [2020]), which
focused on improving service resilience and describing how it can improve net-
work recovery from BNG failures. Newton [2020] describes typical approaches,
such as to have backup Layer-2 tunnels. This is relatively simple to implement,
and can be implemented per access node. Possible drawbacks include the need
for subscribers to re-authenticate after the failure occurs, and the time for ser-
vice restoration is unpredictable, as it is dependent on the gateway reconnection.
Also, a large number of gateways trying to reconnect simultaneously can lead to
signalling storms. Another approach is state synchronization between an active
and a standby BNG. Compared with the first strategy, the subscriber state is
maintained during the failure, and the service suffers little to no outage. How-
ever, its more difficult to implement and often requires proprietary state syn-
chronization protocols, eventually leading to higher infrastructure costs to have
dedicated standby nodes. The second issue of TR-459 ( Wan [2023]) released
in 2023, improved resilience, scalability and faster deployment time, providing
better user experience to the subscriber.
The DBNG aims to provide resilience, without the drawbacks of traditional
strategies. By splitting the user and control planes, and having the CP re-
sponsible for multiple UP, master session states are (logically) centralized in
the CP, which sends forwarding and QoS rules to the UP. Session states are
synchronized between CP and UP using a standardized interface: State Control
— 71 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
Interface ( SCi). The configuration complexity is also simpler due to the CP
having an overall view of all UP, compared to configuring resilience schemes in
several traditional BNG independently.
Decoupling the control and user planes allows independent scaling of both. As a
result, an operator can keep up with the growth of subscribers and their band-
width demands independently, without the consequence of over-provisioning one
part.
The traditional BNG model uses proprietary protocols and interfaces for this
communication, within the same device. BBF proposes three interfaces (and
matching protocols) to provide the CP-UP communication in a standardized
way:
• Management Interface – allows the CP to manage the UP instances using
Yet Another Next Generation ( YANG) data models.
• State Control Interface – uses the Packet Forwarding Control Protocol
(PFCP) to program the forwarding state of a subscriber session, the default
route to redirect control packets between user plane and control plane.
• Control Packet Redirect Interface – redirects subscriber control packets
(e.g., DHCP and PPPoE) between the CP and UP.
The architecture divides the BNG into two main blocks: the Control Plane ( CP)
and the User Plane ( UP). In line with the concept of SDN plane separation,
the CP is responsible for control and management functions. Some examples in-
clude managing subscriber session states ( PPPoE and IPoE sessions), IP address
assignment, accounting, AAA, and Lawful Intercept ( LI). On the other hand,
the UP contains functions for traffic forwarding and policy enforcement includ-
ing, traffic management, subscriber packet statistic collection, IPTV multicast,
routing, and keepalive messages.
Academic Initiatives
One of the first proposals describing a vBNG PoC in the academic literature
was presented by Bifulco et al. [2013]. Authors also include performance tests,
specifically the number of PPPoE sessions successfully established per second,
resources consumed, boot times, and traffic handling capabilities. Their take on
the BNG virtualization includes the possibility to decompose its functionality
to increase deployment flexibility. As a result, some functions can be deployed
closer to the subscribers when needed. The architecture is based on ClickOS (a
combination of MiniOS and Click modular router), a modular software primarily
designed to perform network processing operations effectively, with little over-
head.
According to presented results, a ClickOS-based BNG can be as quick as 50ms
to instantiate, depending on the number of instances. Moreover, a simple com-
modity server equipped with two Intel L5640 CPUs and 24GB of memory can
potentially run hundreds of instances. Although this particular test was done
without traffic load, it demonstrates the flexibility improvement brought by a
vBNG to move instances between locations when balancing subscriber load. For
— 72 —

4.2. RELATED WORK AND STATE OF THE ART
network usage, the server was able to fill the 10Gbps port in almost all packet
sizes when instantiating up to 120 vBNG instances. However, authors do not
mention the enforcing of traffic policies (such as shaping or QoS enforcement)
that could reduce the reported system performance.
This work was further continued by Dietz et al. [2015] with an extended design,
detailed performance evaluation, and advanced features such as live session mi-
gration. The prototype was able to forward packets at rates up to 1.5Mbps
in a single CPU, while applying shaping and ACL rules to the traffic, with an
instantiation time of 50ms. Moreover, the system can migrate between BNG
instances within 800ms without dropping sessions. Although sessions are not
dropped, there is a measurable downtime at the end of the switching process
with no traffic being forwarded. Live migration is done using Xen’s VM mi-
gration feature, which does an incremental copy of the VM, with the downtime
depending on the VM load and throughput used by the clients. The results show
between 900ms and 1200ms with 2Gbps and 10Gbps of throughput, respectively.
For the session migration two BNGs are running in parallel and zero downtime
is achieved by moving all session states between VMs and then turning off the
original VM. Authors also simulate a BNG failure and recovery (one of the
problems in traditional BNG architectures), and were able to boot a new VM
and install all sessions under 800ms.
Woesner et al. [2016] analysed the impact of NFV in energy consumption, using
the BNG as test case. They used a software-based BNG in different deployment
settings: as a bare-metal implementation using Intel DPDK, a standard cloud
network stack, and an I/O optimized version. Their results were not able to
provide a definitive answer due of the number of variables involved. However,
they state the standard way of using VirtIO does not scale to the required band-
widths, and hardware acceleration I/O is essential. PCI pass-through provides
a satisfactory throughput (18.98 Mbps vs. 21.95 Mbps), with little penalty on
the energy consumption (125.2W vs. 138.9W). However, PCI pass-through is
unsuitable for a production deployment, as the number of VM instances per host
would be too limited (using PCI pass-through, the device is only available for
one VM). SR-IOV allows a NIC to be logically sliced into multiple instances,
each to be delivered directly to a VM. As a result, the number of VMs per
host would be considerable higher. However, due to limitations of driver and
software configurations, they were not able to test this scenario.
Nobach et al. [2017] studied the use of bare-metal switches in a carrier-grade
environment using BNG functions as a use case. Bare-metal switches are similar
to traditional ones, but are shipped with no OS installed. This has advantages in
some scenarios (especially in data centre settings), including a lower cost from
software licensing, increased efficiency and performance (by removing unused
services from the firmware), and improved connection with OSS/BSS systems
as a result of having a customized OS such as Open Network Linux ( ONL)2.
The prototype from Nobach et al. [2017] uses a different approach than the
NFV-based vBNGs, and as a result provides less flexibility. Authors deploy
2http://opennetlinux.org/
— 73 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
the functions in a bare-metal switch (Edge-Core AS5712-54x), on top of ONL.
Subscriber termination is VLAN-based. The PoC implemented a set of BNG
functions such as subscriber authentication (although simplified), packet for-
warding using OF-Data Plane Abstraction ( DPA), and management through
a WebSocket service. The results were focused on functional testing, as the
used testbed was not capable of reaching the switch backplane specification
limit of 720Gbps. This work evolved to align with the needs of large oper-
ator deployments regarding scalability, and to support features such as PPPoE
encapsulation/decapsulation of subscriber connections.
A different approach was presented by Kundel et al. [2019], taking advantage of
the processing capabilities of FPGA boards to perform BNG network functions.
Most cases of VNF implementation is designed using COTS hardware. However,
for specific applications the performance may not be satisfactory when compared
with the specialized hardware traditionally used in those applications, such as
custom-designed network switching ASICs. A middle ground may be achieved
by extending the processing using Graphics Processing Units ( GPUs) or FPGAs
(Kfoury et al. [2024]). In the specific case of the latter, although the performance
is still not as good as an ASIC-based solution, it brings a number of advantages
related to flexibility and cost reduction for development. In the proposal presen-
ted by Kundel et al. [2019], the goal was to achieve the best performance and to
reduce the penalty commonly associated to using commodity hardware. They
demonstrated how P4 can describe a high performance BNG data plane using
FPGAs. Although the proposal was within the scope of the CORD project data
centre architecture, BNG CP functions were not considered.
In their experiment, Kundel et al. [2019] found a significant difference in for-
warding latency between using FPGA and Linux, with the latter also showing
packet loss when forwarding traffic above a threshold throughput. Also, there
was a noticeable difference in latency between different FPGA implementations,
with the NetFPGA being better when compared with the Netronome Smart-
NIC. In both cases, the latency performance did not change when the number
of subscribers increased (from 32 to 4000), and no packet loss was observed.
This work was further detailed by Kundel et al. [2021].
Figueiredo and Kassler [2021] proposed a Hardware Abstraction Layer ( HAL)
between the UP and the CP of BBFs DBNG, as specified in TR-459 ( Wan
[2020]). A DBNG scenario can have one CP managing multiple UPs of dif-
ferent models and vendors. As a result, an infrastructure may have different
management UP APIs. An abstraction layer has the potential to reduce the
heterogeneity across an infrastructure.
Industry Initiatives
Similarly to the academy and standardization bodies, the industry has been
following the shift towards virtualizing the functions performed by the BNG.
The benefits of a virtualized infrastructure, including the reduction of Total Cost
of Ownership ( TCO) and time to market in new services, together with an easier
management and consolidation of hardware, has spark their interest to evolve
the traditional BNG to a virtualized implementation. Different areas of the
— 74 —

4.2. RELATED WORK AND STATE OF THE ART
industry need to come together to develop a vBNG, including operators, software
providers and hardware vendors. Some reports already describe commercial
deployments with live subscribers, as the case of Croatian Hrvatski Telekom
(HT).
In 2020, a group of operators formed the Open BNG initiative ( BT Group
et al. [2020]). In their position paper, they describe the problems they have
been increasingly facing in traditional BNG development and operation, and
how a software-based BNG on open hardware platforms can be beneficial for
all involved. The paper identifies the main components of a horizontal/vertical
disaggregated BNG, decomposed horizontally into two main blocks: Service
Edge ( SE) and Router. The first is responsible for subscriber session termina-
tion, service application, and policy enforcement. The latter aggregates/disag-
gregates traffic, serving as an IP router for several protocols including Border
Gateway Protocol ( BGP)/Intermediate System to Intermediate System ( IS-IS),
and MPLS. Each block can be composed of several modules, each performing a
specific task.
On a different perspective, the industry has been researching the BNG virtualiz-
ation topic prior to the Open BNG Initiative. Intel [2014] published a reference
architecture for a virtualized BRAS as an use case for NFV, also comparing
the performance of different deployments (on bare metal and virtualized), using
two different Intel processors (E5-2690 and E5-2690v2). The results show the
importance of hardware and the evolution of CPU performance in virtualized
environments, as both CPU models achieve similar values in bare metal, but
the older CPU is considerably lower for packet sizes of 256 Bytes and smaller
between the BNG and CPE. In scenarios with 512 Bytes packets or higher, all
the configurations achieve similar performance.
Chowdhury et al. [2019] presented an NFV-enabled vBNG. Several aspects were
considered to build this prototype. First, the ability to scale up and down the
resources used depending on workload. Also, the reference pipeline implements
a run to completion model. This means that all pipeline functions performed on
a packet are done on the same core. The reasoning is to avoid moving packets
between cores, minimizing cache misses and overall latency. As each vBNG
pipeline instance is allocated to a single core, scaling the capacity is done by
spinning up multiple instances. The traffic of each subscriber is directed to a
specific vBNG instance. As a result of typical traffic patterns, pipeline processing
requirements are not symmetrical. The downlink pipeline uses more cycles per
packet because of overall larger packets. Considering this, the downlink pipeline
is allocated a full CPU (two hyper-threaded cores), while the uplink is assigned
half CPU core (one hyper-threaded core).
More recently, a white paper ( Intel [2021b]) describes a cloud-based BNG project
by Croatia operator HT, in cooperation with Intel and the 5x9 Networks software
vendor. One of the goals was to understand if using a software-based BNG on top
of COTS hardware could lower the cost per gigabit on broadband traffic. Using
DPDK and Control and User Plane Separation ( CUPS), deployed in Hewlett
Packard Enterprise (HPE) servers running KVM as hypervisor for an OpenStack
— 75 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
platform, with two Intel®Xeon®Gold processors and eight 10/25Gigabits per
second ( Gbps) Ethernet NICs, resulting in a theoretical 200 Gbps per server.
Horizontal scaling is achieved by adding new vBNG instances.
Tests in a commercial traffic deployment reached 80 000 subscribers, providing
80Gbps per server. However they see potential for reaching 200 Gbps and doub-
ling the subscribers by further data plane developments. HT states that these
results reduce TCO (measuring 35% savings on the investment) and meet the
throughput and stability of physical BNGs. At the time of the report, subscriber
migration was ongoing and they had 450 000 live subscribers using the Cloud
BNG, and were planning a full migration of its broadband subscribers to the
cloud-based infrastructure.
Huawei, in cooperation with China Mobile, has also presented a cloud-based
BNG in a white paper ( Huawei [2017]), describing an architecture using CUPS.
This architecture is similar with the DBNG standardized by the BBF in TR-
459, separating the control functions to one central element managing multiple
UPs. The whitepaper notes the benefits of centralized management resulting
in simpler tasks, including a better use of the available IP range as a result of
centralized control. Moreover Huawei mentions the improved resource utilization
from having the UP hardware dedicated to forwarding functions, without the
storage and computing needs of CP tasks in each device as in a traditional
BNG scenario. Also, Huawei notes that new service provisions and updates
are made at CP level. In a traditional BNG scenario, for an operator with
hundreds of devices, an update could take up to one year to be completed,
as each BNG would have its CP functions updated. A centralized CP allows
that operation to be significantly shorter, and simpler. This cloud-based BNG
was tested in cooperation with Vodafone Portugal ( Vodafone [2018]), using the
CUPS architecture. The tests were done in two phases, between 2017 an 2018,
in a fixed broadband scenario.
Broadband Forum [2021] described the first implementation of TR-459 DBNG,
which enables CUPS, in a multi-vendor effort, ensuring interoperability between
different vendors. As a result, Vodafone in partnership with Benu Networks,
Casa Systems, Cisco and Nokia, was able to use software and hardware from
multiple vendors, and have the core control functions deployed in the cloud.
The trial was an opportunity to test the disaggregation advantages, including
the independent scaling of components to add more capacity and features to the
network.
4.3 An Architecture for a Virtual BNG
This section presents an architecture for a vBNG. As already described, the
BNG is part of the operator infrastructure, as being responsible for connecting
the subscriber to the operator. Additionally, the BNG is responsible for a set of
network functions that compose the subscriber service. Some of these functions
enable QoS policies enforcing, traffic shaping according to the subscriber plan,
and routing traffic to and from the internet. The BNG is responsible for provid-
— 76 —

4.3. AN ARCHITECTURE FOR A VIRTUAL BNG
ing and controlling access to a significant number of subscribers, often reaching
several thousands in a given CO.
Intel stated that the number of active subscribers per CO was around 5 000 (Intel
[2021a]), and in some cases this number may go up to around 35 000.
The traffic going to the BNG is differentiated depending on its type (e.g., IPTV,
VoIP, internet, GaaS). Depending on its nature, traffic will have different re-
quirements, such as low latency for real-time services such as VoIP or assured
bandwidth in IPTV. This traffic differentiation is used for the application of
QoS policies. Traditionally, the operators define a set of QoS classes defining
traffic priorities. A possible definition is detailed in Table 4.1. These classes are
shown as an example, as each operator will have its definitions with different
traffic classes.
Table 4.1: QoS classes (adapted from Kundel et al. [2021]).
Class Description
BElow (0) The traffic class Best Effort low priority (BElow) is used for hotspot
services where the residential gateway is a public WIFI access point at
the same time. As the customer, paying for this residential Internet
access, should not be restricted by third person traffic, an additional
traffic class is needed.
BE (1) Best Effort (BE) includes all data streams which belong neither to a pri-
ority traffic class nor to BElow. Compared to the other traffic classes, the
bandwidth utilization by this class is typically much higher. All “normal
traffic” of residential subscriber connections belongs to this class.
LD/LL (2/3) Low Delay (LD) and Low Loss (LL) are used for enterprise applications
of business customers only. For residential access, they are typically not
used.
MC (4) Multicast (MC) is used for television products of ISPs. Duplication is
realized at the BNG, and by that, data transfer in the core network is
strongly reduced. Note: This includes only live TV offered by the ISP,
not (third-party) on-demand video streaming.
VoIP (5) Many ISPs offer voice over IP (VoIP) services to their customers as well,
which require a very low latency and loss rates. Note: All application
layer VoIP approaches, e.g., Skype or WhatsApp, are handled as BE
traffic.
Ctrl (6) This traffic class is only used for controlling the ISP network including
access network and RGs.
4.3.1 Network Functions
A BNG performs several functions required to establish the subscriber service,
as well as functions that provide additional services to improve the quality of
the service and increase revenue. Below there is a list of some functions that are
commonly performed by a BNG:
— 77 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
• PPP/PPPoE session management and tunnel termination.
• Interface with AAA services.
• DHCP to assign IP address to subscribers.
• IP forwarding - Provides internet access to the subscriber.
• IGMP (to support Multicast for IPTV, reducing total traffic by duplicating
streams at the BNG).
• Quality of Service ( QoS).
• Virtual Local Area Network ( VLAN) management.
• Voice over Internet Protocol ( VoIP).
• Over-the-top (OTT) video streaming.
• Video game streaming – Gaming-as-a-Service ( GaaS).
• Business VPN services.
• Lawful Intercept ( LI) (allows mirroring for law enforcement agencies).
• Traffic shaping – taking into consideration the subscription plan, and the
service classes.
• ACL – to ensure that packets belong to an established session.
• Address Resolution Protocol ( ARP) Proxy – ARP proxy, replies to ARP
requests coming from interfaces on the BNG IP side.
This list is not exhaustive, and is presented to list some examples. The archi-
tecture is designed to be flexible and to foster the development of new network
functions as it is one of the main drivers for virtualizing components of the op-
erator infrastructure, increasing service flexibility and allowing new functions to
be developed while shortening the time for service design and deployment.
4.3.2 High-Level Architecture
This section proposes a high-level architecture for a vBNG. It follows the
CUPS concept presented in the BBF DBNG proposal described in TR-459 ( Wan
[2020]), separating the control and user planes. As a result, independent scaling
of the infrastructure is possible, depending on the number of active subscribers
(which puts additional load on the CP components), or their traffic (increasing
the DP needs). As an example, when the number of subscribers connected in-
creases or decreases, the operator can scale the CP, allocating more hardware
when needed, but also free it when not in need, independently of the DP. On
the other hand, if traffic increases or new network functions are added, the op-
erator can change the resources allocated to the DP, which handle functions
such as traffic forwarding and QoS enforcing. Figure 4.2 illustrates this archi-
tecture.
— 78 —

Figure 4.2 : An architecture for a vBNG.
Architecture Components
The main components for the this architecture are the following:
• Management – responsible for managing the components of the vBNG.
This includes instantiating and configuring functions, and consulting sub-
scriber information.
• Orchestrator – responsible for orchestration of functions such as vBNG
worker scale in-out and migration.
• vBNG DB – contains information such as service plans, subscribers, plan
associations, and vBNG workers.
• DHCP – service that assigns IP addresses to subscriber gateways
• Function Catalogue – registry of the VNF that can be performed by
the vBNG worker.
• Tunnel Termination – responsible for receiving and establishing PPPoE
connections.
• vBNG Worker – instantiates a set of chained network functions (such
as ACL, QoS, traffic shaper). It can cater to multiple tenants in cases
such as residential n-Play subscribers, or be dedicated to a single tenant
for customized services, especially in the case of business clients. Workers
can be migrated between Data Centre PoPs to balance the load of the
— 79 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
infrastructure. Since different workers can be deployed at the same time,
operators can easily test new services on a limited client base. This agile
development process can potentially accelerate function iteration times
compared with traditional BNGs, as it eliminates the need to wait for
the release of a new BNG appliance version. Once the image registry is
updated with the new VNF version and the BNG instance re-deployed,
the update takes effect.
• vBNG worker catalogue – registry storing information about vBNG
worker types available. This includes which functions they perform, how
they are chained, and if the worker type is locked to a specific subscriber
(for example, in a tailored business plan)
• Steering Function – steers the subscriber traffic to and from its vBNG
worker.
As previously described, the architecture is separated into control and data
planes components (CP and DP). The DP is responsible for tasks such as provid-
ing DHCP addresses, storing subscriber info – including subscription plan(s) –
a catalogue for DP functions that can be instantiated in the vBNG workers, a
catalogue of vBNG worker types, and orchestration of network functions (this
includes vBNG worker scaling in/ou depending on load).
The DP include three main components. The tunnel termination receives
tunnelling requests and establishes the connection between operator and cus-
tomer premises equipment ( vCPE). The vBNG worker processes network
traffic. This component is composed of a set of software-based network func-
tions. Not all subscribers will use the same worker type, and can have different
sets of functions. For example, business subscribers may require custom func-
tions for a tailor-made service. This worker can be single tenant. For residential
subscribers, an operator may offer a set of plans for a customer to subscribe. In
these cases there will be a predefined set of vBNG worker templates available (in
the vBNG worker catalogue). As there will be multiple customers subscribing to
these plans, the workers can be multi-tenant, allowing for a more efficient use of
infrastructure resources. A given PoP will have multiple instances of each type
of worker, depending on how many subscribers the PoP is serving. These can
be scaled in or out depending on how many subscribers are connected, and the
load they put in the vBNG. Finally, the steering function is responsible for
forwarding the traffic from tunnel termination to a specific worker.
4.3.3 Multi-Access Edge Computing
Multi-Access Edge Computing ( MEC) is an ISG within ETSI, representing a
network architecture that extends cloud computing capabilities to the edge of
the network, allowing computing resources and services to be deployed closer
to end-users, devices, and data sources, rather than being centralized in distant
data centres. The main goal is to reduce latency, provide efficient network
operation and service delivery, and improve customer experience.
When first introduced, it had a slightly different scope and was named Mo-
— 80 —

4.3. AN ARCHITECTURE FOR A VIRTUAL BNG
bile Edge Computing. It was an architecture enabling computing resources and
services to be deployed at the edge of mobile networks, such as 4G and 5G. It
aimed to improve the performance and efficiency of mobile applications by bring-
ing computing resources closer to the end-users and devices, focused primarily
on the needs of mobile operators and mobile devices. Its architecture was there-
fore optimized for mobile use cases. Over time, the concept evolved to include
different access technologies, broadening its scope beyond mobile networks. Cur-
rently, it enables computing resources and services to be deployed at the edge of
any access network, such as Wi-Fi, fixed-line broadband, and satellite networks.
This shift prompt ETSI to modify the name to Multi-Access Edge Computing,
to better cope with a wide range of edge computing applications (including those
that are not specific to mobile networks).
Nowadays, MEC is designed to be a more general-purpose edge computing archi-
tecture that can accommodate various access networks and use cases. Aligned
with ETSI NFV. MEC also supports real-time and low-latency applications,
such as AR, Virtual Reality ( VR), and autonomous vehicles. The architec-
ture consists of three main components: the edge cloud, the edge nodes, and
the edge devices. The edge cloud provides the computing resources and ser-
vices that are required to support edge applications. The edge nodes are the
physical devices that host and manage the edge cloud resources, such as serv-
ers, gateways, and routers. The edge devices are the end-user devices, such as
smartphones, laptops, and IoT devices, that connect to the edge nodes and use
the edge cloud resources. The reference architecture is illustrated in Figure 4.3,
with its functional elements and reference points.

The main components of the MEC architecture ( ETSI [2022]) are the follow-
ing:
— 81 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
• MEC host – an entity that contains a MEC platform and a virtualization
infrastructure providing compute, storage, and network resources to run
MEC applications.
• MEC platform – consists of the essential functionality needed to run
MEC applications.
• MEC applications – these are instantiated in the virtualization infra-
structure
• Management – is done at two levels: system, which includes the MEC
orchestrator as core component, overviewing the whole system; and host,
comprising of MEC platform manager, and the virtualization infrastruc-
ture manager.
At MEC system level, the Multi-access Edge Orchestrator ( MEO) is a core
component of management and works with the OSS to manage the MEC host
level. Below at the MEC host level, the MEC host includes the MEC platform
and the virtualization infrastructure that hosts the MEC Apps (e.g., VMs and
containers). This level also includes the MEC platform manager that collects
information about the system and allows information sharing with other local
or remote MEC systems.
The MEC concept is complementary to NFV. However, NFV is not required, as
MEC was designed to have different deployment options, increasing its range of
applicability. ETSI has published a report analysing the deployment of MEC in
an NFV scenario, sharing the same virtualization infrastructure ( ETSI [2018]).
Moreover, management components defined by the ETSI NFV MANO can fulfil
management and orchestration tasks from the MEC infrastructure, increasing
the level of integration.
vBNG functions deployed as MEC applications
The idea behind MEC could be a good candidate for the deployment of vBNG
functions. MEC was designed to provide computing resources near the user. A
MEC-enabled vBNG could improve the subscriber service by having the BNG
network functions deployed in an optimal location. The industry has been de-
fining the edge as having a maximum RTT of 20ms to the subscriber, a value
required for applications such as cloud VR. The vBNG architecture previously
presented is flexible in its deployment, and could fit in a MEC-enabled infra-
structure.
In such a deployment, the data plane components (e.g., the tunnel termination
and vBNG workers) would be deployed as MEC applications, distributing them
across the edge, closer to the subscribers. The control plane components (e.g.,
management and orchestration) could be deployed in a centralized part of the
operator infrastructure.
A deployment example of the architecture in a MEC environment is illustrated
in Figure 4.4. On the centre left, the operator edge is represented, with two
MEC hosts in different locations. For the sake of simplicity, the remaining
components of the MEC architecture are not represented in the diagram. The
— 82 —

4.4. PROOF-OF-CONCEPT VBNG
operator cloud is represented in the centre right. The vBNG control plane
components are deployed here.

The performance of a vBNG worker could be monitored by providing SLAs
that use data provided by the MEC system to ensure that the functions are
performing as intended.
Performance penalty of software-based network functions
Running network functions as software instances has multiple advantages com-
pared with traditional appliances on top of specialized ASICs. However, some
drawbacks, including performance penalties, are still possible. When designing
a vBNG, these need to be taken into consideration to reduce or mitigate them.
This penalty is well documented and the research community has been address-
ing this issue, for example offloading some processing tasks to flexible hardware
such as SmartNICs or FPGAs, which can increase the processing performance
of network functions.
4.4 Proof-of-Concept vBNG
Section 4.3 presented and described an architecture for a vBNG, with the main
goal of leveraging virtualization concepts to provide increased flexibility and
functionality over the traditional BNG. To validate and analyse some of the
concepts supporting the architecture, this section presents a PoC for a software-
based vBNG. The main goal is to evaluate the feasibility of implementing a
vBNG with basic network functions, instantiated in COTS hardware within a
data centre environment. This section starts by describing the PoC and its
— 83 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
components, followed by the details of the implemented testbed. After that fol-
lows the tests description and execution, and a discussion of the results finalizes
the section. A high-level diagram is presented in Figure 4.5, depicting the PoC
scenario.

Figure 4.5 : vBNG PoC – High-level diagram.
4.4.1 Components
For the implementation of this PoC, open-source software was used when pos-
sible. The data centre representation was built using MicroStack to deploy the
VNFs that support vBNG components. Orchestration and management of VNF
is done using ETSI’s OSM. FreeRADIUS is responsible for AAA, receiving the
PPPoE connection requests sent by the subscribers gateways to the vBNG in-
stance, which forwards them to the AAA. The virtualized functions required for
the base functionality are performed using VyOS. This is a well-known virtual
router, with additional services that can be used as BNG functions, including
PPPoE tunnel termination, routing, DHCP for subscriber CPEs devices.
OpenStack
OpenStack3, is a well-known open-source cloud computing platform, both in
public and private clouds, and is used here as the VIM. Since the complete
functionality of OpenStack is not needed, the scenario was implemented using
MicroStack4, a simplified version of OpenStack without the known limitations
of DevStack. Although at the time of writing MicroStack is based on K8s, when
this testbed was built MicroStack was based on Canonical Snaps 5.
Network Function Virtualization Platform
The NFV platform used in the scenario was OSM. As mentioned in Chapter 2,
Open Source Mano ( OSM)6 is an open source MANO stack following ETSI’s
3https://www.openstack.org/
4https://microstack.run/
5https://snapcraft.io/
6https://osm.etsi.org/
— 84 —

4.4. PROOF-OF-CONCEPT VBNG
NFV reference model, aiming to meet NFV requirements for commercial oper-
ator NFV deployments. In the PoC, OSM is used for managing and orchestrating
the NFs, and together with OpenStack represents the NFV platform.
Another NFV platform option would be Open Network Automation Platform
(ONAP)7. However, several factors made it an unsuitable candidate, includ-
ing excessive complexity, too high minimum hardware requirements (224 GB of
RAM, 112 vCPUs, and 160 GB of storage), and limited access to support chan-
nels. Moreover, as OSM is an ETSI-hosted project, it offers better alignment
and support of ETSI-NFV.
VyOS
The software-based router platform VyOS8 is an open source Network Operating
System ( NOS), featuring a set of network services, including routing, firewall,
DHCP, and tunnel termination. This project is based on a fork of the well-known
Vyatta 9, a software-based appliance providing virtual router, firewall, and VPN
functions. In this PoC, VyOS is used to implement the main network functions
of the vBNG, namely carrier-grade Network Address Translation ( CGNAT),
DHCP, routing, PPPoE tunnel termination, subscriber connection management,
AAA forwarding, and ACL.
FreeRADIUS
FreeRADIUS 10 is a free Remote Authentication Dial-In User Service ( RADIUS)
software suite, and one of the most widely used RADIUS server, including ISP
and telco companies. In the PoC, is it used as subscriber authentication server,
and receives the requests forwarded by VyOS.
Customer Premises Equipment
On a real broadband service, the subscriber premises have a gateway device es-
sential for the service. Its main function is to establish the connection between
the subscriber devices and the operator core network. This PoC has a stripped-
down CPE providing basic functionality to initiate and establish the PPPoE
connection to the vBNG. For the validation tests, a CPE is deployed for each
subscriber with two network interfaces, one facing the operator and the other to
the subscriber premises. Private IP address allocation for subscriber devices is
manual. The performance tests use a different CPE implementation, in which
a single machine provides the connection of multiple subscribers. As these are
done using a considerable higher number of subscribers, one instance is used to
represent them all. This also simplifies the test workflow and reduces the test-
bed resource requirements. Considering this, the performance tests were done
between the CPE and a remote iPerf server, with the vBNG in the middle. Since
the main objective is to evaluate the capacity of the vBNG to handle traffic, not
using UE instances reduces variables and the possibility of unwanted bottle-
necks that might have tainted the results, such as the CPE routing/forwarding
7https://www.onap.org/
8https://vyos.io/
9https://docs.vyatta.com/en
10https://freeradius.org/
— 85 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
performance.
For the subscriber connectivity test, a VM was connected to the CPE to perform
the role of a subscriber UE. As the CPE functions are out of the scope of this
PoC, it is not providing all the traditional subscriber-related services such as
DHCP and firewall. Private IP addresses have been set manually in the UE and
CPE, and the latter is forwarding packets between the access network and the
subscriber premises.
Hardware specification
The scenario was set up on a Dell PowerEdge R440 server with two Intel(R)
Xeon(R) Gold 5120 2.20GHz processors, running on 256GB of RAM, with Hard
Disk Drive ( HDD) storage. The server has VMWare ESXi hypervisor installed
to host the VMs.
4.4.2 Tests
This section describes the tests done for validation and evaluation of the vBNG
performance. They are presented in two categories: validation with functional
tests confirming vBNG functionality to set up and authenticate subscribers; and
performance tests to analyse the throughput that a vBNG instance can deliver
using different network functions, while analysing the computing resources used.
The main objective of the performance tests is to measure the impact on resource
consumption when applying traffic policies in software-based network functions
running on COTS hardware.
Validation
Scenario validation was done with a set of tests that assess the main steps
required for a subscriber to be authenticated and to use the broadband service.

Performance
Another set of tests focused on the performance of the vBNG. These aim to eval-
uate the feasibility of using virtualized functions to perform vBNG tasks. Their
goal is to assess the impact of running traffic policies commonly applied in a
broadband service. These are fundamental to allow the distribution of resources
among the connected subscribers, and to assure that the subscription plans are
used within their limits, which includes not allowing subscriber’s download and
upload to go over the limits specified in the plans. The network functions used in
these tests are QoS enforcement, traffic shaping, and subscriber ACL. The lat-
— 87 —

CHAPTER 4. EXPANDING THE VIRTUALIZATION OF OPERATOR’S
INFRASTRUCTURE TO THE BORDER NETWORK GATEW AY
ter assures that only authenticated subscriber traffic is forwarded by the vBNG,
and that it goes to and from the Internet, and not to operator components such
as the RADIUS server. Traditionally, these functions are performed by special-
ized hardware with bespoke ASIC silicon, which are highly efficient but have
known drawbacks, including high cost, low manageability and upgrade flexibil-
ity, and vendor lock-in. Considering this, the performance tests focused on how
vBNG throughput and resource use are penalised when applying traffic policies,
compared with simple packet forwarding.
The testbed represents a scenario where a vBNG instance is serving a set of
subscribers consuming services from the internet. For simplification purposes,
the traffic is flowing in one direction, downstream to the subscriber. The traffic
generated represents an Internet connection with data transfer in a download
session, and an IPTV stream.
Preliminary results showed unsatisfactory performance. This was a result of
the scenario being implemented on a single hypervisor: Microstack was being
deployed on top of the VMWare ESXi, resulting in nested virtualization for
the vBNG VM. As in a production scenario this would not happen, and the
orchestration was not being evaluated in the performance tests, the scenario
was simplified to remove OSM and Microstack, which have been used in the
validation tests, and the vBNG was deployed directly on VMWare ESXi.
Global Settings
The performance tests are done by generating traffic between an iPerf server
and the subscribers with a vBNG instance in the middle, forwarding the net-
work packets. Different scenarios were defined using several combinations of
traffic policy settings. There is also a baseline configuration applying only traffic
forwarding. Two streams were defined, TCP for Internet traffic with no band-
width limit, and a second stream representing the throughput of two channels
of IPTV coded in MPEG-4 using User Datagram Protocol ( UDP), with a total
of 20 Mbps. The global specifications common to all test scenarios are detailed
in Table 4.3.
Table 4.3: Tests specification – global settings.
Item Description
Test Runs 10
Test Duration 60 seconds
Traffic Stream 1 Internet Traffic, TCP without throughput limit
Traffic Stream 2 IPTV. UDP, limited to 20Mbps
Subscriber plan limit 100Mbps (Total)
Number of Subscribers 10, 25, 50, 75, 100, 150
To compare the performance and resource usage of different traffic policies, a
set of scenarios is defined below. The first scenario acts as a baseline, using only
— 88 —

4.4. PROOF-OF-CONCEPT VBNG
one data stream (TCP - Internet), and not applying any policies. The second
scenario uses both streams and enforces the traffic shaping network function,
which limits the total bandwidth of each subscriber to 100 Mbps. Scenarios 3
and 4 add QoS and ACL to the settings of the second scenario. Finally, the
fifth scenario enforces all traffic policies on two data streams (shaping, QoS, and
ACL). The five scenarios are listed below:
• Scenario 1 – One Stream per subscriber (baseline).
• Scenario 2 – Two Streams per subscriber with traffic shaping.
• Scenario 3 – Two Streams per subscriber with traffic shaping and QoS.
• Scenario 4 – Two Streams per subscriber with traffic shaping and ACL.
• Scenario 5 – Two Streams per subscriber with traffic shaping, QoS and
ACL.
Results
The results are illustrated in the following graphs. Each figure presents a scenario
with sub-figures showing the average throughput of each subscriber, CPU usage
of the vBNG VM instance, and network losses. Depending on the configuration
of each scenario, the bandwidth sub-figure can show one or two streams. This
will also reflect on the data loss sub-figure. When a TCP stream is included in
the test, the results will include a TCP stream retransmit sub-figure. As all five
scenarios have an Internet stream, TCP stream retransmits are present in all
Figures. Scenarios with an UDP stream will have a UDP packet loss graph. The
first scenario (baseline) is the only one missing an IPTV stream. As a result,
Scenarios 2 to 5 include a UDP packet loss graph in the Figure.
Figure 4.6 presents the results for Scenario 1. This only has one stream ( TCP)
without traffic policies applied, meaning that the vBNG is forwarding traffic at
maximum capacity for all subscribers options. Multiplying the average bitrate
by the number of subscribers in a test, it is observable that as the number of
subscribers increases, the total throughput goes down from 6309 Mbps with 10
subscribers, to 4140 Mbps when serving 150. This reduction in total through-
put is followed by an increase of CPU consumption, as the vBNG has do deal
with a higher number of connections. More specifically, the average CPU usage
increases from 87.4% and 62.2% (in CPUs 0 and 1) to 100% and 81.3%, respect-
ively. The average retransmits in Figure 4.6.c indicate that the CPU is not able
to process all the packets that are arriving at its network queue. As the queue
is receiving more packets than what can be processed, part of them are dropped
and have to be sent again.
Figure 4.7 presents the results from Scenario 2. This scenario adds a second
data stream ( UDP), representing IPTV streaming, with speed limited to 20
Mbps. The scenario also enforces traffic shaping, which limits a subscriber total
bandwidth to 100 Mbps. It is noticeable that the vBNG can forward all traffic
in tests serving 10 and 25 subscribers. In tests with a sufficiently large number
of subscribers for the vBNG to no longer being able to forward all subscriber
traffic, it uses almost all CPU available. In Figure 4.7.b a great increase in CPU

usage is visible between 25 and 50 users, total throughput goes up 71% from
2420 Mbps to 4130 Mbps, but CPU usage surges up 660% and 600% (for CPU 0
and 1). This is likely due to soft interrupts of the packets reaching the network
interface, as the queues are not being processed at a sufficiently fast rate. When

and UDP packet loss.
Figure 4.8 addresses Scenario 3, which uses mostly the same parameters as Scen-
ario 2, but adds QoS to prioritize the UDP streams over TCP, as it represents
IPTV data. For a comfortable subscriber experience this stream should have
higher priority than Internet traffic, as most applications are tolerant to vari-
able throughput (such as large file download). However, for latency sensitive
applications such as IPTV streaming or VoIP communication, having best ef-
fort priority during peak hours could result in dropped frames, which would
negatively impact the user experience.
When we compare CPU usage, the difference is clearer when the low number
of subscribers does not demand all the resources from the vBNG. For example,
when serving 10 subscribers, the average resource consumption in CPU 0 and
CPU 1 was 3.62%-3.62% in Scenario 2, and 4.90% 5.02% in Scenario 3, for the
same total throughput of 968 Mbps. Although it is a 35% increase in CPU 0
and 39% in CPU 1, it is small in absolute values. When serving 25 subscribers,
CPU 0 and 1 had an increase of 22% and 35%, respectively. Both increases are

Finally, the results from Scenario 5 are illustrated in Figure 4.10. This scenario
applies all traffic policies used in the testbed, namely traffic shaping, ACL, and
QoS. It is similar to Scenario 3, which applied shaping and QoS, but also
includes ACL. Similarly to what happened between Scenarios 2 and 4, the ACL
functionality has little impact on the resources consumed and total throughput,
with the results fluctuating around the same values for both scenarios.
Memory Usage
The memory usage in the vBNG function remained stable through all test scen-
arios. Figure 4.11 shows the memory used by the VM of the vBNG instance,
representing memory used, buffer and page caches. Varying the number of sub-
scribers connected and the traffic policies applied in the traffic forwarding showed
a negligible variation in the memory values, with less than one GB needed to
run one vBNG instance. This value could be lower by streamlining the VM.
However, as this is a PoC, the main goal was not to see how low the memory
requirements could be, but to evaluate if the vBNG instance needed a higher
amount of memory when applying more traffic policies, or with an increase of
connected subscribers.

Looking at the results, its noticeable that for a subscription plan limited to
100 Mbps the vBNG instance in this hardware can deliver full speed while serving
twenty five subscribers in all five scenarios. When connecting fifty or more, all
tests have throughput contention. As a result, there is a visible surge in the
CPU usage for tests serving fifty or more subscribers. This is likely due to a
high interrupt rate, as the number of packets reaching the receiving network
interface are higher than the CPU can process and forward. This is also visible
in the TCP retransmit and UDP packet loss.
It is worth noting that in a real scenario the vBNG would not need to serve all
subscribers at max throughput, as in normal use they do not use all their band-
width constantly and simultaneously. As a result, operators often plan BNG
deployments to be oversubscribed. Contention ratios will vary depending on
several factors, including access technology and location. As a result, in occa-
sional moments some subscribers may not be able to use all the bandwidth in
their subscription plan when a large percentage of subscribers are demanding
high network load. When throttling connection speeds, an operator might en-
force it with a higher intensity on users that meet certain traffic pattern criteria,
such as heavy file download or demanding OTT services. It would not be un-
common to have up to 1:50 contention ratio in a residential service, and 1:20 in
a small business setting. A vBNG infrastructure can improve this, as it becomes
possible to scale up the number of instances, and migrate some connections when
the available vBNGs have higher utilization.

Figure 4.10 : Key measurements for Scenario 5.
Overall, applying traffic policies on network traffic did show some measurable
differences in resource use in this PoC, although small.
Although the main focus of the tests is not to check raw performance, one
aspect that could improve the results is the hardware used in the testbed. The
two Intel®Xeon®Gold 5120 CPUs installed in the testbed hypervisor were first
released in 2017, and newer models would allow an improved performance.
Another performance improvement for the PoC would be to use DPDK in the
vBNG instance. When using DPDK, the network uses a pooling mechanism
instead of the more traditional interruption. By constantly pooling the NIC,
the CPU gets network packets as soon as they are available, rather than waiting
to be interrupted. Also, DPDK reduces CPU overheads from context switching,
which were especially impacting in tests that exhausted the CPU capacity to
process network packets. Moreover, with traditional interrupts, when there is
a large amount of traffic arriving at the NIC (as in the tests with fifty or more
subscribers), the CPU gets overwhelmed, as seen from the larger increase in CPU
usage relatively with the increase of total throughput when serving that amount
of subscribers. A hypervisor with a later model of CPUs installed would allow
for an improvement in packet processing speeds. Lastly, the vBNG instance
OS could be fine-tuned and streamlined for this specific application to improve
performance.

evaluate the impact of enforcing traffic polices, some considerations can be ex-
trapolated to estimate the resources needed to scale the vBNG adjusted to a real
world deployment. Using 25 subscribers per vBNG instance to avoid subscriber
numbers whose tests were limited by the hardware, one physical CPU core can
be shared between 3 vBNG instances with little CPU contention. This result
shows an average utilization of 18% per vCPU (the vBNG was configured with
two).
Using a modern Intel®Xeon®as an example with 30 CPU cores, and hosting
three vBNG instances per physical core, one CPU could host up to 90 instances
without having CPU contention, which would have a max throughput demand of
around 225 Gbps for the server. Up until the release of PCIe 5.0, a server would
struggle to cope with such high network demands, as one 200 Gbps card could

With these values, one rack server with one CPU socket and 180 GB of Random-
access memory ( RAM) could serve around 45.000 subscribers with 1:20 traffic

could be made to the PoC, such as the use of DPDK for network polling. It is
worth noting that some of the values assumed in this scenario could be different
when deploying the vBNG in modern hardware. The value of 25 subscribers
per vBNG instance could potentially be higher, which would result in better
hardware efficiency by having less vBNG instances to host the same number of
subscribers and throughput.
4.5 Integrating the vCPE and the vBNG
As more components of the operator infrastructure shift from dedicated and
specialized appliances – often locked to a specific vendor – to virtualized com-
ponents, operators can look at the traditional service delivery under a new light.
At some point in the future, we might have an infrastructure where the vCPE
(cf. Chapter 3) is joined with the vBNG. This would allow a close proximity
between both. The vCPE has been a topic of research for some time now, and
with the current push for the virtualization of the vBNG, this vision becomes
closer to reality.
Pushing this idea to the limit, vCPE and vBNG could be merged as one en-
tity providing a set of network services to a subscriber. Traditional scenarios
already have some connection between functions both perform, and some net-
work processing is done by both. An example is traffic shaping being a shared
— 96 —

4.5. INTEGRATING THE VCPE AND THE VBNG
responsibility, with the CPE enforcing it on the upstream traffic, and the down-
stream enforced by the BNG.
Such a scenario could bring advantages to an operator allowing increased flex-
ibility and easier management. The subscriber service quality could also benefit
from the closer proximity of these two services.
This section presents two potential scenarios which use different ways of in-
tegrating the vBNG and vCPE. Figure 4.12 presents a scenario where both
components are separated but closely deployed. This reflects the multi-tenancy
support expected from vBNG workers when dealing with large number of sub-
scribers and a limited set of subscription plans for them to choose.

It is worth mentioning that although these are presented separately, they are
expected to coexist in the same infrastructure. The steering function forwards
traffic to the vCPE independently of the subscriber type.
However, there are challenges for the deployment of this architecture. In an oper-
ator infrastructure, the systems supporting subscriber services are complex and
their initial development often comes from legacy systems. They have evolved
by incremental changes, and complete rebuild or changes is seldom seen in the
industry. It is expected for operators to resist major changes on their infra-
structure. Moreover, they usually have such a large number of subscribers that
a sudden change would be unfeasible financially and technically. As a result,
they must be compatible with OSS and BSS systems of the operator, allowing a
gradual migration by having the current and new architectures coexisting.
4.6 Summary
Following the vCPE concept presented in chapter 3, this chapter presented an-
other step in the virtualization of the operator’s infrastructure. The BNG is a
device located in the edge of the operator network connecting the subscribers to
the core network. When a subscriber gateways is turned on, it uses the BNG to
establish the connection with the operator and the Internet. Moreover, the BNG
performs a set of network functions essential to the service, including providing
— 98 —

4.6. SUMMARY
an IP address to the subscriber gateway, enforcing QoS for prioritizing sens-
itive services such as IPTV and VoIP, and applying traffic shaping to assure
that the subscription plans are maintained, and dealing with contention when
oversubscription occurs.
Over the years, several limitations of the traditional BNG model have been
increasingly pressuring operators, including the possibility of becoming a single
point of failure for subscriber connections, being a specialized appliance, often
resulting in vendor lock-in for upgrade or expansion purposes, and usually being
a monolithic appliance without modular capabilities. As a result, operators may
have to pay for all functionality even when only part of it is needed.
In recent years, a new approach for the BNG task has been under research:
moving it to a virtualized instance, where functions can be deployed in COTS
hardware with several potential advantages over the traditional model.
In this chapter, a vBNG was proposed and a matching PoC was presented. The
main goal of this PoC is to study the impact that virtualized network functions
can have on the performance and resource consumption of a software-based
BNG. The tests focused on the impact of applying traffic policies commonly
done by the BNG (more specifically, when applying traffic shaping, QoS, and
ACL).
Observed results showed similar performance and resource usage when compar-
ing the baseline service to having different configuration of traffic policies NF,
namely traffic shaping, ACL and QoS. Finally it was discussed how this vBNG
might scale out in a production environment to support several thousands of
subscribers in one CO. Based on the test results and on the assumptions made
in the tests, an extrapolation was made to calculate how many subscribers could
potentially be supported by one COTS rack server.
— 99 —
