CHAPTER 5. A SECURITY MONITORING FRAMEWORK FOR THE 5G
CORE
T
he SBA approach introduced in 5G allows core components to be de-
ployed as NFs in COTS, and use the Service-Based Interface ( SBI)
to expose the functionality of NFs, as specified in TS 29.500 ( ETSI
[2020]). As a result, operators become less prone to be locked into a specific
vendor and its proprietary hardware, while also opening the system to third-
party developers to create tailored services. This allows not only a greater
number of handheld devices but also to massively connect different types of
equipment, including IoT, M2M, and CPS. Compared with the previous gen-
eration of wireless communication networks, 5G provides a substantial increase
in data rates with improved QoS, exceptionally low latency, and high device
density (Ahmad et al. [2017]).
However, considering the application scenarios introduced by 5G networks and
the increase in types of services and connected devices (in variety and number),
the threat landscape has grown extensively for this fifth generation. Moreover,
the SBA concept also brought additional security risks for the telecommunica-
tions infrastructure itself. To address these problems, this chapter proposes a
security monitoring framework for improving the security of 5G networks (more
specifically, to secure components of the 5G core).
This work was carried out in cooperation with an industrial partner (OneSource),
within the scope of the M5G project. Moreover, it has been partially published
in Proença et al. [2021b].
5.1 Security of 5G networks
Each generation of wireless mobile networks had known security issues. The first
generation (1G) had challenges of interception and cloning due to its analogue
nature. The second generation (2G) became digital and brought some security
features provided by UE anonymity, authentication, signalling, and user data
protection. However, it had some security issues, such as only having one-
way authentication ( UE did not authenticate the operator), and was vulnerable
to message spamming, unwanted broadcast, and injection of false information
(Ahmad et al. [2017]; Ahmad et al. [2019]). Also, the encryption algorithms were
reverse engineered, resulting in the cipher algorithms being subjected to several
attacks ( Cattaneo et al. [2013]; Golić [1997]). Furthermore, it was not capable
of being upgraded to improve its security. The third generation (3G) brought
higher data rates and new services. IP-based communication started to be part
of the infrastructure as well. Although 3G has significantly improved its security
with the five sets of security features specified in TS33.102 ( ETSI [2000]), it still
had some noticeable flaws, such as IP-based vulnerabilities. With the arrival of
4G, IP communication expanded to more points of the mobile network, which
increased IP traffic-induced security risks. Ahmad et al. [2019] detail the main
security mechanisms and challenges of wireless technologies from 1G to 4G.
— 102 —

5.1. SECURITY OF 5G NETWORKS
5.1.1 The need to secure the 5G Core
The core components of the 5G infrastructure may have to deal with a high
number of threats and security concerns as traffic is expected to have a huge in-
crease compared with the previous generations Benseny et al. [2023]. Moreover
its not unusual for signalling traffic to have a higher increase than data traffic.
As a result, the signalling plane can be overwhelmed by a huge number of infec-
ted IoT devices in an attempt to disrupt service via a Denial of Service ( DoS)
attack or to gain unauthorized access ( Ahmad et al. [2019]; Remy Harel [2016]).
As SDN and NFV are expected to be used in the core network to provide flex-
ibility and scalability of services, they also may compromise security if not well
addressed Giambartolomei et al. [2024].
5.1.2 Security Architecture of 5G Networks
Security challenges in 5G have a broad spectrum, as service diversity significantly
widened compared to previous generations, along with changes in the network
architecture. The 5G specification was designed to tackle these new challenges.
Virtualization is expected to be widely adopted (with the introduction of SBA),
as well as multi-tenancy support, which can bring new security issues if not
addressed carefully. Ahmad et al. [2019] highlight that technologies such as
SDN, NFV, MEC, and Massive Multiple Input Multiple Output ( MIMO) can
pose security risks if not used carefully. The NGMN Alliance [2015] lists re-
commendations for 5G, and points out some limitations ( Liyanage et al. [2018]):
flash network traffic, security of radio interface keys, user plane integrity, man-
dated network security, consistency in subscriber level security policies, and DoS
attacks on the infrastructure.
The security in 5G is based on a vision with three main principles: supreme
built-in security, flexible security mechanisms, and automation ( Liyanage et al.
[2018]). Furthermore, it follows a security architecture as defined by 3GPP
[2020] to provide a systematic approach, with six main domains: (1) Network
Access Security, (2) Network Domain Security, (3) User Domain Security, (4)
Application Domain Security, (5) Service-Based Architecture Domain Security,
and (6) Visibility and Configurability Security.
5.1.3 Current Research
The security aspects of 5G have been getting the attention of the research com-
munity. As a general overview, Cao et al. [2020] discuss security aspects of 5G
networks related to the UE access and handover methods, including security
in IoT, Device-to-Device ( D2D), Vehicle-to-everything ( V2X), and network sli-
cing. The authors also list possible solutions for each use case and discusses
some open issues for future research. In this generation, the authentication
between UE and the network uses the evolved packet system-based authentica-
tion and key agreement (EPS-AKA) introduced in Long Term Evolution ( LTE)
because although it introduces some communication overhead and latency, it
does not have any known security vulnerabilities so far. A threat model classi-
— 103 —

CHAPTER 5. A SECURITY MONITORING FRAMEWORK FOR THE 5G
CORE
fying attacks in the 4G and 5G networks was proposed by Ferrag et al. [2018],
discussing the existing surveys for 4G and 5G wireless mobile networks security.
Authors classify the attacks into four categories: privacy, integrity, availability,
and authentication.
The security of 5G networks will also depend on the options that are chosen
for each implementation. A considerably wide range of possibilities in the spe-
cification allows configuration options that may leave the network with security
breaches that increase the network’s vulnerability to attacks, as noted by Jover
and Member [2019]. For example, null encryption and null authentication are
still supported, and valid network configurations might use them. Another ex-
ample is in function chain configuration, as described by Li et al. [2018], where
a rule management framework is proposed to implement security rule config-
uration with anomaly resolution. There are also mechanisms that may only
be useful with global cooperation, which is unlikely to happen. This coopera-
tion would be needed to guarantee security against pre-authentication message
exploits, and each Universal Subscriber Identity Module ( USIM) would have
to include the public keys from all the network operators available worldwide
without exception, which would be extremely difficult (if not impossible) to
achieve.
The security of the 5G infrastructure has been the subject of research in the
academic community from the beginning. To address the security of the SDN-
based network use in 5G, Luo et al. [2015] proposed a vulnerability assessment
for SDN-based mobile network using attack graph algorithms and the Analytic
Hierarchy Process ( AHP). A security architecture for 5G is proposed by Arfaoui
et al. [2018], following the security architecture of X.805 from ITU-T [2003]. It
is presented as a methodology for secure instantiation of systems based on (1)
domain separation, (2) classification of strata through a high-level view of pro-
tocols, data, and functions that are related to each other in a common threat
environment, (3) security realms, and (4) security control classes. Another se-
curity architecture for 5G networks, proposed by Fang et al. [2018], was tested
in scenarios of handover procedure and signalling load analysis. They point out
four security domains defined in the 3rd Generation Partnership Project ( 3GPP)
security architecture for 5G networks: (1) network access security, (2) network
domain security, (3) user domain security, and (4) application domain secur-
ity.
Deployment and configuration of the security services can also determine the
overall security of a system. The 5G architecture can be composed by chaining
different functions together. This may also apply to its security systems. Rules
configuring a security system can have an impact on security if the rules are
misconfigured. Different systems can have different rules, and chaining compon-
ents with different rules will increase this risk. Li et al. [2018] proposed a rule
management framework to reduce the risk of rule configuration in a security
function chain environment, which will, in turn, minimize security breaches in
security architecture.
Using a 5G-based vehicular networking environment, a software-defined dynamic
— 104 —

5.2. SECURITY MONITORING COMPONENTS
security defence is proposed by Xu et al. [2019] for 5G Software-Defined Vehicu-
lar Network ( SDVN). This proposal dynamically generates and deploys security
strategies in a software-defined way after evaluating the security level of the cur-
rent system. The security is evaluated following three aspects of information se-
curity: confidentiality, integrity, and availability. A mathematical model is used
to assess security using a hybrid decision-making model based on fuzzy AHP
and Technique for Order Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution ( TOPSIS).
The dynamic security policies are then generated and applied using SDN.
5.2 Security Monitoring Components
The security components proposed in this section are part of the security frame-
work developed within the M5G project, and focus on the detection of security
attacks targetting 5G core elements. Specifically, two distinct components are
proposed: an application intrusion detection and prevention system (AppIDPS);
and an Event Processing and Visualization Platform ( EPVP) composed by an
Event Collector and a Dashboard. For validation, the Network Exposure Func-
tion ( NEF) from a typical 5G core is used as an example. Figure 5.1 illus-
trates the main components of a typical 5G network plus the main elements
of these components (AppIDPS and Event Collector). In this scenario, the
AppIDPS analyses the requests and responses of the NEF to detect unwanted
behaviour. Any suspicious activity detected by the AppIDPS will trigger a se-
curity event that is further processed by the EPVP and displayed to security
personnel through a dashboard.

(adapted and extended from ETSI [2020]).
5.2.1 Architecture
These components, as illustrated in Figure 5.2, represent a subset of the complete
security architecture developed within the context of the M5G project. The
NEF will act as the core component to protect (for the sake of demonstration
purposes, since other NFs could also benefit from protection), with the AppIDPS
monitoring its activity. For contextual understanding, Figure 5.2 also includes
— 105 —

CHAPTER 5. A SECURITY MONITORING FRAMEWORK FOR THE 5G
CORE
other probes developed in the context of the M5G project, including DNS, IDPS,
Honeynet, and V AS, which can send events to the EPVP. However, it should be
noted that these probes fall outside the scope of this document and, therefore,
will not be described in detail.

5G Core Probes
Event Collector Dashboard
Figure 5.2 : M5G security framework high-level architecture.
5.2.2 Event Processing and Visualization Platform
The EPVP receives security events from several 5G core nodes and presents them
to human operators in a simple, easy-to-understand web interface. Moreover,
the information can be sent from probes that collect relevant data from 5G core
components. Then, the EPVP is tasked with receiving, processing, logging, and
presenting the processed security event data and respective node metrics to the
network manager. It follows a microservice-oriented architecture and is built
upon three main components/microservices: a Kafka cluster, which takes care
of event forwarding and centralization; a Middleware, where pre-processing and
data treatment functions are applied, together with some calculations of node
specific data; and a Web UI (Dashboard), necessary for outputting the treated
information to the security operator. The deployment of the EPVP is done
using Docker-based containers, which simplify the management and deployment
processes. Docker1 was also chosen with scalability in mind, complementing the
easily scalable Kafka services.
The architecture (Figure 5.3) was designed for optimal event processing. By sep-
arating the web interface and data processing into different components, we can
achieve higher throughput numbers and better overall performance, effectively
separating the workload without compromising data integrity, while keeping all
data relevant and up-to-date. There is also a datastore, which was created
specifically to store older events, directly reducing the resource usage at the
Middleware. Those events can then be looked up by the user at the web inter-
face.
The events received are transmitted in a JavaScript Object Notation ( JSON)
format, using a common data model that specifies message structure and variable
limits. It has some generic fields that are common to all components, and
metadata fields that are extensible to allow it to be custom fitted.
5.2.3 AppIDPS
The AppIDPS was developed as an extra layer of security at the application level.
It analyzes information from the selected secured components and the requests

The internal architecture of the AppIDPS includes diverse elements: (1) scal-
able component to gather information from Network Functions and Application
Function that is being monitored; (2) correlation component to perform the se-

flaws and attacks using the information provided; (3) security server compon-

and performs the integration with external dashboards; (4) real-time database
components that are responsible for providing the information in real-time to
support the security analysis of the correlation components. The internal view is

Component Description
Policy Enforcer Applies policies in response to certain events and keeps a record
of applied policies in its internal database.
MQTT MQTT is an ISO standard publish-subcribe based messaging pro-
tocol. This component is the broker for messages, allowing receiv-

and retrieval of time series data. It stores large quantities of mon-
itoring data from the Telegraph.
Kapacitor Data processing engine to process data from InfluxDB with cus-
tom logic or user defined functions to process alerts with dynamic
load balancing.
Dashboard Allows human interaction with the system, and the definition of
rules and policies. It also handles situations that the automatic
system cannot handle by itself.
SecurityDB Database with ACLs to provide access control to MQTT server
and its topics. It also includes AAA for the MQTT server and the
Dashboard.
5.2.3.2 Metrics and Policy Messages
Probes send metrics to AppIDPS and receive policies whenever actions take
place. Messages are sent using JSON and the Message Queuing Telemetry
Transport (MQTT) protocol. The information that is sent for analysis includes
diverse items and contains both a common header and a body with app-specific
information. The fields in the common header are the source IP address of the
application client, access timestamp, network function ID, and network function
type (according to 3GPP specifications). As for policies, the body format is also
defined by each app.
— 108 —

5.3. V ALIDATION
5.2.4 Network Exposure Function
The NEF is a 5G standard component that exposes an API of network services
and capabilities in a developer-friendly manner. As already mentioned, NEF
was selected as an example of a sensitive NF potentially benefiting from the
proposed security framework. The NEF used in the validation work described
next was developed in Python following the 3GPP OpenAPI specifications. The
internal components of NEF include (1) the API worker itself, running as a
stateless RESTful service that can be easily scaled out/in; (2) the load balan-
cer that sits in front of the API and has a dual purpose of distributing load
among existing API workers and blocking requests from malicious entities (this
reflects the existing integration with AppIDPS, which receives information from
a security probe located in the NEF that is analyzing all the requests); (3) a
database containing all the exchanges and subscriptions performed by each NEF
client (application function). Scaling policies are based on an external orches-
trator based on metrics and performance indicators gathered by a NEF internal
monitoring agent. Scaling out is done by launching additional API workers and
notifying the load balancer.
5.3 Validation
This subsection describes the tests made to the EPVP, AppIDPS, and NEF, as
well as a global integration scenario.
5.3.1 Integration Scenario
An integration scenario was designed and implemented as described next. The
application scenario uses a commercial device called BodyKit, which interacts
with the 5G infrastructure to change network parameters such as delivery pri-
ority. The BodyKit is a wearable device to be worn in emergency scenarios
by emergency personnel such as firefighters or the police. It enhances current
Command and Control Center capabilities by providing field data that increases
situational awareness for emergency response situations, including advanced 2-
way voice, video communications, and a large array of data collected from en-
vironmental (e.g., gas, smoke, temperature, humidity, location) and biosensors
(e.g., electrocardiogram, respiration rate, blood pressure, heart rate, body tem-
perature). It also has automated alarms that include the detection of man-down
events and the presence of adverse and dangerous atmospheric environmental
conditions such as high temperatures and toxic levels of gases. All the inform-
ation sent to the 5G network is prioritized according to its QoS profile. In
order to accomplish that, the BodyKit server interacts with the 5G Core via
NEF, requesting the desired QoS profile for each flow at the Policy Control
Function.
In this scenario, the BodyKit is being used and requests a change through the
NEF component. At some point, an attacker interacts with the system, sending
unauthorized requests to the NEF, which could compromise the actions of the
BodyKit by changing network parameters or triggering a DoS by overloading the
— 109 —

CHAPTER 5. A SECURITY MONITORING FRAMEWORK FOR THE 5G
CORE
NEF component. The requests targeting the NEF are analyzed by the AppIDPS
and an anomalous NEF request pattern is detected. In response, the AppIDPS
sends a security event to the EPVP, which is displayed in a dashboard to be
analyzed by a security employee that acts accordingly.
Testbed
The integration scenario is set up in a testbed and the services are deployed
in VMs, with their interactions following the diagram illustrated in Figure 5.6.

CHAPTER 5. A SECURITY MONITORING FRAMEWORK FOR THE 5G

5.3.2 Event Processing and Visualization Platform Tests
Considering the EPVP architecture previously described, there is a key compon-
ent that needs to be tested, given the possibility of bottleneck of the incoming
data: the Middleware. As for the Kafka Broker(s), its performance has been
already studied in the academic community ( Le Noac’h et al. [2017]), so it will
not be tested individually. The tests conducted on the Middleware include the
average processing time of an event, as well as integrity testing to verify its ro-
bustness when dealing with invalid or wrongful data. All tests were conducted
on a VMWare Virtual Machine with 4 vCPUs – Intel Xeon X5660 @ 2.80GHz,
16 GB of DDR RAM, and 40 GB of Solid State Drive ( SSD) storage.
The Middleware and Dashboard components work together to funnel the data
consumed from the Kafka Brokers into easy-to-understand visual elements for
the end user’s interpretation of the 5G core network security behaviour over
time. While the Middleware collects and treats the incoming events, there is a
limit to how much data it can store at a given time. The data is kept in memory
to be easily and quickly accessed by the WebUI via WebSocket. However, with
increasing amounts of data, the number of events to be kept in memory without
affecting performance had to be studied, allowing the optimization of the Dash-
board user experience without consuming excessive resources.
The Middleware recalculates metrics that consider the whole set of in memory
events when a new event arrives. The tests regarding the average event pro-
cessing time at the Middleware had the goal of estimating the maximum number
of events the Middleware should keep at any given time without noticeably af-
fecting processing speed when recalculating metrics. Once that limit is reached,
the Middleware starts discarding the older events. Since the data is kept in array
data structures, to be easily accessed by the Dashboard, larger amounts of data
directly correlate to slower array cycle times. These tests were run under differ-
ent values (10 000, 25 000, 50 000, 75 000, and 100 000) for the maximum number
of events kept at a time (Middleware buffer), and processing time samples were
then measured (see Figure 5.8) and compared. Events were generated randomly,
using a synthetic event generator, effectively simulating a live event feed, while
imposing the different buffer capacities listed previously. Each set was repeated
three times for each buffer capacity value. Since NodeJS (the programming lan-
guage this component is based on) is inherently single-threaded, the buffer is
filled in a sequential order, with events arriving at the Middleware via a Kafka
Consumer module, which directly consumes events from the Kafka Broker. The
event is then processed and sent to the Dashboard along with the necessary
visual data corresponding to that event. A copy of the event is also stored in
memory at the Middleware.

5.3. V ALIDATION
The results for the capacity test show increases in average processing time (Fig-
ure 5.9) by up to 16 times with event buffer sizes above fifty thousand events.
Increasing the buffer size translates into a rapid growth in average event pro-
cessing time, which adds overhead to the system for each incoming event. This
overhead is partially explained by the limitations of NodeJS itself (mainly the
lack of multi-thread support – which means there is no parallel event processing),
and also by the size of the buffer array, in which operations requiring iteration
throughout the array become significantly slower at larger sizes. Therefore,
based on the results obtained, it is advised to keep the buffer size less than or
equal to seventy-five thousand entries to ensure optimal Middleware event pro-
cessing time. This test helped find a good ratio between how much data should
be kept in the Middleware and the time it takes to process an event. However,
it is also important to note that a solution for the problem was implemented: a
side application to store/retrieve older events to/from disk, for the sake of data
logging and to look up old events via the Dashboard itself, solving the problem
of keeping all data in memory. This way, the Middleware can keep relevant new
data and all the metrics obtained from it in memory, while still providing a way
for the user to look up older events.
Figure 5.9 : Effective processing time test for each “buffer” capacity threshold
proposed.
Throughput tests were also performed, with Figure 5.10 and Figure 5.11 illus-
trating tests results from the Middleware and Stream Apps, to test how many
events per minute the PoC can support.
The results of the Middleware (Figure 5.10) show significant increase of pro-
cessing time as the number of events per minute rises. This is a result of the
buffer becoming filled with events. In the scenario with 100k events per minute,
the middleware proved incapable of handling such a volume, processing only
16k events. This test aimed to assess the limit of the PoC. Nonetheless, the
processing capabilities that the middleware is capable of were in line with the
requirements of the M5G project.
The Kafka Streams App is responsible for event windowing, metric processing,
and feed the results to another Kafka topic. To ensure that the limitations
observed in the Middleware (as shown in Figure 5.10) were not caused by the
Streams App’s processing, throughput tests were conducted. Figure 5.11 demon-
strates that the Streams App not only successfully processed all events in the
— 113 —

CHAPTER 5. A SECURITY MONITORING FRAMEWORK FOR THE 5G
CORE
Figure 5.10 : Middleware throughput – A verage event processing time for three
different sets, over the period of one minute.
Figure 5.11 : StreamApps throughput – A verage event processing time for three
different sets, over the period of one minute.
100k scenario but also exhibited low average processing time. These results
indicate that the Streams App did not pose a bottleneck in terms of event pro-
cessing throughput.
Robustness Middleware tests were also made. The tests were split into two
categories: (1) message structure – validates the integrity of the JSON tuple
message and its structure, as well as presence of the mandatory fields and (2)
message contents – validates the JSON message fields. All events tested were
correctly identified as valid or invalid.
5.4 Summary
This chapter proposed a set of solutions to improve the security of 5G core
components using the 5G NEF as an example. A testbed was created, and
the NEF was the representative target of attacks that were detected by an Ap-

pIDPS, generating security events that were processed and shown to a security
operator. Future work will address performance and integration tests, extend-

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
T
he previous chapters described three complementing applications of
virtualization concepts to the telecommunications network infrastruc-
ture.
This chapter discusses how those virtualization concepts can also be used to
improve other application domains. More specifically, it describes how a security
management framework has been evolved to take full advantage of virtualization
concepts.
This security management framework, developed in the scope of the ATENA
H2020-DS-2015-1 project, was designed and demonstrated in the field of Critical
Infrastructures Protection ( CIP) and complex Industrial Automation and Con-
trol Systems ( IACS). Nevertheless, it can also be useful in more generic scen-
arios.
This chapter starts by providing an introduction to CI and IACS, as background
to the application scenario. Next, it presents a security monitoring framework
designed to detect cyber-attacks in the scope of Critical Infrastructures Pro-
tection, considering its use in virtualized scenarios. Section 6.3 presents two
proposals of specialized security probes that feed the intrusion and anomaly
detection components. The work presented in this chapter has been partially
published in Cruz et al. [2014a]; Cruz et al. [2014b]; Cruz et al. [2015a]; Proença
et al. [2015]; Cruz et al. [2014c]; Simões et al. [2015]; Cruz et al. [2016a]; Cruz
et al. [2016b]; Rosa et al. [2017]; and Adamsky et al. [2018].
6.1 Understanding IACS Environments
This section provides an overview of IACS environments and addresses chal-
lenges of securing these systems. Critical Infrastructuress are an essential part
of modern life, as they support several sectors of society, including utilities re-
sponsible for energy grids, water treatment and distribution, industrial sites,
transportation systems, and telecommunications infrastructures. Being essen-
tial, maintaining their security and safety is crucial. Another traditional charac-
teristic of CIs is the interconnectivity and inter-dependability between them. As
a result, an incident affecting one specific CI can lead to a cascade of events im-
pacting other (interdependent) infrastructures. As an example, a catastrophic
failure of the energy grid could disable telecommunications networks, as sev-
eral of its components are energy-dependent. On the other hand, a failure
in telecommunications networks can cause problems on the energy grid, as it
requires communication between different sites for grid management and con-
trol purposes. Although the most critical dependabilities may have redundancy
strategies to mitigate some incidents (for example, a hospital being powered by
more than one energy grid segment, and having generators for part of its load)
they will never be completely safe.
— 118 —

6.1. UNDERSTANDING IACS ENVIRONMENTS
In the CI realm, multiple designations are used to define common control tech-
nologies, protocols and systems that control the industrial processes associated
with the CI: Supervisory Control And Data Acquisition ( SCADA), Operations
Network ( OT), Control Systems, and Industrial Automation and Control Sys-
tems ( IACS). Although some details change between the different nomen-
clatures, the main functions are similar. These are used in several scenarios
(not exclusive to CI), including automation of production lines, control of power
plants (nuclear, thermoelectric, wind farms), management of distribution grids
(electricity, gas, oil, water), control of sewage treatment facilities, transportation
and medical systems.
Initially, IACS were restricted to isolated environments, and were considered safe
from intrusion by external actors. These old systems were considerably simple
by nature, mostly because there were no formal data processing or memory
mechanisms involved, being little more than reactive systems interconnecting
sensors and visual indicators. However, that simplicity was also their main
drawback, being unfeasible for use in anything other than small-scale and phys-
ically limited scenarios. Also, since data logging was impossible, and error or
failure-debugging capabilities were very limited.
Over time, IACS gradually evolved to their present situation, with the use of
specialized control devices, more evolved data processing and networking techno-
logies, and using distributed topologies across several physical sites. However,
some of their components, such as Programmable Logic Controllers ( PLCs),
have a lifecycle that frequently spans several decades and it is common to find
devices based on architectures with 20 or more years still in use. This longevity
has to do with maturity – a feature favoured in critical systems, traditionally
associated with reliability. This greatly contrasts with the ICT domain, where
frequent hardware updates are the norm to increase security and take advant-
ages of higher performance and improved efficiency of new hardware architec-
tures.
Communication and interoperability compatibility is one area where IACS have
evolved considerably. While original systems were isolated and self-contained by
nature, they were progressively opened to the exterior world, making use of data
communication networks for its own internal purposes and to share information
with the outside world or even other systems. These connections might exist
for various reasons: with the general-purpose corporate LAN, to exchange in-
formation with performance auditing or stock management applications; a Wide
Area Network ( W AN) connection to reach other facilities (for instance, two geo-
graphically separated sites) or to a remote operations control centre. Such W AN
connections might be ensured using leased lines, dial-up, or the Internet itself
(Ten et al. [2008]; Davis et al. [2006]). Additionally, it is frequent for original
device manufactures to provide remote assistance using such mechanisms, which
needs connection to the outside. Moreover, proprietary equipment and proto-
cols have traditionally been the norm on older IACS infrastructures, limiting
interoperability between devices from different manufacturers (and sometimes,
albeit less frequently, between different models of the same manufacturer). As
a result, this often creates a situation of vendor lock-in, forcing customers to
— 119 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
remain attached to a specific device family from a particular manufacturer due
to excessively high migration costs. More recently, equipment and protocols
have been increasingly standardized, and COTS hardware has been used when
possible-for example for LAN communication or to host Human-Machine Inter-
face ( HMI) software ( Igure et al. [2006]).
The introduction of data processing capabilities in IACS, and the evolution of
embedded systems, lead to the use of more commonly available OSs. Contrast-
ing to the use of proprietary systems in early infrastructures, they evolved to the
point where Windows or Unix derivatives ( Creery and Byres [2005]) were gradu-
ally introduced, together with real-time operating systems such as VXWorks or
Real-Time Linux ( Davis et al. [2006]).
This evolution brought significant benefits to IACS management and operation,
relatively to their functionality, rationality and cost. However, it also highlighted
vulnerabilities and security problems that have been hidden by the traditional
obscurity and isolation practices, so common in early systems, as they became
more and more exposed to increasingly open environments. The progressive
move to more open scenarios, together with the use of ICT and the increasing
adoption of open/documented protocols, exposed serious security weaknesses.
Moreover, the growing trend towards the interconnection of the IACS network
with organizational ICT network infrastructures, and even with outside net-
works (for instance, for connection with internal company systems or for remote
management by external contractors) created a new wave of security problems
and incidents. This resulted in an increase in the number of externally initiated
attacks on IACS systems, compared to internal ones ( Kang et al. [2011]). As
a result, the old practice of security by obscurity became unfeasible. Still, the
problem of security in IACS has been more or less ignored for several years, and
even now serious issues persist. Insecure protocols such as Modbus 1 are still
used in production systems.
Moreover, as pointed by Clarke et al. [2003], features such as the auto-
configuration capabilities of certain equipment (plug-and-play) further exposed
them to be exploited, as attackers found it to be a valuable resource for attack
planning and execution. Nonetheless, the old-school security mind-set persisted,
with some process managers assuming that IACS systems were isolated and im-
plicitly secure as they initially were ( Krutz [2005]), disregarding the need for
regular security updates or software patching procedures – and thus increasing
the probability of successful attacks.
While such procedures are trivial matters that are part of the regular mainten-
ance routine in the ICT world, they must be dealt in a different way when it
comes to IACS for two main reasons:
• Several works, such as Fovino et al. [2010] and Zhu et al. [2011], identified
a significant number of IACS components that must work on a continuous
basis, having no toleration to interruptions, up to the point of working
several years without being reinitialized. Stopping or reinitializing these
1https://www.modbus.org/specs.php
— 120 —

6.2. Intrusion and Anomaly Detection System
components, even for short periods of time and planned maintenance win-
dows, may provoke a considerable cost in the industrial processes they
manage.
• Equipment manufacturers must carefully and extensively test any software
release before formal acceptance for usage on IACS platforms. Addition-
ally, components still in use on IACS platforms often reach end-of-life sup-
port for specific devices or software frameworks. Even the OS on which
they are hosted may be discontinued and without active support or secur-
ity updates. To make things worst, components of the IACS software may
be not certified to be installed on a newer OS version.
Overall, when it comes to IACS, technology and platform maturity are valued as
an implicit recognition of value and reliability, and even the disclosure of security
issues related to them seems to have had mostly no effect in discouraging their
usage or prompting the adoption of countermeasures to protect them. This has
become the root cause of many IACS security issues that have been exploited
with a variable degree of success, such as the seminal Stuxnet Trojan ( Falliere
et al. [2011]).
The next Section presents the security monitoring framework designed to im-
prove the security of IACS in the scope of the ATENA Project.
6.2 Intrusion and Anomaly Detection System
The ATENA2 H2020 project aimed at protecting and increasing the resilience
of interconnected CIs against cyber-physical attacks. It encompassed several
dimensions, with the work presented in this section being focused on detection
of unwanted and malicious activities.
This project follows upon Project CockpitCI 3, which had as goal the develop-
ment os a security platform for interdependent CIs. The ATENA project evolved
previous research, by mitigating security issues in CI interdependencies, and
evolving a domain-specific Intrusion Detection System ( IDS) to a distributed
and virtualized architecture – the so-called Intrusion and Anomaly Detection
System.
The work presented in this thesis contributed to the virtualization infrastructure
that supports the IADS, to the collective design of the IADS architecture, and
also to the development of two domain-specific probes: the IACS honeypot and
the Shadow Security Unit ( SSU).
6.2.1 High-Level Architecture
As part of the ATENA project, the IADS collects and processes monitoring
data to detect cyber-attacks in the specific-domain of IACS. It is based on the
Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) paradigm, which combines
2https://cordis.europa.eu/project/id/700581
3https://cordis.europa.eu/project/id/285647
— 121 —

information and event management. This approach became popular after the
rise of high-impact and advanced threats such as Stuxnet( Falliere et al. [2011];
Chen and Abu-Nimeh [2011]), WannaCry Hsiao and Kao [2018], and Flame
Moubarak et al. [2017].
The IADS continuously monitors the protected infrastructure to detect anomal-
ous activity and behaviour. Its high-level architecture is presented in Figure 6.1,
and its components can be divided into three main layers (from bottom to top):
(1) the probes to collect monitoring data; (2) the domain processors to pre-
process information from the probes to reduce noise and aggregate events before
their analysis; (3) and the distributed big data SIEM, to implement analytics
(Adamsky et al. [2018]).

6.2.2 Distributed Monitoring
Systems such as IACS have been increasingly deployed in geographically dis-
tributed locations. As a result, several branches of the same infrastructure are
present in physically separated sites. Considering this, the monitoring systems
should keep up with the infrastructure. However, this does not mean that all
components of the security monitoring should be deployed in all sites. Rather,
as they are all part of the same infrastructure, event processing should take a
broad perspective into consideration under the scope of a logically centralized
entity with a distributed nature.
This detection architecture has evolved to better cope with the increase of
geographically distributed deployments of the systems it monitors. Figure 6.2
presents the IADS architecture adapted to a distributed deployment, being sep-
arated into local event collection and first processing stage (to be deployed near
the protected CI, at the edge), and a centralized SIEM (to be deployed centrally,
— 122 —

6.2. Intrusion and Anomaly Detection System
at the core). Also, this architecture can be lined-up with ETSI’s MEC (cf. Sec-
tion 4.3.3), with the Domain Processing deployed at the operator’s edge and the
big-data SIEM deployed in the infrastructure core. This approach potentially
results in a standardized deployment, with the benefit of local processing being
placed near the IACS site. Moreover, this also means that the domain-specific
processing can scale according to monitored infrastructure.

Given the distributed nature of the IADS, the whole architecture becomes vir-
tualizable, as it can be seen further in this chapter. Next, the main components
of the IADS architecture are described.
Probes
The probes make the lowest layer of this architecture. They are the main re-
sponsible for the detection capabilities by collecting the data related to suspi-
cious events on the infrastructure, to be used by the other components on the
layers above. The architecture allows these probes to be deployed as virtualized
instances as explored, for instance, by Freitas et al. [2020].
Different types of probes are expected to feed the IADS. They include network,
— 123 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
device and host security agents, but also more specific probes such as the Shadow
Security Unit (SSU) and the IACS honeypot. To simplify integration, the probes
use a uniform data model. For legacy probes from third-party data sources,
adaptors are used for integration with the IADS framework. The main purpose
of the adaptors is to parse the data and logs of the third-party security probes.
The IADS probes can be categorized into three main groups:
• Statistical Protocol Probes – they gather several statistical attributes and
send them to the domain processor. These can be used to identify network
protocols ( Köhnen et al. [2010], Hjelmvik and John [2009]). This analysis
uses specific attributes to define a flow fingerprint, distinguishing between
compressed, encrypted, or clear-text protocols.
• SDN-assisted probes – the framework takes advantage of SDN principles
to ease the deployment of virtualized probes, which can also be referred
to as VNFs. Being deployed as VNF improves the flexibility and reduces
the security operator effort to instantiate and deploy probes across the
network infrastructure.
• Network Signature Agents – these probes combine the advantages of
signature-based detection with the advantages of machine learning de-
tection from the domain processor. A signature-based IDS is used as a
stand-alone agent. Its security signatures are updated from the IADS,
and the events are sent to the data streaming platform.
Domain Processors
The middle layer of the architecture is composed by the domain processors,
which act as pre-processors for the events collected from the security probes.
The main task of the domain processors is not to analyse data, but to prepare it
for the big data SIEM. To achieve this, they reduce unnecessary data transmit-
ted on the network, reducing streaming noise. Example tasks are aggregation,
and removal of duplicated events. To maximize the benefits of streaming noise
reduction, the domain processors should be ideally deployed near the probe de-
ployment points.
Big Data SIEM
The big data SIEM performs the main analysis capabilities for the IADS, us-
ing two types of data processing methods: streaming (also referred to as fast
path), and batch processing (slow path, for analysis that can take more time to
complete, using data from a larger time windows).
The following sections present probes that have been developed by the author of
this Thesis. Two examples are presented: the IACS honeypot for IACS/SCADA,
and the SSU, capable of stealthily monitoring a PLC to detect abnormal or
malicious behaviour.
— 124 —

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
6.3 Domain-Specific Probes
This Section presents two domain-specific probes designed in the scope of the
IADS: the IACS honeypot and the SSU. This Section also discusses how these
probes can be integrated within a virtualized environment, taking full advantages
of its benefits.
6.3.1 IACS Honeypot
Honeypots are a well-known method of defence in traditional ICT environments.
By definition, a honeypot is a decoy or dummy target set up to attract and detect
or observe attacks. By being exposed to probing and attack, its purpose is to
lure and track intruders as they advance. Deploying and running a honeypot
infrastructure requires a careful approach: it has to be planned in advance so
that the infrastructure itself cannot be used to increase the attack surface, while
keeping a low profile.
In general honeypots can be classified in two groups: research honeypots and
production honeypots. Research honeypots are used to obtain intelligence in-
formation about attack methods, usually in generalist scenarios, while produc-
tion honeypots are used to explicitly protect an ICT infrastructure by providing
advance warning of attacks against its production components. Honeypots can
also be distinguished by the ability of the attacker to interact with the applica-
tion or services ( Sanders [2020]; Spitzner [2002]):
• High-interaction honeypots can be probed, attacked and compromised.
These allow the attacker to interact with the system to capture the max-
imum amount of information regarding his/her intrusion and exploitation
techniques. As a result, high-interaction honeypots have no restrictions
regarding what the hacker can do once the system is compromised. Be-
cause of this they need to be closely monitored, and carefully deployed as
a compromised honeypot could be used to attack the infrastructure. Hon-
eyIoT ( Guan et al. [2023]) is an example of a high-interaction honeypot
for IoT.
• Low-interaction honeypots emulate vulnerabilities rather than exposing
real weaknesses. This naturally restricts the range of interaction the at-
tacker has (Provos [2004]). Mainly used as decoys to detect the presence of
an attacker, they are also less flexible. On the other hand, they are more
secure to the whole infrastructure, as there is considerably less possibilit-
ies of an attacker compromise it and exploit the production infrastructure.
Honeyd4, honeydoc 5 and Nephentes ( Baecher et al. [2006]) are some ex-
amples of low interaction honeypots.
Finally, honeypots may be classified as server honeypots or client honey-
pots:
4https://www.honeyd.org/
5https://github.com/jqreator/honeydoc
— 125 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
• Server honeypots are designed to passively wait for attacks, to be dis-
covered and probed by attackers. Example of server honeypots are Galah 6
and Trapster Community 7
• Client honeypots can actively search for malicious servers and behave
as victims, a useful feature for detecting client-side browser exploits
(Qassrawi and Zhang [2010]). Examples of client honeypots are Shelia 8
and Honeyspider 9.
All these honeypot categories have been identified based on a perspective that,
whilst generalist, is mostly based on the ICT field. When applied to the specific
context of IACS, it is also necessary to consider the operation scope intended
for the honeypot, since in addition to the ICT domain, there is also the IACS
components in the OT, and different scenarios require specific approaches and
technologies.
A simplified IACS follows the Purdue Model ( Williams [1993]) and is aligned
with ISA-99 10 and IEC-62443 11, which has been recognized as an horizontal
standard in 2021. A IACS often entails tree levels: the first two are part of the
OT (including supervisory components, such as master stations or HMI devices
and historic process databases); and the field networks (where basic control and
process devices, such PLC are deployed), controlling and monitoring a critical
process.

Apart from the first two levels, which are specific to process control, there is

management).

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
In the OT, for instance, a low-interaction honeypot might simulate the operation
of a network server (e.g., SCADA control station). In the field network a honey-
pot may be implemented using a system capable of simulating the operation of
a PLC (e.g., a SCADA protocol emulator). Finally, in the ICT network, high-
interaction honeypots might be adequate (even in the form of virtual machines,
co-located on a same host), as well as low-interaction honeypots simulating min-
imal services. Moreover, in certain circumstances, some attacks targeting the
system can be redirected to the honeypot, therefore providing more information
about the attacker and his intentions.
Few alternatives exist for a IACS-specific honeypot. Three initiatives stand out
as example implementations: the SCADA Honeynet Project ( Cisco [2004]) and
the Digital Bond’s SCADA Honeynets 12, and conpot 13.
The SCADA HoneyNet Project aimed at creating a framework to simulate indus-
trial networks. It started circa 2004 (although it is no longer maintained), lead
by the Cisco Critical Infrastructure Assurance Group, and is able to simulate
several levels of the system:
• Stack level – for simulation of the TCP/IP stack of a device.
• Protocol level – for simulation of specific industrial protocols, such as
Modbus, and Ethernet/IP.
• Application level – for simulation several SCADA applications, such as
web services and management consoles.
• Hardware – for simulation of serial ports and modems present in some
SCADA devices.
Digital Bond provided another approach to SCADA honeynets (or honeypot
networks). This solution used two or more hosts: one to monitor the network
activity (in this specific case using a Generation III Honeywall) and the other
to simulate a PLC with several services made available to the attacker (Mod-
bus/TCP, FTP, Telnet, HTTP and SNMP).
The approach detailed in this section distinguishes itself from those two initiat-
ives by presenting a low cost, modular and highly configurable and manageable
solution for an automation/field network honeypot. By bringing down the costs
of honeypots and providing a flexible and modular approach to its deployment,
we aim at large-scale deployment of honeypots in IACS process control net-
works, turning them into commodity devices. Furthermore, by improving their
manageability, we aim at effectively integrating them into the security manage-
ment platforms already in place. The honeypot is part of a broader platform
that encompasses a distributed probe system for cyber attack detection in IACS
environments, but could be easily integrated into most existing security man-
agement platforms.
Finally, conpot is a low interaction honeypot, capable of simulating and indus-
12https://github.com/digitalbond
13http://conpot.org/
— 127 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
trial devices. This is a parallel and contemporary effort to the IACS honeypot
proposed in this section, with some similarities.
6.3.1.1 Honeypot Architecture
The honeypot here presented was designed to behave and operate as a PLC, co-
existing with the remaining PLC devices, binding to unused IP addresses. The
honeypot operates with the objective of emulating the behaviour and service
footprint of a commercial PLC, to persuade an attacker that it is a valuable
target. Under normal conditions, the honeypot waits for a connection attempt
from someone probing the network or accessing it with the intent of imperson-
ating a master station. In practice, any attempt at contacting the honeypot
device may potentially generate a security event since, by definition, any activ-
ity in the honeypot is illegal and unauthorized (with the possible exception of
management operations).
The presented architecture is generic and compatible with the majority of IACS
protocols. For the development of the prototype, the Modbus protocol was used
mainly due to three main factors: standardization, popularity, and being based
on a open and well documented specification. From this point onward, Modbus
components will be mentioned although these could be changed to add support
for more IACS/SCADA protocols.
Figure 6.4 illustrates the honeypot architecture. This is presented as a hybrid
honeypot, as it runs both simulated and complete implementations of services
that are commonly found on PLC devices. Next is described the main compon-
ents of the architecture.

Honeypot Front-End Interface
The front-end interface is responsible for interacting with the control network.
These components will be reachable to the attacker. It is composed of four main
— 128 —

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
blocks:
• The Modbus API emulates a regular PLC, accepting and replying to Mod-
bus commands. It provides the protocol functionality, such as access to
registers through read and write Modbus operations.
• A File Transfer Protocol module (FTPD), providing a File Transfer Pro-
tocol ( FTP) service like the ones commonly found on commercial PLC.
• A Simple Network Management Protocol ( SNMP) module that provides
the SNMP (Case et al. 1990) device management interfaces and function-
alities found on PLC (SNMPD). This module and the usage of SNMP in
IACS environments, in general.
• A Port Scan detection module that is able to detect any probing activity
in the remaining TCP/IP service ports.
The Modbus API module specifically implements the Modbus TCP protocol
variant, widely used in IACS systems. This protocol has a simple operation.
A master station sends commands to a PLC (slave device). When the control
system is in production, most operations between the two will be read and write
commands. The master station regularly polls and changes register values of
the PLC. Mimicking the behaviour of a real PLC, the Modbus API module im-
plements variables to act as PLC registers for storing values. The attack can
interact with this module, polling and editing its values, to which the Modbus
API will reply with the appropriate message. The main idea is to deceive the
attacker into thinking it is interacting with a real PLC while gathering inform-
ation on its activity. The Modbus API module also includes a message parser,
which is used to separate the fields of a Modbus message (transaction identi-
fier, protocol identifier, length field, unit identifier, function code and payload).
These are then sent to the Event Monitor (detailed below). To have more in-
formation on the attacker interaction, additional data is stored such as source
IP and timestamp of the message.
Several aspects of the Modbus API are configurable/programmable (for example
its register banks). This allows the honeypot to better impersonate a real PLC
in different settings. If only one configuration was available for the honeypot, it
would be easier to define a define a specific behaviour profile, and compromising
its stealthiness. The longer a honeypot is able to deceive an attacker, more data
it is able to collect from their behaviour.
Often PLCs have other services available that are required for several manage-
ment operations. Services such as FTP and SNMP can be used for file transfer
for update operations, and general device monitoring and management. In the
honeypot architecture, the FTPD and SNMPD modules provide these services
and are available to be accessed. As event logging and reporting are an essen-
tial part of a honeypot, these modules include log monitoring and send attacker
activity to the Event Monitor module for further analysis.
In a honeypot not only the active services have interest in being monitored.
Activity in unused ports can also be useful. For this reason, the Port Scan
— 129 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
module monitors the access attempts to unused ports to detect the presence of
attackers, which can be probing the network.
Event Monitor
The information obtained in the honeypot Front-End Interface is analysed by
the Event Monitor. The module is divided into four sub-modules:
• Filter.
• Event reduction and aggregation (Reductor).
• Event Assembly.
• Event Transmission ( Event Tx ).
Any event generated from a honeypot Frontend Interface module passes through
all the Event Monitor sub-modules in the following sequence: Filter (1); Event
reduction and aggregation (2); Event Assembly (3); Event Transmission (4). The
Filter and Event reduction and aggregation modules pre process security events,
optimizing system resources (e.g., processing and network) and contributing to
increase the scalability of the solution up to larger IACS network scenarios.
The Filter module is used to select relevant events according to previously
defined configurations, which are stored in a file that is read when the mod-
ule starts or by a Management and Watchdog module request. The Filter
module can, for example, discard events of no interest. Relevant ones are sent
to the Event reduction and aggregation module, which processes events in or-
der to aggregate them by similar characteristics (for instance, grouping related
events).
The Event Assembly module is responsible for creating the security event mes-
sages, to be then sent to the integrated security architecture that will process
and analyse these events, to generate security alarms to the operators when
necessary.
Honeypot Management and Watchdog
The honeypot contains a Management and Watchdog module for remote man-
agement. This module allows security staff to modify the honeypot configura-
tions (e.g. configuration of modules such as Filter and Event reduction and ag-
gregation; configuration of the “Modbus” behaviour) from an authorized device.
This is the only authorized connection to the honeypot. The watchdog module
also allows some actions to be remotely performed such as restarting a mod-
ule.
The connection to the watchdog module is protected by a secure channel (either
using in-band or out-of-band management) and authenticated in both ends,
using the Transport Layer Security ( TLS) protocol. For cases were even out-of-
band management is not an option due to security restrictions, this communic-
ation may also use one-way communication devices such as the Waterfall uni-
directional security gateways ( Waterfall Security [2023]) or data diodes ( Freitas
et al. [2019]), with the negative consequence of reducing its functionality.
— 130 —

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
Firewall
Deploying and running honeypots can also pose some security risks, that should
be minimized. The level of interaction offered by the honeypot influences how
exposed it is from being exploited by the attacker. To prevent an attacker from
gaining access to the IACS production components, and turning the honeypot
into an attack vector, some containing measures can be planned. The firewall
provides a level of security to the honeypot, as it should allow all connections
reaching the honeypot, but must deny or drop connections from the honeypot
to the remaining components, except for the responses to the attacker. This
contrasts with typical firewall configuration, where usually it is configured to
protect internal components from external attackers. This should be understood
by the system administrator to avoid firewall misconfiguration and blocking the
attacker from reaching it.
Use of SNMP in IACS Environments
The importance of including an SNMP service on the honeypot comes from
it being frequently used in IACS environments, despite the fact that some au-
thors recommend not allowing SNMP traffic in IACS networks for safety reasons
(Krutz [2005]).
In fact, SNMP is widely used not only to manage PLC devices (the original
purpose of SNMP) but also, in the case of some manufacturers, to poll pro-
cess information from those control devices – thus complementing the IACS
operations. The implementation of a hybrid IACS/SNMP changes for different
manufacturers. Wingpath, for instance, uses a Modbus to SNMP converter 14
that acts as a gateway between Modbus slaves and an SNMP manager. To poll
a value from a given PLC the SNMP manager sends a SNMP GET command
to the ModSnmp server. This server then sends a Modbus request to the PLC,
receives the corresponding response, and sends a GET RESPONSE message
to the SNMP manager. Other approaches use SNMP traps to poll data from
PLCs.
For all these reasons, and given the tendency for IACS processes to remain
active and unchanged up to several decades, it was deemed as fundamental to
include a full-fledged SNMP agent in the honeypot, to act as decoy for potential
attackers (that might use SNMP as an attack vector for PLC devices). This
SNMP agent is able to mimic the expected device management functionalities
typical of SNMP and, if necessary, can also be configured to provide advanced
access to industrial process information.
6.3.1.2 Deployment Options
The honeypot here presented may be deployed using two different approaches.
The first is using low cost hardware appliances, physically and logically placed
in strategic locations of the automation network. The second approach corres-
ponds to the deployment of virtualized honeypots as a VNF. With this approach
14https://wingpath.co.uk/modbus/modsnmp.php
— 131 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
the honeypot is logically placed in the automation/field network but physically
instantiated in the data centre, having several advantages over a physical de-
ployment, which will be further discussed.
While the basic concept for the IACS honeypot is potentially effective in a simple
deployment, with the device being placed on the automation or field network,
actively monitoring scouting and attack preparation procedures for an intrusion
attempt, there are ways to improve its capabilities. Those could be further
enhanced and explored by creating a fake HMI system console (deployed in the
process control network), configured to interact with a set of field/automation
network honeypots – this configuration broadens the attack footprint detection
capabilities, while exposing the honeypot as an interesting target for an attacker
compromising the HMI as a preliminary intrusion vector.
IACS Honeypot as a Low Cost Hardware Appliance
The presented architecture is designed to run on a Single Board Computer
(SBC), which results in a cost-effective solution. For this deployment, the PoC
implementation was done using a Raspberry Pi 15 SBC equipped with a Broad-
com BCM2835 System on Chip, 512 megabytes of RAM and Ethernet commu-
nication. The Raspberry Pi ran the Linux 3.2.27+ armv6l operating system.
At the time of its design, the total cost for the complete PoC was less than 50
Euro.
The Modbus API implementation uses two Python libraries to manipulate Mod-
bus data. The python module responsible for the Modbus protocol simulation
is based on the Modbus-tk Python library 16. For parsing the Modbus messages,
a module based on the Pymodbus library was developed 17. The SNMPD and
FTPD modules are both composed by a complete service implementation and
a script to check its logs. The SNMPD module runs NET-SNMP 18, while the
FTPD module uses VSFTPd 19. To integrate these services with the honeypot
architecture, each one includes a python-based script that parses service logs,
looking for activity to create security events. Lastly, the Port Scan module is a C
program coded using the Libpcap 20 library and developed in-house for reduced
footprint and computational requirements.
There were several potential pitfalls related with the usage of low cost SBC
solutions as the basis for the honeypot, including reliability, performance and
compatibility with industrial regulations.
Regarding performance, experimental tests showed that SBC solutions such as
the Raspberry Pi easily provide more than enough resources. This is not a
surprise, since the PLC devices mimicked by the honeypot typically have even
less computational resources than the Raspberry Pi. Despite the increased pro-
cessing overhead honeypots have to deal with, their response time is more than
15https://www.raspberrypi.org/
16https://github.com/ljean/modbus-tk
17https://github.com/pymodbus-dev/pymodbus
18http://www.net-snmp.org/
19https://linux.die.net/man/8/vsftpd
20https://github.com/the-tcpdump-group/libpcap
— 132 —

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
adequate (both from the perspectives of the attacker and the IDS system it is
connected to).
Regarding reliability we do not have definitive results, but our empirical tests
show that by using carefully selected hardware components it is possible to
have remarkably stable honeypots, functioning without interruption for at least
several months, even when exposed to less than ideal environmental conditions.
These tests obviously need to be confirmed by more extensive validation work,
but they are nonetheless quite promising.
IACS Honeypot as a Virtualized Appliance
While in some cases the physical location of the honeypot in strategic locations of
the automation/network is relevant from a security point of view, in other cases it
is enough to have the honeypot logically located in the automation/field network
– since most external attackers have no way to determine its physical location.
For those situations, an interesting alternative to physical honeypot deployment
is the use of virtualized honeypots appliances as a VNF instance.
Our PoC implementation is fully portable to a virtualized environment, being
compatible with most commercial virtualization platforms ( Simões et al. [2015]).
The advantages of a virtualized honeypot are manifold. As a result, the CI op-
erator is able to deploy multiple virtualized honeypots on-demand consolidating
within the computing resources of its data centre. This reduces cost as no addi-
tional hardware is necessary, but more importantly, it allows quick and flexible
deployment on-the-fly in strategic points of the network to react to suspicious
activity. Furthermore, the usage of the virtualization environment allows soph-
isticated network solutions (such as external monitoring of the traffic to/from
the honeypot, advanced routing of non-legitimate traffic to the honeypot and
advanced protection of the management traffic between the honeypot and the
IADS platform).
Within the scope of the ATENA H2020 Project, the honeypot evolved to a
natively virtualized instance. It was deployed as an SDN-assisted containerized
VNF (illustrated in Figure 6.5) as described in Freitas et al. [2020]. Having
the honeypot deployed as a virtualized instance can have several potentially
benefits:
• Dynamic and on-the-fly instantiation of the honeypot in strategic points
of the network, as a response to suspicious activity.
• Dynamic removal of honeypots as fully virtualized instances, allowing for
a complete management of their life-cycle. This also simplifies software
update procedures of the honeypots, and their watchdog monitoring mech-
anisms.
• Active traffic steering, allowing for one honeypot to cover several unused
IP addresses. In extreme cases, if the security framework detects an at-
tack, the IACS honeypot can receive the blacklisted traffic oriented to a
production PLC. This would allow the security operator to collect more
information about the attacker, while keeping him/her occupied with a
— 133 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
dummy device.
• Software-based networking enables the possibility of isolating the traffic
of a specific honeypot if the monitoring system detects that an attacker
might have compromised the honeypot. This would reduce the change of
a compromise honeypot be used as an attack vector.
Figure 6.5 : Virtual honeypot.
6.3.1.3 Summary
In summary, the IACS honeypot presented in this section was designed to op-
erate in IACS environments. It is able to detect attackers by simulating a
Modbus TCP incorporating not only the Modbus protocol, but also other ser-
vices commonly found in commercially available PLCs including SNMP and
FTP. Moreover, it also includes a Port Scan module capable of monitoring the
remaining network ports in order to detect additional attacks or probes.
Other strong points of this honeypot device are the low cost hardware require-
ments (for a physical deployment), and its configurability, allowing security man-
agers to fine-tune the monitoring specifications through the different modules
of the honeypot. Moreover, the possibility of it being deployed as a virtualized
instance (VNF), increases deployment flexibility and eases its management, fur-
ther reducing the hardware costs necessary, as it can be consolidated in the data
centre.
Despite being a value proposition as a stand-alone device for CIP, the capabilities
of the proposed honeypot can be explored to its full when deployed within an
integrated security architecture such as the IADS (cf. Section 6.2), especially
as a virtualized appliance, providing a security probe mechanism to attract and
decoy attackers which might be trying to exploit the infrastructure.
— 134 —

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
6.3.2 The Shadow Security Unit
The protection of PLC devices is a complex task, given their lack of security
mechanisms from design, despite being a crucial part of the IACS infrastruc-
ture. As a result they have been a successful target of several attacks, some
with serious consequences as seen in the Stuxnet incident. Their unsecure char-
acteristics makes them susceptible to several types of attack, including flooding,
buffer overflow, and man-in-the-middle. Several strategies have been used to pro-
tect these devices, such as bump-in-the-wire VPN, restricted access control-list
mechanisms, or mutual authentication. Indirectly, the IACS honeypot previ-
ously presented. Although these can increase security, their deployment is not
always feasible due to multiple constraints specific to the IACS domain including
latency overheads Zhu et al. [2011], reliability or the consequence of introducing
changes to well-established protocols and architectures.
These constraints have led to the proposal and development of a device capable of
stealthily monitoring the PLC, while being minimally intrusive for the industrial
process – the SSU (see Figure 6.6).

Figure 6.6 : SSU deployment (adapted from Cruz et al. [2015a]).
The SSU is deployed in parallel to a PLC and is able to capture and decode
the IACS protocol information flow, other network activity (e.g., snort), and
capture the status of the physical I/O modules that interface with sensors and
actuators on the field. The data collected can then be correlated to generate
a redundant security-checking mechanism, following a black-box approach to
monitor and analyse the device behaviour. From the IACS perspective, this se-
curity approach is transparent and not in the critical path of the control system,
as it does not interfere with the PLC communication channels. As a result, it is
transparent to the production system, requiring minimum changes to the exist-
ing architecture and, since it is out of the critical control path, it cannot interfere
with the operation of the system, further minimizing any other potential impact
such as latency overhead or an eventual malfunction on the monitored device.
Also, this security approach can be capable of detecting attacks that change the
system behaviour as seen in with Stuxnet, as it is possible to detect and alert to
abnormal behaviour from a compromised and reprogrammed PLC. Given that
— 135 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
it is the PLC behaviour that is monitored, this would be possible for direct re-
programming, as well as originated from a legitimate (but compromised) master
station or HMI.
Although the SSU shares some functionality with a conventional Network Intru-
sion Detection System ( NIDS), specifically the interception of the communica-
tion flow, the latter has some limitations. If deployed in inline mode, the NIDS
intercepts all traffic, which may result in a single point of failure. Moreover,
it might introduce additional latency that would not be desirable or allowed in
most real-time control scenarios. On the other hand, the NIDS could be con-
figured in passive mode, receiving the network traffic from a mirror port. In
large-scale scenarios this can lead to contention given that a mirror port might
not be able to forward all traffic aggregated traffic to the NIDS, and overflow
packets are dropped. Eventually, using a NIDS might not be feasible at all if the
PLC is deployed on a remote location, served by a low bandwidth connection
(such as VHF links used for telemetry).
6.3.2.1 Shadow Security Unit Architecture
Figure 6.7 illustrates the SSU architecture implemented for Ethernet with Mod-
bus/TCP protocol scenarios, as detailed next.
