Communication Stream Analysis
This module is responsible for capturing and processing the commands ex-
changed between the master station and the PLC. In an Ethernet-based scen-
ario, this can be achieved using an active aggregation switch TAP installed
between the monitored PLC and the communication network. This forwards all
network traffic to the SSU, with little change to the infrastructure.
The SSU network interface connected to the TAP device has no IP configuration
and does not reply to ARP requests, working in promiscuous mode to capture
all the network traffic and pre-process it using a rule based filtering mechanism.
Moreover, the TAP can be configured to not allow communication in the dir-
ection from the SSU to the network infrastructure, for additional security and
— 136 —

6.3. DOMAIN-SPECIFIC PROBES
compliance with the control system requirements. The captured packets are
then decoded and analysed to check if there is any error or inconsistency in the
Modbus protocol messages. Moreover, the SSU is able to decode the semantics
of each command and track the exchanged messages between Master stations
and the monitored device, making it possible to perform integrity checks, detect
unauthorized access or scouting attempts.
Some statistics can also be extracted from the traffic flowing in this channel.
Packet rates and inter message arrival times can be calculated and used to dia-
gnose or uncover problem in the communication. Malware infection, Distributed
Denial of Service ( DDoS), flooding or brute-force attacks can be detected, as well
as equipment failures or even zero-day threats.
Physical I/O Probing
Physical I/O probing components provide information about the physical (dis-
crete and/or continuous) inputs and outputs of the PLC that constitute the
process cyber-physical interface. These are the interfaces that are connected to
the control system sensors and actuators. To achieve this, the SSU bundles (soft)
real-time data acquisition capabilities – a signal pre-conditioning stage coupled
to a precision differential voltage probe using operational amplifier technology
feeds an 8 channel, 10-bit, Successive Approximation Register ( SAR) Analog to
Digital Converter (ADC) (Microchip Inc. [2008]) with a 200K sample per second
conversion rate (5ms cycle), adequate for discrete I/O scenarios – for analogue
I/O, a 12 or 16-bit ADC may be required.
After the acquisition, the data stream is processed at a fixed sampling rate, and
given a timestamp. This prepares the data to be correlated with the informa-
tion captured by the communication stream analysis module. The correct in-
terpretation of data will depend on the modules being properly configured. The
characteristics of each physical interface, including direction (input/output) is
needed, as well as its type (discrete/analogue) and voltage ranges.
Local Processing and Correlation
The information from both acquisition modules (communications and I/O prob-
ing) is fed to an embedded local correlator 21. This correlator assesses the mon-
itored device status, and checks if commands have a legitimate source, if they
are coherent with the expected control interface flow, or if the physical I/O data
is in-line with expected values (range checking). Moreover, in discrete control
scenarios, the correlator includes a series of logical state maps (extracted from
the PLC programming) that are used to check if the PLC is behaving as expec-
ted. The correlator incorporates pre-processing capabilities, for event reduction
and aggregation within predefined time windows. Correlation rules are managed
using the management API.
As the SSU is implemented using a low-cost Single Board Computer (SBC)
system (Raspberry Pi), its processing capabilities are limited, which restricts
the ability to perform a deeper analysis of the captured information. Also, the
21https://simple-evcorr.github.io/
— 137 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
information collected by the SSU can be analysed more extensively when paired
with data from more probes and devices, in a logically centralized point with a
board view of the entire infrastructure. For these reasons, the SSU is able to
operate in a flexible way, either in standalone wih a tighter scope of detection,
or as part of an integrated security architecture such as the IADS.
Eventing and Reporting
The events generated by the SSU correlation module are sent to a security man-
agement platform. Its detection capabilities can be further reinforced with the
use of an optional message checker system (illustrated in Figure 6.6). This cre-
ates a closed loop for command flow control, to analyse if the system behaviour
is as expected.
Management
The SSU architecture supports an out-of-band HTTP REST API for device,
service/component and software module management. For security reasons, it
is accessible through a separated network interface (which is also used by the
eventing and reporting module to send SSU events). Each internal module is re-
sponsible for its own configuration parameters (i.e., state and semantics) mapped
into a common data model using an internal management adaptor. This sep-
arations assures the modularity of the SSU. The internal adaptor maps the
attributes for the specific configurations of each SSU module, via an XML data
model structure (described in an XSD file), creating a uniform management in-
terface. When a data model property is changed, the adaptor for that component
translate that change into an action on the corresponding module.
Real-time, secure execution environment
To improve the capture capacity of the SSU, (soft) real-time processing capabil-
ities are supported using the Xenomai ( Brown and Martin [2010]) Linux kernel
extensions. Moreover, the individual modules are deployed within Linux Con-
tainers LXC22, providing filesystem, network, root privilege and system resource
isolation. Three main benefits of this approach are: increased security provided
by isolation between SSU modules; increased overall resilience in the event of an
individual module failure (as it is isolated from the remaining system), ease of
management as all components correspond to different software packages that
can be updated individually. Inter-module communication is done using local
UNIX domain sockets. Module management is provided by the management
API.
Watchdog
Component and service operation monitoring is done with the watchdog mod-
ule. There are two main level of operation. Correct component operation is
done using a set of software routing to check periodically component operation,
attempting a recovery or restart when module stalling is detected. The second
level of action is done at system level, with a kernel module working with a
watchdog service, keeping a regular heartbeat to the hardware watchdog timer
22https://linuxcontainers.org/
— 138 —

6.4. SUMMARY
fo the SBC (Howard [2012]). A hardware watchdog enables the possibility of
rebooting the entire SSU platform independently in the event of a critical failure
if a predefined number of missed heartbeats is met.
Virtualization of the Shadow Security Unit
Although the SSU was initially proposed for physical deployment near a PLC
within an IACS, it can also benefit from virtualization technologies. Considering
its architecture and device monitoring capabilities, it is clear that a complete
virtualization of the SSU is not possible. More specifically, this is a result of
its physical connection to the monitored device to collect inputs and outputs
to physical sensors and actuators. Nonetheless, part of its architecture may
be virtualized, with some potential advantages. The control loop (responsible
for local processing and correlation), and event and reporting components (cf.
Figure 6.7) may be instantiated as virtualized components, following ETSI’s
MEC. Potential benefits of this approach include:
• Simplified hardware, as a considerable amount of processing requirements
would be moved out of the SSU physical device.
• Reduced on-site maintenance, as a result of having less components on
the physical device, troubleshooting of some modules would be done on
virtualized instances.
• Closer proximity to the IADS domain correlation infrastructure.
The SSU reference architecture presented in this section was designed consider-
ing traditional deployment strategies of the current PLC model, which does not
allow the SSU to be deployed as a completely virtual component. However, pro-
posals for virtualizing IACS components might push virtualization possibilities
of future SSU developments a bit further.
Cruz et al. [2016c] proposed an SDN-based virtual Programmable Logic Con-
troller ( vPLC), which decouples the PLC execution environment from the I/O
modules (illustrated in Figure 6.8). In this proposal, the PLC connects to the
field equipment using connected I/O modules by an SDN-enabled network fabric.
This would allow for a completely virtualized SSU, as it would not be necessary
to tap the physical connections to the PLC I/O. The SSU could receive a copy
of the data directly from the network fabric controller.
Summarizing, the SSU concept presented in this section is a low-cost solution
for securing IACS systems, being complementary to existing SIEM architectures
and constituting an new approach to the problem of security monitoring in
IACS.
6.4 Summary
This chapter described how virtualization concepts can be applied to security
frameworks in different domains (in contrast with the telecommunications op-
erator networks discussed in previous chapters). It presented the IADS security
— 139 —

CHAPTER 6. USING VIRTUALIZATION CONCEPTS TO EVOL VE
DOMAIN-SPECIFIC SECURITY FRAMEWORKS
Figure 6.8 : vPLC deployment (adapted from Cruz et al. [2016c]).
architecture, which was originally developed in the scope of the ATENA Pro-
ject, but can be expanded to other application domains. This architecture was
designed and planned to be used in virtualization environments, including to as-
sist the deployment and operation of virtualized components. Also, it presented
innovative security probes ( IACS honeypot and SSU), which provided collected
data to be processed in the IADS to detect security events.

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK
R
egardless of whether the application context is focused on computing
or networking infrastructures, virtualization technologies have paved
the way for a significant paradigm shift, impacting all aspects of the
Information Technology ( IT) ecosystem, from endpoints to service support in-
frastructures.
Specifically, these technologies have created new opportunities for telecom op-
erators to enhance the efficiency of current service delivery while providing a
stronger foundation for the development of new and improved services. This
contrasts with traditional infrastructures, which have been evolving on the same
foundation for decades and trace their origins back to much older systems.
In this context, the focus of this thesis was fundamentally exploratory, with the
core research questions organized around a single overarching idea: to explore
the extent to which virtualization can be leveraged to develop and improve
infrastructures and service offerings, as well as to streamline operational pro-
cesses.
7.1 Synthesis of the Thesis
This thesis explores various aspects of network virtualization within the domain
of telecom operator networks. The research is divided into specific areas, each
covered in a different chapter.
Chapter 2 provides an introductory overview that establishes the common found-
ation for the subsequent chapters, introducing the concepts of SDN and NFV,
which serve as the basis for current network virtualization paradigms.
Chapter 3 introduces the concept of virtualizing telecommunications subscriber
gateways, referred to in this thesis as RGW. The chapter provides a SoA re-
view, detailing the rationale for the vRGW and discussing existing research. It
also proposes a taxonomy for classifying vRGW-related proposals. Finally, it
presents two PoCs designed and implemented during the course of this work,
each with a different objective. The first PoC aims to evaluate how shifting to
a virtualized RGW environment could benefit a telecom operator by reducing
time to market in various aspects, including service design and the implement-
ation of RGW functions. The second PoC presents a container-based vRGW
architecture to explore different deployment locations for the virtualized func-
tions of the vRGW, whether within the telco infrastructure or at the customer
premises, be it residential or business.
Chapter 4 shifts focus to a different component of the operator’s infrastructure,
still related to the broadband service subscriber-operator connection described in
the previous chapter, but concentrating on the operator’s side of the connection.
Here, the virtualization of the BNG is the main topic. The BNG acts as the
gatekeeper for the operator’s infrastructure, serving as the target for customer
— 142 —

7.2. CONTRIBUTIONS
RGW authentication, and enabling access to broadband services. The BNG
provides various services to customers, including traffic forwarding to and from
the Internet, and enforcing subscriber plan-related policies such as traffic shaping
and QoS. In addition, Chapter 4 discusses the limitations of the traditional BNG
approach and the motivations driving operators to virtualize this component,
similar to the vRGW, but with a distinct set of challenges. Additionally, the
chapter proposes an architecture for a vBNG and presents a PoC to evaluate the
use of virtualized functions on COTS hardware. The primary goal is to assess the
performance impact of traffic policies in a COTS-based vBNG scenario.
Chapter 5 explores a different aspect of virtualization in telecommunications
operators environments. These advances in networking have been leveraged
in the deployment of 5G networks. Virtualization is a key enabler of several
features in 5G, including SBA and network slicing. This chapter focuses on the
security aspects of 5G, particularly in securing the components of the 5G core.
While the openness of 5G brings benefits in terms of new services and vendor
interoperability, it can also potentially expose the network to new vulnerabilities.
The PoC presented in this chapter outlines an architecture designed to collect,
process, and display 5G-related security events.
Finally, Chapter 6 discusses the use of virtualization to improve domain-specific
security frameworks. This chapter also presents a collaborative proposal for a
IACS-specific Intrusion and Anomaly Detection System architecture, and dis-
cusses the uses of virtualization concepts to evolve this framework to improve
the flexibility of deploying its components, to improve its manageability, and
the improvement of its components and probes both in network monitoring and
life-cycle management, by using SDN principles.
7.2 Contributions
The research work conducted in the context of this thesis was driven by the
objectives described in Chapter 1. With these objectives in mind, this thesis led
to the contributions described below:
• A SoA on telecommunications operator subscriber gateways, from the tra-
ditional architectures and the evolution to current virtualized proposals.
These contributions were published in the following publications: Proença
et al. [2021a]; Proença et al. [2021]; Proença et al. [2018]; Proença et al.
[2017]; Proença et al. [2015].
• A SoA on the telecommunications operator BNG path to its virtualization.
These contributions are currently under review for publication ( Proença
et al. [2024]).
• An architecture for virtualizing the subscriber gateway, and the presenta-
tion of two PoCs to explore different aspects of its virtualization, namely:
service time to market reduction and deployment of vCPE functions in
different locations. These contributions were published in the following
publications: Proença et al. [2021]; Proença et al. [2017].
— 143 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK
• An architecture proposal for virtualizing the operator BNG and a PoC
for assessing the impact of applying traffic policies of virtualized BNG
functions deployed on COTS hardware. These contributions are currently
under review for publication ( Proença et al. [2024]).
• A security architecture to secure the SBA functions of the 5G core, col-
lecting security probe events and process them to show on an security
operator dashboard. These contributions were published in the following
publication: Proença et al. [2021b].
• A collaborative proposal of an architecture for intrusion and anomaly de-
tection in the IACS domain, designed to be deployed in virtualization
environments, and two domain-specific probes PoCs. Moreover, it also
discusses the advantages of a virtualization-enabled architecture, pointing-
out its potential benefits with the two probes as examples. These con-
tributions were published in the following publications: Adamsky et al.
[2018]; Cruz et al. [2014a]; Cruz et al. [2014b]; Cruz et al. [2016a]; Cruz
et al. [2016b]; Proença et al. [2015]; Cruz et al. [2014c]; Rosa et al. [2017];
Simões et al. [2015], Cruz et al. [2015a].
7.3 Future Work
This Thesis contributes and discusses ongoing integration of virtualization con-
cepts across multiple domains of telecommunications networks. In the future,
they are expected to continue closely tied to virtualization.
In the domain of telecommunications operators, the demand for flexible and
scalable solutions initially driven by 5G will evolve within beyond 5G and 6G
architectures. Edge Computing ( MEC) emerges as a key enabler across mul-
tiple domains. Autonomous vehicles can leverage these architectures for effi-
cient communication and processing required to operate and manage vehicle
convoys. Also, smart grids can benefit from MEC for a more efficient use of en-
ergy consumed and generated, both for traditional power plants and distributed
microgeneration across the grid.
In a different perspective, network infrastructures must also adapt to the grow-
ing influence of Artificial Intelligence ( AI) and ML. For example, the concept
of Digital Twin ( DT), including network, site, and subscriber, will heavily rely
on virtualization for setting up and managing the computing and network infra-
structures that support these scenarios. The detailed DT models generated can
potentially ease AI model training and testing without impacting the production
infrastructure. The uses of DTs is not confined to improve the efficiency of the
systems it is copying, but can also be used in its cyberprotection. A DT allows
security operators to safely develop and test security strategies, reducing the
probability of negatively impacting the original systems, while improving their
results. This poses several challenges for deploying and managing the virtualized
infrastructure, including the integration with MEC for a more reliable copy of a
distributed IACS. Moreover Network Slicing also plays an important role, as it
can be leverage to provide isolation between the communications of the real and
— 144 —

7.3. FUTURE WORK
copied systems, and assuring identical QoS metrics to allow the DT to behave
as close as possible to the original system.
